## Q57

Biology · both · Biotechnology: Principles and Processes

What is the criterion for DNA fragments movement on agarose gel during gel electrophoresis?

- (1) Negatively charged fragments do not move
- (2) The larger the fragment size, the farther it moves
- **(3)** The smaller the fragment size, the farther it moves
- (4) Positively charged fragments move to farther end

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. During gel electrophoresis, DNA fragments separate (resolve) according to their size through sieving effect provided by agarose gel.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. DNA is negatively charged (phosphate backbone), so under an electric field it moves towards the anode, which rules out options $1$ and $4$.
2. The agarose matrix acts as a sieve, so fragments are separated by size: the smaller the fragment, the farther it moves, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Separation & Isolation of DNA Fragments (Gel Electrophoresis) · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 57](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q057_631bed7d_30102026.png?v=631bed7d64cd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 57](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q057_9bc2150b_30102026.png?v=9bc2150b3df1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** On an agarose gel the negatively charged DNA fragments run towards the anode, and the gel's sieving effect lets the smaller fragments travel farther
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  Reasoning from charge alone, a longer fragment carries more phosphate groups and so seems to be pulled harder, which states the size relation the other way round; it is the gel's pores, not the charge, that set how far a fragment gets.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated direction of the size-to-distance relation on an agarose gel, demonstrated by the paired options 'The larger the fragment size, the farther it moves' and 'The smaller the fragment size, the farther it moves'.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier recorded statement_options on the strength of four full-sentence options, but the stem is a content question, 'What is the criterion for DNA fragments movement on agarose gel during gel electrophoresis?', not an instruction to judge statements, and no lettered block is printed, so the shape recorded is a plain MCQ.

## Q59

Biology · botany · Respiration in Plants

Which statement is wrong for Krebs' cycle?

- **(1)** The cycle starts with condensation of acetyl group (acetyl CoA) with pyruvic acid to yield citric acid
- (2) There are three points in the cycle where $\mathrm{NAD^{+}}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{NADH + H^{+}}$
- (3) There is one point in the cycle where $\mathrm{FAD^{+}}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{FADH_2}$
- (4) During conversion of succinyl CoA to succinic acid, a molecule of GTP is synthesised

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Krebs cycle starts with condensation of acetyl CoA (2C) with oxaloacetic acid (4C) to form citric acid (6C).

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The first step of Krebs' cycle is condensation of acetyl CoA ($\mathrm{C_2}$) with oxaloacetic acid ($\mathrm{C_4}$) and water to give citric acid ($\mathrm{C_6}$), not with pyruvic acid, so option $1$ is wrong.
2. $\mathrm{NAD^{+}}$ is reduced to $\mathrm{NADH + H^{+}}$ at three points (isocitrate $\to$ $\alpha$-ketoglutarate, $\alpha$-ketoglutarate $\to$ succinyl CoA, malate $\to$ OAA), so option $2$ is correct.
3. FAD is reduced to $\mathrm{FADH_2}$ at one point (succinate $\to$ fumarate), so option $3$ is correct as NCERT states it.
4. Succinyl CoA $\to$ succinic acid is coupled to substrate-level synthesis of one GTP, so option $4$ is correct; the wrong statement is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Aerobic Respiration And Tricarboxylic Acid Cycle · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 59](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q059_2c258631_30102026.png?v=2c258631a316)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 59](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q059_cdfa6fd7_30102026.png?v=cdfa6fd7fcfc)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** The citric acid cycle opens with acetyl CoA condensing with oxaloacetic acid, and one turn yields three NADH, one FADH2 and one GTP
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 4  
  The sentence printed after the GTP step says that the GTP is converted to GDP with the simultaneous synthesis of ATP, so a student carrying only 'one ATP by substrate-level phosphorylation' reads the printed GTP as the error the stem is asking for.
- **Why:** This question requires each printed statement checked against the source's own sentences on the cycle, demonstrated by option 1 reading 'condensation of acetyl group (acetyl CoA) with pyruvic acid' where the source reads oxaloacetic acid. Trap test applied: replacing 'GTP' with an unlike name leaves the pull intact, because the pull is the ATP printed in the adjacent sentence, so nearby_fact and not look_alike_term.
- **Note:** All three wrong options are statements the source prints as true, so the paper's own device sits on the key, which substitutes 'pyruvic acid' for oxaloacetic acid inside an otherwise verbatim sentence. The trap recorded is therefore the strongest route by which a true statement gets read as the wrong one.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q60

Biology · zoology · Evolution

Artificial selection to obtain cows yielding higher milk output represents

- (1) Stabilizing followed by disruptive as it stabilizes the population to produce higher yielding cows
- (2) Stabilizing selection as it stabilizes this character in the population
- **(3)** Directional as it pushes the mean of the character in one direction
- (4) Disruptive as it splits the population into two one yielding higher output and the other lower output

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Artificial selection to obtain cow yielding higher milk output will shift the peak to one direction, hence, will be an example of Directional selection.
2. In stabilizing selection, the organisms with the mean value of the trait are selected.
3. In disruptive selection, both extremes get selected.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Breeders keep choosing cows from the high-yield end of the milk-yield distribution, which shifts the population mean of that trait toward higher values in one direction. That is directional selection, option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Types of Natural Selection · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Directional selection shifts the population mean of a trait towards one extreme, stabilizing selection favours the existing mean, and disruptive selection favours both extremes and splits the population
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 2  
  A breeder's aim reads as fixing the high-yield character in the herd, and this option supplies that very wording -- 'stabilizes this character in the population' -- as the justification for the first of the three selection modes.
- **Why:** This question requires the definition of each selection mode applied to a described case, demonstrated by the options printing the criteria themselves -- 'pushes the mean of the character in one direction' against 'stabilizes this character' and 'splits the population into two'. Trap test applied: replacing 'stabilizing' and 'directional' with two unlike words leaves the pull intact, since it rides on the option's own printed justification and on the three modes being taught in one figure, so nearby_fact.

## Q63

## Q64

Biology · zoology · Digestion and Absorption

A baby boy aged two years is admitted to play school and passes through a dental check-up. The dentist observed that the boy had twenty teeth. Which teeth were absent?

- (1) Molars
- (2) Incisors
- (3) Canines
- **(4)** Pre-molars

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Total number of teeth in human child $= 20$.
2. Premolars are absent in primary dentition.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At two years a child has milk (deciduous) teeth, and the full milk set has $20$ teeth.
2. The milk dental formula is $\frac{2102}{2102}$: $I = 2$, $C = 1$, $PM = 0$, $M = 2$ in each half jaw.
3. Total $= (2 + 1 + 0 + 2) \times 4 = 5 \times 4 = 20$ teeth, which matches the boy's count.
4. Premolars are $0$ in the milk set, so the absent teeth are pre-molars, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Human Digestive System: Alimentary Canal and Associated Glands · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 64](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q064_a2dc62f9_30102026.png?v=a2dc62f9afd2)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 64](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q064_eb0817c0_30102026.png?v=eb0817c03875)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** The milk set is twenty teeth -- two incisors, one canine and two molars in each half jaw -- and premolars appear only with the permanent set
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 1  
  Molars are learnt as the back teeth that arrive last, so a two-year-old's set reads as a set still waiting for its molars; the molars are in fact present and it is the premolar column of the milk formula that is empty.
- **Why:** This question requires the milk dental formula applied to a stated tooth count, demonstrated by the stem supplying 'the boy had twenty teeth' and then asking 'Which teeth were absent?'. Trap test applied: renaming the premolars as bicuspids leaves the pull intact, because what carries it is the back-of-the-jaw eruption fact and not the shared word 'molar', so nearby_fact and not look_alike_term.

## Q66

Biology · both · Biotechnology: Principles and Processes

DNA fragments are

- (1) Either positively or negatively charged depending on their size
- (2) Positively charged
- **(3)** Negatively charged
- (4) Neutral

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. DNA fragments are negatively charged because of phosphate group.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The sugar-phosphate backbone of DNA has negatively charged phosphate groups, so DNA fragments are negatively charged and move towards the anode in gel electrophoresis, regardless of their size. This is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Separation & Isolation of DNA Fragments (Gel Electrophoresis) · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 66](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q066_ec7a8dde_30102026.png?v=ec7a8ddef0d6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** DNA carries a negative charge from its phosphate backbone, which is why fragments migrate towards the anode in gel electrophoresis
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  The remembered line is that DNA fragments run towards the anode, and reading the electrode's sign onto the molecule gives "positively charged"; it is the anode's positive charge that pulls negative DNA.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated charge on DNA that the electrophoresis passage rests on, demonstrated by the stem "DNA fragments are" and options that differ only in the sign of that charge.

## Q68

Biology · zoology · Reproductive Health

The function of copper ions in copper releasing IUD's is :

- (1) They inhibit ovulation
- **(2)** They suppress sperm motility and fertilising capacity of sperms

## Q69

## Q70

Biology · botany · Environmental Issues

Which one of the following statements is not valid for aerosols?

- (1) They have negative impact on agricultural land
- (2) They are harmful to human health
- (3) They alter rainfall and monsoon patterns
- **(4)** They cause increased agricultural productivity

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Aerosols can cause various problems to agriculture through its direct or indirect effects on plants.
2. However continually increasing air pollution may represent a persistent and largely irreversible threat to agriculture in the future.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Aerosols are suspended particulate pollutants that harm human health, reduce crop yield on agricultural land and change cloud formation, so they alter rainfall and monsoon patterns; options $1$, $2$ and $3$ are valid, while increased agricultural productivity is not, so the answer is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Air Pollution and Its Control · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Aerosols harm human health, damage agricultural land and alter rainfall and monsoon patterns; no printed effect of theirs raises crop productivity
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  Options 1 and 4 are the two claims about agriculture and option 1 is the valid one of the pair, so a reading of the stem as "which is the aerosol-and-agriculture fact" lands on it instead of on the claim that is not valid.
- **Why:** This question requires each printed claim about aerosols to be graded against the stem's negation, demonstrated by "Which one of the following statements is not valid for aerosols?" and the option "They cause increased agricultural productivity".
- **Note:** The trap is the stem's polarity. The retired per-option vocabulary called this true_statement_trap; its family on the live 12-value axis is answers_other_question, since a true statement answers the un-negated question the stem did not ask. All three wrong options are reachable by the same polarity slip, so all three carry routes; option 1 is recorded as the primary because it pairs with the key on agriculture.

## Q71

Biology · zoology · Excretory Products and Their Elimination

Which of the following statements is correct?

- (1) The descending limb of loop of Henle is permeable to electrolytes
- **(2)** The ascending limb of loop of Henle is impermeable to water
- (3) The descending limb of loop of Henle is impermeable to water
- (4) The ascending limb of loop of Henle is permeable to water

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Descending limb of loop of Henle is permeable to water but impermeable to electrolytes while ascending limb is impermeable to water but permeable to electrolytes.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Recall: the descending limb of the loop of Henle is $\text{permeable to water}$ but almost $\text{impermeable to electrolytes}$, and the ascending limb is $\text{impermeable to water}$ but allows electrolyte transport. So only option $2$ is correct.

**Subtopic:** Function of the Tubules and their types · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

![printed question 71](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q071_ffdef3cd_30102026.png?v=ffdef3cdb179)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** In the loop of Henle the descending limb lets water out and holds electrolytes, and the ascending limb does the reverse — that swap is what builds the concentration gradient
- **Trap:** **Wrong site attacked** (`wrong_site_attacked`) — the reagent is right but it attacks the other site, atom or bond than the one the question turns on — option 3  
  "Impermeable to water" is the right property and this option hangs it on the descending limb; the limb water cannot cross is the ascending one, while the descending limb is where water leaves.
- **Why:** This question requires the printed permeability of one named limb of the loop of Henle, demonstrated by four options that each pair a limb with a permeability and by the key "The ascending limb of loop of Henle is impermeable to water".
- **Note:** asks is fact_line and not compare: one printed line about the ascending limb settles all four options, and the discrimination between the two limbs is recorded where it belongs, on the trap axis.

## Q72

Biology · botany · Microbes in Human Welfare

1. Primary treatment is a physical process which involves sequential filtration and sedimentation.

## Q73

Biology · zoology · Human Reproduction

GnRH, a hypothalamic hormone, needed in reproduction, acts on

- (1) Posterior pituitary gland and stimulates secretion of LH and relaxin
- (2) Anterior pituitary gland and stimulates secretion of LH and oxytocin
- **(3)** Anterior pituitary gland and stimulates secretion of LH and FSH
- (4) Posterior pituitary gland and stimulates secretion of oxytocin and FSH

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Hypothalamus secretes GnRH which stimulates anterior pituitary gland for the secretion of gonadotropins (FSH and LH).

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. GnRH (gonadotropin releasing hormone) from the hypothalamus reaches the anterior pituitary via the hypophyseal portal system and stimulates secretion of the gonadotropins LH and FSH, which is option $3$; options $1$, $2$ and $4$ wrongly involve the posterior pituitary, oxytocin or relaxin.

**Subtopic:** Hormonal Regulation of Reproduction · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** GnRH from the hypothalamus acts on the anterior pituitary, which answers by secreting the two gonadotropins LH and FSH
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  The gland is right and LH is right, and only the second hormone fails: oxytocin comes from the posterior lobe, while GnRH's output is the gonadotropin pair LH and FSH.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated target and output of one hypothalamic hormone, demonstrated by options that each pair a pituitary lobe with two hormone names, so that "Anterior pituitary gland and stimulates secretion of LH and FSH" must hold in every part.

## Q74

Biology · botany · Transport in Plants

Which of the following facilitates opening of stomatal aperture?

- (1) Longitudinal orientation of cellulose microfibrils in the cell wall of guard cells
- (2) Contraction of outer wall of guard cells
- (3) Decrease in turgidity of guard cells
- **(4)** Radial orientation of cellulose microfibrils in the cell wall of guard cells

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Cellulose microfibrils are oriented radially rather than longitudinally which makes easy for the stoma to open.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Stomatal opening is aided by the radial (not longitudinal) orientation of cellulose microfibrils in guard cell walls; increased, not decreased, turgidity and outward bulging of the outer wall drive opening, so the answer is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Transpiration and Stomatal Opening and Closing · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

- **Concept:** Guard cells open the pore because water entering raises their turgor and the radially laid cellulose microfibrils let the thin outer wall bow outwards

  The microfibril point is taught as a contrast, radial against longitudinal, and a student who recalls that both words were in it picks the wrong half; it is the radial laying that lets the pore open.

## Q75

Biology · zoology · Principles of Inheritance and Variation

The genotypes of a Husband and Wife are $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{B}}$ and $\mathrm{I^{A}i}$. Among the blood types of their children, how many different genotypes and phenotypes are possible?

- (1) 4 genotypes ; 4 phenotypes
- (2) 3 genotypes ; 3 phenotypes
- (3) 3 genotypes ; 4 phenotypes
- **(4)** 4 genotypes ; 3 phenotypes

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Husband $\times$ Wife: $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{B}}$ $\qquad$ $\mathrm{I^{A}i}$

![printed figure: solution_1](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q075_sol_1_45d118e2_30102026.svg?v=45d118e2f9bd)

2. Number of genotypes $= 4$
3. Number of phenotypes $= 3$
4. $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{A}}$ and $\mathrm{I^{A}i} = \mathrm{A}$
5. $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{B}} = \mathrm{AB}$
6. $\mathrm{I^{B}i} = \mathrm{B}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Husband $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{B}}$ forms gametes $\mathrm{I^{A}}$ and $\mathrm{I^{B}}$; wife $\mathrm{I^{A}i}$ forms gametes $\mathrm{I^{A}}$ and $\mathrm{i}$.
2. Crossing gives offspring $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{A}}$, $\mathrm{I^{A}i}$, $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{B}}$, $\mathrm{I^{B}i}$, i.e. $4$ distinct genotypes.
3. $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{A}}$ and $\mathrm{I^{A}i}$ are both blood group A, $\mathrm{I^{A}I^{B}}$ is AB (codominance), $\mathrm{I^{B}i}$ is B, i.e. $3$ phenotypes.
4. $4$ genotypes and $3$ phenotypes is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Incomplete Dominance & Co-dominance · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 10)

![printed question 75](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q075_a8c298e3_30102026.png?v=a8c298e31f18)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 75](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q075_d10b3fb1_30102026.png?v=d10b3fb11d70)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In ABO inheritance $I^A$ and $I^B$ are codominant but both are dominant over $i$, so a cross can yield more genotypes than blood groups
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 1  
  The square gives four boxes, $I^AI^A$, $I^Ai$, $I^AI^B$ and $I^Bi$, and the genotype count of 4 is read straight across to the phenotypes; $I^AI^A$ and $I^Ai$ are both blood group A, so only three phenotypes appear.
- **Why:** This question requires the cross to be worked out and its outcomes counted twice over, demonstrated by the stem "how many different genotypes and phenotypes are possible?" and options that print the two counts side by side.
- **Note:** E1's pattern called this statement_options; the options are two counts bundled together and judged whole, not statements, so the shape is recorded as two_part. asks is calculate rather than apply_rule because two integers are produced from the cross.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q76

Biology · botany · Organisms and Populations

Plants which produce characterstic pneumatophores and show vivipary belong to

- (1) Hydrophytes
- (2) Mesophytes
- **(3)** Halophytes
- (4) Psammophytes

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Halophytes growing in saline soils show
2. (i) Vivipary which is in-situ seed germination
3. (ii) Pneumatophores for gaseous exchange

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Pneumatophores and vivipary (seed germination while the fruit is still on the parent plant) are the defining features of mangroves such as $Rhizophora$, which grow in saline swampy soil, so they are halophytes, option $3$.

---

<!--ecat-->

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Pneumatophores and viviparous seeds are the mangrove equipment for salty waterlogged soil, which places those plants with the halophytes
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 1  
  Pneumatophores are the breathing roots of waterlogged soil, so a student who files the clue under water rather than under salt reaches for the water-habitat class in the same printed list; mangrove mud is saline, which is what halophyte records.
- **Why:** This question requires the habitat class NCERT attaches to two named mangrove features, demonstrated by the stem "Plants which produce characterstic pneumatophores and show vivipary belong to" and four options that are coordinate habitat-based classes.
- **Note:** Applied the swap test: all four options end in -phyte, so the suffix discriminates nothing, and the pull to hydrophyte comes from the content link between waterlogged soil and water plants, which survives renaming — hence nearby_fact and not look_alike_term.

## Q78

Biology · both · Molecular Basis of Inheritance

DNA replication in bacteria occurs

- (1) Just before transcription
- (2) During S-phase
- (3) Within nucleolus
- **(4)** Prior to fission

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. DNA replication in bacteria occurs prior to fission.
2. Prokaryotes do not show well marked S-phase due to their primitive nature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Bacteria are prokaryotes, so they have no nucleus and no nucleolus, which rules out option $3$; they also lack the eukaryotic $G_1$-$S$-$G_2$-$M$ cell cycle with a defined S-phase, and replication is not timed to transcription, so options $2$ and $1$ go too. The circular DNA is replicated so that each daughter cell gets a copy when the cell divides by binary fission, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Replication · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 11)

![printed question 78](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q078_af44150a_30102026.png?v=af44150a3fab)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A bacterium has no cell cycle phases and no nucleus, so its single chromosome is copied in step with binary fission
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  S-phase is where DNA replication sits in the eukaryotic cell cycle, and dropping the stem's "in bacteria" carries that rule straight across; a bacterium has no S-phase because it has no cell cycle phases at all.
- **Why:** This question requires the timing of replication in a cell that has no cell cycle phases, demonstrated by the stem "DNA replication in bacteria occurs" and the option "During S-phase" standing beside "Prior to fission".
- **Note:** condition_missed rather than answers_other_question: the condition dropped is printed in the stem — the word "bacteria" — and removing it would make option 2 correct, which is the codebook's test for this label.

## Q87

Biology · both · Cell Cycle and Cell Division

Which of the following options gives the correct sequence of events during mitosis?

- (1) Condensation $\rightarrow$ arrangement at equator $\rightarrow$ centromere division $\rightarrow$ segregation $\rightarrow$ telophase
- (2) Condensation $\rightarrow$ nuclear membrane disassembly $\rightarrow$ crossing over $\rightarrow$ segregation $\rightarrow$ telophase
- **(3)** Condensation $\rightarrow$ nuclear membrane disassembly $\rightarrow$ arrangement at equator $\rightarrow$ centromere division $\rightarrow$ segregation $\rightarrow$ telophase
- (4) Condensation $\rightarrow$ crossing over $\rightarrow$ nuclear membrane disassembly $\rightarrow$ segregation $\rightarrow$ telophase

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. The correct sequence of events during mitosis would be as follows
2. (i) Condensation of $\mathrm{DNA}$ so that chromosomes become visible occurs during early to mid-prophase.
3. (ii) Nuclear membrane disassembly begins at late prophase or transition to metaphase.
4. (iii) Arrangement of chromosomes at equator occurs during metaphase, called congression.
5. (iv) Centromere division or splitting occurs during anaphase forming daughter chromosomes.
6. (v) Segregation also occurs during anaphase as daughter chromosomes separate and move to opposite poles.
7. (vi) Telophase leads to formation of two daughter nuclei.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In prophase chromatin condenses into chromosomes, and by the end of prophase the nuclear membrane disassembles: $\text{condensation} \rightarrow \text{nuclear membrane disassembly}$.
2. In metaphase chromosomes are arranged at the equator (metaphase plate).
3. At the onset of anaphase the centromere splits, and the sister chromatids then segregate to opposite poles: $\text{centromere division} \rightarrow \text{segregation}$.
4. Telophase follows; crossing over occurs only in meiosis (prophase I), which rules out options $2$ and $4$.
5. Option $1$ omits nuclear membrane disassembly, so the complete correct sequence is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Prophase and Metaphase · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 12)

![printed question 87](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q087_2057f70d_30102026.png?v=2057f70d12ab)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 87](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q087_79804d24_30102026.png?v=79804d248eb4)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** Mitosis runs condensation, nuclear-envelope disassembly, alignment at the equator, centromere division, segregation and telophase - and crossing over belongs to meiosis, not here.
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 1  
  Rebuild the sequence from the phase headings - prophase condenses, metaphase aligns, anaphase splits the centromeres, telophase - and every event in option 1 is a genuine mitotic event in the right order. What is missing is the disassembly of the nuclear envelope, so the set is true but incomplete.
- **Why:** This question requires the mitotic events put in their printed order, demonstrated by four options that differ only in which events they carry and where - 'arrangement at equator' against 'crossing over'.

## Q88

Biology · zoology · Principles of Inheritance and Variation

A disease caused by an autosomal primary non-disjunction is

- (1) Sickle cell anemia
- **(2)** Down's syndrome
- (3) Klinefelter's syndrome
- (4) Turner's syndrome

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Down’s syndrome is caused by non-disjunction of $21^{\mathrm{st}}$ chromosome.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Down's syndrome is trisomy of autosome $21$ ($2n+1 = 47$), caused by non-disjunction of chromosome $21$, so it is an autosomal aneuploidy; Klinefelter's ($XXY$) and Turner's ($XO$) are sex-chromosome aneuploidies and sickle cell anemia is a single-gene point mutation, so the answer is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Chromosomal Disorders · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 12)

![printed question 88](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q088_6257e459_30102026.png?v=6257e459478b)

#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Down's syndrome is trisomy of chromosome 21, an autosome, so it is the aneuploidy caused by autosomal non-disjunction; Klinefelter's and Turner's involve the sex chromosomes.
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 3  
  Klinefelter's (47, XXY) is produced by non-disjunction and shows the same one-chromosome-too-many picture as trisomy 21, so a student holding 'non-disjunction' and letting the word 'autosomal' go ticks it. The extra chromosome there is the X, a sex chromosome.
- **Why:** This question requires one disorder selected by two printed criteria at once, demonstrated by the stem's 'autosomal primary non-disjunction' set against options that each satisfy only one of the two.

## Q91

Physics · Oscillations

A spring of force constant $k$ is cut into lengths of ratio $1:2:3$. They are connected in series and the new force constant is $k'$. Then they are connected in parallel and force constant is $k''$. Then $k':k''$ is

- (1) $1:14$
- (2) $1:6$
- (3) $1:9$
- **(4)** $1:11$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Spring constant $\propto \frac{1}{\text{length}}$
2. $k \propto \frac{1}{l}$
3. i.e, $k_1 = 6k$
4. $k_2 = 3k$
5. $k_3 = 2k$
6. In series
7. $\frac{1}{k'} = \frac{1}{6k} + \frac{1}{3k} + \frac{1}{2k}$
8. $\frac{1}{k'} = \frac{6}{6k}$
9. $k' = k$
10. $k'' = 6k + 3k + 2k$
11. $k'' = 11k$
12. $\frac{k'}{k''} = \frac{1}{11}$ i.e $k' : k'' = 1:11$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a spring, $k \propto \frac{1}{l}$, so a piece of length $\frac{l_0}{n}$ has constant $nk$.
2. The lengths are $\frac{L}{6}, \frac{2L}{6}, \frac{3L}{6}$, so $k_1 = 6k$, $k_2 = 3k$, $k_3 = 2k$.
3. Series: $\frac{1}{k'} = \frac{1}{6k} + \frac{1}{3k} + \frac{1}{2k} = \frac{1+2+3}{6k} = \frac{1}{k}$, so $k' = k$.
4. Parallel: $k'' = 6k + 3k + 2k = 11k$.
5. $k' : k'' = k : 11k = 1:11$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Combination of Springs · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 13)

![printed question 91](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q091_fd7b9a72_30102026.png?v=fd7b9a72962b)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 91](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q091_98cea6e7_30102026.png?v=98cea6e7a8c1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Cutting a spring raises its force constant in inverse proportion to length, so the three pieces are $6k$, $3k$ and $2k$; rejoined in series they give back $k$, in parallel they add to $11k$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  Treating the three pieces as three springs of constant $k$ gives the standard identical-spring result, series $k/3$ against parallel $3k$, which is $1:9$. Cutting changes each constant, so the pieces are $6k$, $3k$ and $2k$ and the ratio is $1:11$.
- **Why:** This question requires each cut piece's force constant to be found before either combination can be used, demonstrated by "cut into lengths of ratio $1:2:3$" being the only thing in the stem that fixes the three constants.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q92

Physics · Thermodynamics

Thermodynamic processes are indicated in the following diagram. Match the following

![printed figure: P-V diagram: four processes I-IV from state i on the 500 K isotherm, with 300 K, 500 K and 700 K isotherms](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q092_bf981710_30102026.svg?v=bf9817107037)

*P-V diagram: four processes I-IV from state i on the 500 K isotherm, with 300 K, 500 K and 700 K isotherms*

![drawn: P-V diagram: four processes I-IV from state i on the 500 K isotherm, with 300 K, 500 K and 700 K isotherms](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q092_question_07ec8c5b_30102026.svg?v=07ec8c5b745d)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

| List-I | | List-II | |
|---|---|---|---|
| P | Process I | a | Adiabatic |
| Q | Process II | b | Isobaric |
| R | Process III | c | Isochoric |
| S | Process IV | d | Isothermal |

- (1) P $\rightarrow$ d, Q $\rightarrow$ b, R $\rightarrow$ a, S $\rightarrow$ c
- (2) P $\rightarrow$ a, Q $\rightarrow$ c, R $\rightarrow$ d, S $\rightarrow$ b
- **(3)** P $\rightarrow$ c, Q $\rightarrow$ a, R $\rightarrow$ d, S $\rightarrow$ b
- (4) P $\rightarrow$ c, Q $\rightarrow$ d, R $\rightarrow$ b, S $\rightarrow$ a

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Process I = Isochoric
2. II = Adiabatic
3. III = Isothermal
4. IV = Isobaric

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Process I is a vertical line on the $P$-$V$ diagram, so $V$ is constant: isochoric, $P \rightarrow c$.
2. Process IV is a horizontal line, so $P$ is constant: isobaric, $S \rightarrow b$.
3. Process III starts at $i$ and ends at $f$ on the same $500\,\text{K}$ curve, following it: isothermal ($T$ constant), $R \rightarrow d$.
4. Process II is steeper than the isotherm and takes the gas from the $500\,\text{K}$ curve to the $300\,\text{K}$ curve as it expands, as $PV^{\gamma} = \text{const}$ requires: adiabatic, $Q \rightarrow a$.
5. $P \rightarrow c,\ Q \rightarrow a,\ R \rightarrow d,\ S \rightarrow b$ is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Different Graphs of P, V, and T, Thermodynamic Processes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 13)

![printed question 92](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q092_8ae2d2b1_30102026.png?v=8ae2d2b1c928)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 92](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q092_a0fb0c37_30102026.png?v=a0fb0c37bcf7)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Match the columns** (`match_columns`) — two labelled lists to pair, the answer is a pairing code
- **Asks:** **Figure reading** (`read_diagram`) — read a figure, graph or drawn structure and answer from it
- **Concept:** On a P-V diagram the geometry of a path names the process: vertical holds the volume fixed (isochoric), horizontal holds the pressure fixed (isobaric), a path that stays on one isotherm is isothermal, and one that ends on a cooler isotherm is adiabatic
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  'The adiabatic is steeper than the isotherm' is applied to the steepest thing drawn from i, the vertical segment I, so I is named adiabatic and the label isochoric is left over for II.
- **Why:** This question requires four printed paths to be named from their geometry on the diagram, demonstrated by 'Thermodynamic processes are indicated in the following diagram' with List-II holding adiabatic, isobaric, isochoric and isothermal.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a vector spec and was read from it: from state i on the 500 K isotherm, I runs vertically and ends on the 300 K isotherm, II is a curve that leaves 500 K and ends on the 300 K isotherm, III stays on the 500 K isotherm, IV is horizontal. That supports the printed key, Answer (3). The trap label follows codebook section 5.0's instruction to carry a retired name onto its live family: the device is one swapped pair in a match code, retired as wrong_pairing, whose live family is half_right, since option 2 holds two of its four pairs.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q93

Physics · Capacitance

A capacitor is charged by a battery. The battery is removed and another identical uncharged capacitor is connected in parallel. The total electrostatic energy of resulting system

- (1) Increases by a factor of 2
- (2) Increases by a factor of 4
- **(3)** Decreases by a factor of 2
- (4) Remains the same

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A capacitor C charged by a battery V in one rectangular loop](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q093_sol_1_0796aab8_30102026.svg?v=0796aab8e3ad)

*A capacitor C charged by a battery V in one rectangular loop*

![drawn: A capacitor C charged by a battery V in one rectangular loop](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q093_solution_1_aaa4939b_30102026.svg?v=aaa4939b04b1)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. Charge on capacitor
2. $q = CV$
3. when it is connected with another uncharged capacitor.

![printed figure: Charged capacitor C (charge q) connected in one loop with an identical uncharged capacitor C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q093_sol_2_abe9b06a_30102026.svg?v=abe9b06a4244)

*Charged capacitor C (charge q) connected in one loop with an identical uncharged capacitor C*

![drawn: Charged capacitor C (charge q) connected in one loop with an identical uncharged capacitor C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q093_solution_2_0d6f9afd_30102026.svg?v=0d6f9afdfb9d)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

4. $V_c = \frac{q_1 + q_2}{C_1 + C_2} = \frac{q + 0}{C + C}$
5. $V_c = \frac{V}{2}$
6. Initial energy
7. $U_i = \frac{1}{2}CV^2$
8. Final energy
9. $U_f = \frac{1}{2}C\left(\frac{V}{2}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{2}C\left(\frac{V}{2}\right)^2$
10. $= \frac{CV^2}{4}$
11. Loss of energy $= U_i - U_f$
12. $= \frac{CV^2}{4}$
13. i.e. decreases by a factor (2)

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Initially the capacitor holds charge $Q$ with energy $U = \frac{Q^2}{2C}$.
2. With the battery removed, the total charge $Q$ is conserved when the identical uncharged capacitor is connected.
3. Two identical capacitors in parallel give $C' = C + C = 2C$.
4. $U' = \frac{Q^2}{2C'} = \frac{Q^2}{2(2C)} = \frac{Q^2}{4C}$.
5. $\frac{U'}{U} = \frac{Q^2/4C}{Q^2/2C} = \frac{1}{2}$, so the energy decreases by a factor of $2$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Redistribution of Charges, Energy Stored in a Capacitor · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 13)

![printed question 93](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q093_570b0778_30102026.png?v=570b07787cd1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 93](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q093_8a91aefa_30102026.png?v=8a91aefa618b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** With the battery removed the charge is fixed, so sharing it with an identical capacitor doubles $C$ at constant $Q$ and halves the stored $Q^{2}/2C$
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 1  
  Holding the voltage at the battery's value and using $U = \tfrac{1}{2}CV^{2}$ with $C$ doubled gives twice the energy. With the battery removed nothing holds $V$; what is fixed is $Q$, and $Q^{2}/2C$ halves.
- **Why:** This question requires the factor by which one quantity moves when another is changed, demonstrated by options that are only factors — "Increases by a factor of 2", "Decreases by a factor of 2" — with no number anywhere in the stem to compute from.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q94

Physics · Mechanical Properties of Fluids

A U tube with both ends open to the atmosphere, is partially filled with water. Oil, which is immiscible with water, is poured into one side until it stands at a distance of $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ above the water level on the other side. Meanwhile the water rises by $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ from its original level (see diagram). The density of the oil is

![printed figure: U-tube open at both ends (Pa): oil column AB in the left limb, water in the right limb and bend; levels A, E, D, B-C marked with 10 mm and two 65 mm spacings](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q094_b54a0172_30102026.svg?v=b54a0172d2d4)

*U-tube open at both ends (Pa): oil column AB in the left limb, water in the right limb and bend; levels A, E, D, B-C marked with 10 mm and two 65 mm spacings*

![drawn: U-tube open at both ends (Pa): oil column AB in the left limb, water in the right limb and bend; levels A, E, D, B-C marked with 10 mm and two 65 mm spacings](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q094_question_4f1d46e7_30102026.svg?v=4f1d46e7b297)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- **(1)** $928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$
- (2) $650\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$
- (3) $425\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$
- (4) $800\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $h_{\mathrm{oil}}\,\rho_{\mathrm{oil}}\,g = h_{\mathrm{water}}\,\rho_{\mathrm{water}}\,g$
2. $140 \times \rho_{\mathrm{oil}} = 130 \times \rho_{\mathrm{water}}$
3. $\rho_{\mathrm{oil}} = \frac{13}{14} \times 1000\,\mathrm{kg/m^{3}}$
4. $\rho_{\mathrm{oil}} = 928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Water on the oil side is pushed down by $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ while it rises $65\,\mathrm{mm}$ on the other side, so the water column above level $BC$ is $h_w = 65 + 65 = 130\,\mathrm{mm}$.
2. The oil top stands $10\,\mathrm{mm}$ above the final water level, so the oil column is $h_o = 130 + 10 = 140\,\mathrm{mm}$.
3. Points $B$ and $C$ are at the same level in the same water, so $P_a + \rho_o g h_o = P_a + \rho_w g h_w$.
4. $\rho_o = \rho_w \frac{h_w}{h_o} = 1000 \times \frac{130}{140} \approx 928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Hydrostatics · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 14)

![printed question 94](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q094_bec369c2_30102026.png?v=bec369c210ac)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 94](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q094_862e2794_30102026.png?v=862e279459d5)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In a U-tube the oil column balances the water level difference, $\rho_{oil}h_{oil} = \rho_{w}h_{w}$, and the water falls in one limb by as much as it rises in the other
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 4  
  $800\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$ is the density oil is handed in standard hydrostatics problems, so it answers "how dense is oil" without any of this tube's level bookkeeping. Here the geometry fixes the ratio at $130/140$, giving $928\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$.
- **Why:** This question requires a number worked out from the printed lengths, demonstrated by "stands at a distance of 10 mm above the water level on the other side" and "the water rises by 65 mm" being the only data, and the options being four densities.
- **Note:** The figure is confirmatory rather than required: the stem's 10 mm and 65 mm already fix the oil column at 140 mm and the level difference at 130 mm, and the drawn spec's A, E, D and B-C levels with its 10 mm and two 65 mm spacings agree with that, so asks is calculate and not read_diagram. Worth recording that the commonest physics error here — taking the level difference as the printed 65 mm instead of 130 mm — gives $867\,\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}$, which this paper does not print, so no option carries it.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q95

Physics · Modern Physics

The de-Broglie wavelength of a neutron in thermal equilibrium with heavy water at a temperature $T$ (Kelvin) and mass $m$, is

- (1) $\dfrac{2h}{\sqrt{mkT}}$
- (2) $\dfrac{h}{\sqrt{mkT}}$
- **(3)** $\dfrac{h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$
- (4) $\dfrac{2h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. de-Broglie wavelength
2. $\lambda = \frac{h}{mv}$
3. $= \frac{h}{\sqrt{2m(\mathrm{KE})}}$
4. $= \frac{h}{\sqrt{2m(\frac{3}{2}kT)}}$
5. $\lambda = \frac{h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A neutron in thermal equilibrium at temperature $T$ has mean kinetic energy $E = \frac{3}{2}kT$.
2. Momentum from kinetic energy: $p = \sqrt{2mE} = \sqrt{2m \cdot \frac{3}{2}kT} = \sqrt{3mkT}$.
3. de-Broglie wavelength: $\lambda = \frac{h}{p} = \frac{h}{\sqrt{3mkT}}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** de Broglie Wavelength of Matter Waves · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 14)

## Q96

Physics · Gravitation

The acceleration due to gravity at a height $1\,\mathrm{km}$ above the earth is the same as at a depth $d$ below the surface of earth. Then

- **(1)** $d = 2\,\mathrm{km}$
- (2) $d = \frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{km}$
- (3) $d = 1\,\mathrm{km}$
- (4) $d = \frac{3}{2}\,\mathrm{km}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Above earth surface
2. $g' = g\left(1 - \frac{2h}{R_e}\right)$
3. $\Delta g' = g\frac{2h}{R_e}$ $\ldots(1)$
4. Below earth surface
5. $g' = g\left(1 - \frac{d}{R_e}\right)$
6. $\Delta g = g\frac{d}{R_e}$ $\ldots(2)$
7. From (1) & (2)
8. $d = 2h$
9. $d = 2 \times 1\,\mathrm{km}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At small height $h \ll R$: $g_h = g\left(1 - \frac{2h}{R}\right)$.
2. At depth $d$: $g_d = g\left(1 - \frac{d}{R}\right)$.
3. Set $g_h = g_d$: $\frac{2h}{R} = \frac{d}{R}$, so $d = 2h$.
4. $d = 2 \times 1\,\mathrm{km} = 2\,\mathrm{km}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Variation in g Due to Height and Rotation, Variation in g Due to Depth Inside the Earth · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 14)

![printed question 96](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q096_1868cbc3_30102026.png?v=1868cbc341b1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 96](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q096_c5f550f2_30102026.png?v=c5f550f2f41d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** $g$ falls off twice as fast with height as with depth — $g(1-2h/R)$ above the surface against $g(1-d/R)$ below it — so equal values need $d = 2h$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  Attaching the factor 2 to the depth rather than the height turns $d = 2h$ into $d = h/2$ and gives $\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{km}$. The 2 belongs with the height, because $g$ drops twice as fast going up as going down.
- **Why:** This question requires a value produced by setting two relations equal, demonstrated by the options being four lengths "$d = 2\,\mathrm{km}$", "$d = \frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{km}$" and so on, with the 1 km height as the only datum.

## Q97

Physics · Kinematics

The $x$ and $y$ coordinates of the particle at any time are $x = 5t - 2t^{2}$ and $y = 10t$ respectively, where $x$ and $y$ are in meters and $t$ in seconds. The acceleration of the particle at $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$ is

- (1) $-8\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$
- (2) $0$
- (3) $5\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$
- **(4)** $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $x = 5t - 2t^{2} \qquad y = 10t$
2. $\frac{dx}{dt} = 5 - 4t \qquad \frac{dy}{dt} = 10$
3. $v_{x} = 5 - 4t \qquad v_{y} = 10$
4. $\frac{dv}{dt}x = -4 \qquad \frac{dv}{dt}y = 10$
5. $a_{x} = -4 \qquad a_{y} = 0$
6. Acceleration of particle at $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$ is $= -4\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $v_x = \frac{dx}{dt} = 5 - 4t$.
2. $a_x = \frac{dv_x}{dt} = -4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, independent of $t$.
3. $v_y = \frac{dy}{dt} = 10$, so $a_y = \frac{dv_y}{dt} = 0$.
4. At $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$, $\vec{a} = -4\,\hat{i}\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, i.e. $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Instantaneous Acceleration · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 14)

![printed question 97](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q097_f24cff8f_30102026.png?v=f24cff8f0fe7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 97](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q097_7ee1de5f_30102026.png?v=7ee1de5f05e2)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Acceleration is the second derivative of position, so $x = 5t - 2t^{2}$ gives the constant $-4\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$ and $y = 10t$, being linear in $t$, adds nothing
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  Differentiating the $-2t^{2}$ term once leaves $-4t$, and the stem's $t = 2\,\mathrm{s}$ substituted into that gives $-8$. The second derivative is $-4$ and has no $t$ left to substitute.
- **Why:** This question requires a number obtained by differentiating the printed functions twice, demonstrated by "$x = 5t - 2t^{2}$ and $y = 10t$" and options that are four accelerations.
- **Note:** E1_question_type reads multi_part_respectively on the stem's word "respectively", but the printed shape has no multi-part answer: "respectively" attaches the two coordinate equations to $x$ and $y$, and the question asks for one number with four single-value options. Recorded as mcq, the shape on the page.

## Q98

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

In a common emitter transistor amplifier the audio signal voltage across the collector is $3\,\mathrm{V}$. The resistance of collector is $3\,\mathrm{k\Omega}$. If current gain is $100$ and the base resistance is $2\,\mathrm{k\Omega}$, the voltage and power gain of the amplifier is

- (1) $20$ and $2000$
- (2) $200$ and $1000$
- (3) $15$ and $200$
- **(4)** $150$ and $15000$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Current gain $(\beta) = 100$
2. Voltage gain $(A_{\mathrm{V}}) = \beta\frac{R_c}{R_b}$
3. $= 100\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)$
4. $= 150$
5. Power gain $= A_{\mathrm{V}}\,\beta$
6. $= 150\,(100)$
7. $= 15000$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Voltage gain of a CE amplifier: $A_V = \beta \frac{R_C}{R_B}$.
2. $A_V = 100 \times \frac{3\,\mathrm{k\Omega}}{2\,\mathrm{k\Omega}} = 150$.
3. Power gain: $A_P = \beta \times A_V$.
4. $A_P = 100 \times 150 = 15000$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Transistor as an Amplifier (Common Emitter Configuration): Voltage, Current and Power Gain · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 98](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q098_df4e2aaf_30102026.png?v=df4e2aafec07)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 98](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q098_b930c797_30102026.png?v=b930c7971d66)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In a common-emitter amplifier the voltage gain is $\beta R_C/R_B$ and the power gain is the current gain times the voltage gain, $\beta \times A_V$
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  The chain the printed numbers invite ends on 20: $I_C = 3\,\mathrm{V}/3\,\mathrm{k\Omega} = 1\,\mathrm{mA}$, $I_B = 10\,\mu\mathrm{A}$, input signal $= 10\,\mu\mathrm{A}\times 2\,\mathrm{k\Omega} = 20\,\mathrm{mV}$. That 20 is a voltage, not a gain, and carrying it into $\beta A_V$ gives the $100\times 20 = 2000$ printed beside it.
- **Why:** This question requires two numbers computed from the stem's four data, demonstrated by "the voltage and power gain of the amplifier is" and options that pair them — "$150$ and $15000$" — so half an option is worth nothing.
- **Note:** E1_question_type reads plain_mcq, but each option is a pair of values accepted or rejected whole, which is the printed shape recorded here as two_part. The stem's 3 V is not needed for either gain; it only fixes the collector current, which is why the 20 mV input signal is reachable without ever forming a ratio.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q99

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

An arrangement of three parallel straight wires placed perpendicular to plane of paper carrying same current ‘$I$’ along the same direction is shown in Fig. Magnitude of force per unit length on the middle wire ‘$B$’ is given by

![printed figure: Three parallel wires A, B, C perpendicular to the page (current out of the page); BC = d horizontal, BA = d vertical, 90 degrees at B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q099_fd317184_30102026.svg?v=fd317184c426)

*Three parallel wires A, B, C perpendicular to the page (current out of the page); BC = d horizontal, BA = d vertical, 90 degrees at B*

![drawn: Three parallel wires A, B, C perpendicular to the page (current out of the page); BC = d horizontal, BA = d vertical, 90 degrees at B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q099_question_54575f44_30102026.svg?v=54575f4410a8)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- **(1)** $\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{\sqrt{2}\pi d}$
- (2) $\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$
- (3) $\frac{2\mu_0 I^2}{\pi d}$
- (4) $\frac{\sqrt{2}\mu_0 I^2}{\pi d}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Force between $BC$ and $AB$ will be same in magnitude.

![printed figure: Forces F on wire B due to wires C and A, at 90 degrees](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q099_sol_1_f4d5a64e_30102026.svg?v=f4d5a64e3778)

*Forces F on wire B due to wires C and A, at 90 degrees*

![drawn: Forces F on wire B due to wires C and A, at 90 degrees](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q099_solution_1_e58b6e6e_30102026.svg?v=e58b6e6eeb3f)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

2. $F_{BC} = F_{BA} = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$
3. $F = \sqrt{2}F_{BC}$
4. $= \sqrt{2}\frac{\mu_0}{2\pi}\frac{I^2}{d}$
5. $F = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{\sqrt{2}\pi d}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. All three currents point the same way (out of the page), so each pair of wires attracts.
2. Force per unit length on $B$ due to $A$ (distance $d$): $F_A = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$, directed from $B$ toward $A$.
3. Force per unit length on $B$ due to $C$ (distance $d$): $F_C = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$, directed from $B$ toward $C$.
4. From the figure, $BA \perp BC$, so $F_A$ and $F_C$ are at $90^\circ$.
5. $F_{net} = \sqrt{F_A^2 + F_C^2} = \sqrt{2}\,\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d}$.
6. $\sqrt{2}\,\frac{\mu_0 I^2}{2\pi d} = \frac{\mu_0 I^2}{\sqrt{2}\pi d}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Force Between Two Parallel Current-Carrying Conductors · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 99](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q099_e9e46bb9_30102026.png?v=e9e46bb9978c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 99](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q099_5dd39da7_30102026.png?v=5dd39da76f69)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Each neighbouring wire pulls on the middle one with $\mu_0I^2/2\pi d$ per unit length, and the two pulls are at $90^\circ$, so the resultant is $\sqrt{2}$ times one of them
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  $\mu_0I^2/2\pi d$ is the force per unit length from one neighbour; wire $B$ has two of them at right angles, so the answer carries a factor $\sqrt{2}$ that this option has dropped.
- **Why:** This question requires an expression built by combining two computed forces, demonstrated by "Magnitude of force per unit length on the middle wire '$B$'" and four symbolic forms of $\mu_0I^2/\pi d$.
- **Note:** The figure is where the geometry comes from — $BC = d$ horizontal, $BA = d$ vertical, $90^\circ$ claimed at $B$ and measured within 1.5 deg in the drawn spec — but the demand it gates is the vector sum, so asks is calculate rather than read_diagram.

## Q100

Physics · Gravitation

Two astronauts are floating in gravitational free space after having lost contact with their spaceship. The two will:

- (1) Will become stationary
- (2) Keep floating at the same distance between them
- **(3)** Move towards each other
- (4) Move away from each other

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Both the astronauts are in the condition of weightness.
2. Gravitational force between them pulls towards each other.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In gravitation-free space there is no external gravitational field acting on the astronauts.
2. Each astronaut still attracts the other with $F = \frac{G m_1 m_2}{r^2}$, directed along the line joining them.
3. This attractive force gives each an acceleration $a_i = \frac{F}{m_i}$ towards the other, so their separation decreases, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Universal Law of Gravitation · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 100](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q100_4753c763_30102026.png?v=4753c763269c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 100](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q100_db341f88_30102026.png?v=db341f888483)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Every pair of masses attracts, so two astronauts far from any planet still accelerate towards each other under their own mutual gravitation
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  "Gravitational free space" is read as "no gravity at all", so no force acts and nothing changes. The phrase removes external fields only; the two bodies are still masses, and $F = Gm_1m_2/r^2$ acts between them.
- **Why:** This question requires the universal law of gravitation applied to a described case with no quantity produced, demonstrated by "Two astronauts are floating in gravitational free space" and options that are only outcomes — "Move towards each other", "Move away from each other".

## Q101

Physics · Thermodynamics

A Carnot engine having an efficiency of $\frac{1}{10}$ as heat engine, is used as a refrigerator. If the work done on the system is $10\,\mathrm{J}$, the amount of energy absorbed from the reservoir at lower temperature is

- (1) $100\,\mathrm{J}$
- (2) $1\,\mathrm{J}$
- **(3)** $90\,\mathrm{J}$
- (4) $99\,\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\beta = \frac{1-\eta}{\eta}$
2. $= \frac{1-\frac{1}{10}}{\frac{1}{10}} = \frac{\frac{9}{10}}{\frac{1}{10}}$
3. $\beta = 9$
4. $\beta = \frac{Q_2}{W}$
5. $Q_2 = 9 \times 10 = 90\,\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Carnot efficiency: $\eta = 1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1} = \frac{1}{10}$, so $\frac{T_2}{T_1} = \frac{9}{10}$.
2. Coefficient of performance of the same engine run as a refrigerator: $\beta = \frac{T_2}{T_1 - T_2} = \frac{1-\eta}{\eta}$.
3. $\beta = \frac{9/10}{1/10} = 9$.
4. Heat absorbed from the lower-temperature reservoir: $Q_2 = \beta W = 9 \times 10\,\mathrm{J} = 90\,\mathrm{J}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Refrigerator and Heat Pump, Carnot Engine · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 101](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q101_b5cb50a0_30102026.png?v=b5cb50a05cd9)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 101](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q101_2a2e369f_30102026.png?v=2a2e369f5df8)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Run backwards, a Carnot cycle of efficiency $\eta$ has coefficient of performance $(1-\eta)/\eta$, so $Q_L = W(1-\eta)/\eta$ — equivalently $Q_H - W$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  Taking the refrigerator's coefficient of performance to be the reciprocal of the efficiency, $1/\eta = 10$, gives $Q_L = 10\times 10 = 100\,\mathrm{J}$. The reciprocal of $\eta$ is $Q_H/W$, so 100 J is the heat delivered to the hot reservoir, and the cold reservoir gives up $Q_H - W = 90\,\mathrm{J}$.
- **Why:** This question requires a number produced by chaining the efficiency to the energy balance, demonstrated by "the work done on the system is $10\,\mathrm{J}$" and four energies as options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q102

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

A 250-Turn rectangular coil of length $2.1\,\mathrm{cm}$ and width $1.25\,\mathrm{cm}$ carries a current of $85\,\mu\mathrm{A}$ and subjected to a magnetic field of strength $0.85\,\mathrm{T}$. Work done for rotating the coil by $180^\circ$ against the torque is

- (1) $1.15\,\mu\mathrm{J}$
- **(2)** $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$
- (3) $4.55\,\mu\mathrm{J}$
- (4) $2.3\,\mu\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $W = MB\,(\cos\theta_{1} - \cos\theta_{2})$
2. When it is rotated by angle $180^\circ$ then
3. $W = 2MB$
4. $W = 2\,(NIA)B$
5. $= 2 \times 250 \times 85 \times 10^{-6}[1.25 \times 2.1 \times 10^{-4}] \times 85 \times 10^{-2}$
6. $= 9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Area of the coil: $A = (2.1\times10^{-2}\,\mathrm{m})(1.25\times10^{-2}\,\mathrm{m}) = 2.625\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$.
2. Magnetic moment: $m = NIA = 250 \times 85\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{A} \times 2.625\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2} = 5.578\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{A\,m^2}$.
3. Product with the field: $mB = 5.578\times10^{-6} \times 0.85 = 4.741\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{J}$.
4. Work to rotate from $\theta = 0^\circ$ to $\theta = 180^\circ$: $W = mB(\cos 0^\circ - \cos 180^\circ) = 2mB$.
5. $W = 2 \times 4.741\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{J} = 9.48\times10^{-6}\,\mathrm{J} \approx 9.5\,\mu\mathrm{J}$.
6. The nearest option is $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Work Done or Potential Energy of a Current-Carrying Loop in a Uniform Magnetic Field · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 102](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q102_c6f4af24_30102026.png?v=c6f4af24afeb)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 102](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q102_0ec6572a_30102026.png?v=0ec6572a2289)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A coil of moment $M = NIA$ turned right round in a field costs $W = U(180^\circ) - U(0^\circ) = 2MB$
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 3  
  Stopping at $W = MB$ instead of $W = MB\cos 0^\circ - MB\cos 180^\circ = 2MB$ halves the result, and $4.55\,\mu\mathrm{J}$ is exactly half the key's $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$.
- **Why:** This question requires a number assembled from five printed data, demonstrated by "$250$-Turn", "$2.1\,\mathrm{cm}$", "$1.25\,\mathrm{cm}$", "$85\,\mu\mathrm{A}$" and "$0.85\,\mathrm{T}$" with four energies as options.
- **Note:** Arithmetic recorded, not re-judged: the printed data give $2MB = 2 \times 250 \times 85\times10^{-6} \times 2.625\times10^{-4} \times 0.85 = 9.48\,\mu\mathrm{J}$, while the keyed option reads $9.1\,\mu\mathrm{J}$ (a width of $1.2\,\mathrm{cm}$ instead of $1.25\,\mathrm{cm}$ would give $9.1$ exactly). $9.1$ is still the nearest printed value by a wide margin, so the tag follows the printed key and the halving ladder is quoted against $9.1$.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q103

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

Which one of the following represents forward bias diode?

- (1) ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series between a 3 V end and a 5 V end](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q103_option_1_5beb261d_30102026.svg?v=5beb261d9efd) *A diode and a resistor R in series between a 3 V end and a 5 V end* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series between a 3 V end and a 5 V end](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q103_option_1_52c81e3b_30102026.svg?v=52c81e3b90e7)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $3\,\mathrm{V}$ — diode — $R$ — $5\,\mathrm{V}$
- **(2)** ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series between 0 V and -2 V](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q103_option_2_077440fe_30102026.svg?v=077440feb584) *A diode and a resistor R in series between 0 V and -2 V* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series between 0 V and -2 V](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q103_option_2_1c6a49cf_30102026.svg?v=1c6a49cfa90c)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $0\,\mathrm{V}$ — diode — $R$ — $-2\,\mathrm{V}$
- (3) ![printed figure: Diode then resistor R in series on one wire, -4 V at the left end and -3 V at the right end](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q103_option_3_d1f1ce9e_30102026.svg?v=d1f1ce9ec098) *Diode then resistor R in series on one wire, -4 V at the left end and -3 V at the right end* ![drawn: Diode then resistor R in series on one wire, -4 V at the left end and -3 V at the right end](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q103_option_3_293a7faf_30102026.svg?v=293a7faf0bfa)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $-4\,\mathrm{V}$ — diode — $R$ — $-3\,\mathrm{V}$
- (4) ![printed figure: A diode and a resistor R in series between -2 V and +2 V](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q103_option_4_c834cfc7_30102026.svg?v=c834cfc73c31) *A diode and a resistor R in series between -2 V and +2 V* ![drawn: A diode and a resistor R in series between -2 V and +2 V](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q103_option_4_b69aba76_30102026.svg?v=b69aba76aff6)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec* — $-2\,\mathrm{V}$ — diode — $R$ — $+2\,\mathrm{V}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In forward bias, $p$-type semiconductor is at higher potential w.r.t. $n$-type semiconductor.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In all four figures the diode triangle points to the right, so the anode ($p$-side) is on the left and the cathode ($n$-side) faces the resistor on the right.
2. Forward bias requires $V_{\text{anode}} > V_{\text{cathode}}$, i.e. $V_{\text{left}} > V_{\text{right}}$.
3. Option 1: $V_{\text{left}} = 3\,\mathrm{V} < V_{\text{right}} = 5\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is reverse biased.
4. Option 2: $V_{\text{left}} = 0\,\mathrm{V} > V_{\text{right}} = -2\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is forward biased.
5. Option 3: $V_{\text{left}} = -4\,\mathrm{V} < V_{\text{right}} = -3\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is reverse biased.
6. Option 4: $V_{\text{left}} = -2\,\mathrm{V} < V_{\text{right}} = +2\,\mathrm{V}$, so it is reverse biased.
7. Only option $2$ represents a forward-biased diode.

**Subtopic:** PN Junction Diode Under Forward Bias · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 16)

![printed question 103](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q103_5bc7fcd1_30102026.png?v=5bc7fcd1672e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 103](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q103_e94e86a6_30102026.png?v=e94e86a60923)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** A diode is forward biased when its p-side sits at the higher potential — what decides it is the difference between the two terminal potentials, not their signs
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  Comparing $-4\,\mathrm{V}$ and $-3\,\mathrm{V}$ by size rather than by value puts the $-4$ end higher and makes the diode look forward biased; $-4\,\mathrm{V}$ is the lower potential, so this one is reverse biased.
- **Why:** This question requires one rule applied to four printed cases with no quantity produced, demonstrated by "Which one of the following represents forward bias diode?" and options that are only terminal potentials across a diode and a resistor.
- **Note:** E1_question_type reads statement_options on "full-statement options", but no option is a statement: each is a linearised circuit, "$0\,\mathrm{V}$ — diode — $R$ — $-2\,\mathrm{V}$", so the shape recorded is mcq. Our record does not carry the diode's drawn orientation, but it is fixed by the key together with there being one answer: if any option had its p-side on the right, that option would also be forward biased (option 1's right end is 2 V higher, option 3's 1 V, option 4's 4 V), and two answers would be keyed. With all four p-sides on the left, option 2 is the unique forward-biased circuit, which is what the key reads — so the routes above are written on that reading.
