## Q104

Physics · Modern Physics

The ratio of wavelengths of the last line of Balmer series and the last line of Lyman series is

- (1) $0.5$
- (2) $2$
- (3) $1$
- **(4)** $4$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. For last Balmer series
2. $\frac{1}{\lambda_b} = R\left[\frac{1}{2^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right]$
3. $\lambda_b = \frac{4}{R}$
4. For last Lyman series
5. $\frac{1}{\lambda_l} = R\left[\frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right]$
6. $\lambda_l = \frac{1}{R}$
7. $\frac{\lambda_b}{\lambda_l} = \frac{\frac{4}{R}}{\frac{1}{R}}$
8. $\frac{\lambda_b}{\lambda_l} = 4$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The last line of a series is its series limit, the transition from $n_i = \infty$.
2. Balmer limit: $\frac{1}{\lambda_B} = R\left(\frac{1}{2^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right) = \frac{R}{4}$, so $\lambda_B = \frac{4}{R}$.
3. Lyman limit: $\frac{1}{\lambda_L} = R\left(\frac{1}{1^2} - \frac{1}{\infty^2}\right) = R$, so $\lambda_L = \frac{1}{R}$.
4. $\frac{\lambda_B}{\lambda_L} = \frac{4/R}{1/R} = 4$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Spectral Lines of Hydrogen Atom · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 16)

![printed question 104](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q104_fb6225bd_30102026.png?v=fb6225bd9743)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 104](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q104_65bcd485_30102026.png?v=65bcd4857f3e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The last line of a hydrogen series is its limit, $n\to\infty$, so $\lambda = n_1^{2}/R$ — $4/R$ for Balmer and $1/R$ for Lyman
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  The two limits are $4/R$ and $1/R$, so the ratio is 4. Reading it straight off the series' lower levels, 2 against 1, loses the square that $1/\lambda = R/n_1^{2}$ puts on them.
- **Why:** This question requires a number obtained from the series formula twice over, demonstrated by "The ratio of wavelengths of the last line of Balmer series and the last line of Lyman series" and four bare numbers as options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q105

Physics · Electromagnetic Induction

Figure shows a circuit contains three identical resistors with resistance $R = 9.0\,\Omega$ each, two identical inductors with inductance $L = 2.0\,\mathrm{mH}$ each, and an ideal battery with emf $\varepsilon = 18\,\mathrm{V}$. The current '$i$' through the battery just after the switch closed is

![printed figure: Battery ε and open switch feeding three parallel branches: L in series with R; R alone; R in series with L, where L is in parallel with C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q105_bd863851_30102026.svg?v=bd8638513c43)

*Battery ε and open switch feeding three parallel branches: L in series with R; R alone; R in series with L, where L is in parallel with C*

![drawn: Battery ε and open switch feeding three parallel branches: L in series with R; R alone; R in series with L, where L is in parallel with C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q105_question_2a73c158_30102026.svg?v=2a73c1584030)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $0$ ampere
- (2) $2\,\mathrm{mA}$
- (3) $0.2\,\mathrm{A}$
- **(4)** $2\,\mathrm{A}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Battery ε feeding three parallel branches: L1 in series with R1, R2 alone, and R3 in series with L2, where L2 is shunted by capacitor C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q105_sol_1_de7ef202_30102026.svg?v=de7ef202bee8)

*Battery ε feeding three parallel branches: L1 in series with R1, R2 alone, and R3 in series with L2, where L2 is shunted by capacitor C*

![drawn: Battery ε feeding three parallel branches: L1 in series with R1, R2 alone, and R3 in series with L2, where L2 is shunted by capacitor C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q105_solution_1_27c1f3d1_30102026.svg?v=27c1f3d1fa00)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. At $t = 0$, no current flows through $R_1$ and $R_3$

![printed figure: Battery of emf ε in one loop with resistor R2, current i clockwise](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q105_sol_2_7be22bb5_30102026.svg?v=7be22bb5b02e)

*Battery of emf ε in one loop with resistor R2, current i clockwise*

![drawn: Battery of emf ε in one loop with resistor R2, current i clockwise](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q105_solution_2_30cf7396_30102026.svg?v=30cf7396b574)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

2. $i = \frac{\varepsilon}{R_2}$
3. $= \frac{18}{9}$
4. $= 2\,\mathrm{A}$
5. Note : Not correctly framed but the best option out of given is (4).

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The figure shows three parallel branches across the battery: $L$ in series with $R$; $R$ alone; $R$ in series with ($L \parallel C$).
2. Just after the switch closes, an inductor opposes any sudden change of current, so $i_L(0^+) = 0$ and each inductor acts as an open circuit.
3. Branch with $L$ and $R$ in series: $i_1 = 0$.
4. Branch with $R$ alone: $i_2 = \frac{\varepsilon}{R} = \frac{18\,\mathrm{V}}{9.0\,\Omega} = 2\,\mathrm{A}$.
5. Branch with $R$ and $L$ (the stem lists only resistors, inductors and the battery, so the drawn $C$ is ignored): $L$ is open, so $i_3 = 0$.
6. $i = i_1 + i_2 + i_3 = 0 + 2 + 0 = 2\,\mathrm{A}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** LR Circuits · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 16)

![printed question 105](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q105_61112fbb_30102026.png?v=61112fbb5999)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 105](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q105_4ca6e9e2_30102026.png?v=4ca6e9e2b159)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** An inductor holds its own branch at zero current for the instant after switching, so only the purely resistive branch conducts: $i = \varepsilon/R$
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 1  
  The inductor rule — the current in it cannot start instantly — is applied to the battery current, but one of the three branches is a bare resistor with nothing to delay it, so $18\,\mathrm{V}/9\,\Omega = 2\,\mathrm{A}$ flows at once.
- **Why:** This question requires a current computed from the circuit at one instant, demonstrated by "$R = 9.0\,\Omega$ each", "$\varepsilon = 18\,\mathrm{V}$" and "just after the switch closed", with four currents as options.
- **Note:** Two things for a reviewer holding the paper, neither of which changes the tag. (1) Our stored figure carries a capacitor $C$ in parallel with the second inductor — it is in the drawn netlist and in the figure's own caption — but the stem's component list is "three identical resistors ... two identical inductors ... and an ideal battery", with no capacitor, and with an uncharged $C$ shorting that branch the current would be $4\,\mathrm{A}$, which is not among the options. Stem and option set therefore both say the third branch is $R$ in series with $L$ alone, and the tag follows that; the stray $C$ should go to figure repair. (2) The answer line as printed reads "Answer (4*)", so the source itself has flagged this question. The key is tagged as printed either way, since the route above does not depend on the capacitor: with or without it, the bare-resistor branch carries $2\,\mathrm{A}$ and $0\,\mathrm{A}$ is wrong.

## Q106

Physics · Laws of Motion

Two blocks $A$ and $B$ of masses $3m$ and $m$ respectively are connected by a massless and inextensible string. The whole system is suspended by a massless spring as shown in figure. The magnitudes of acceleration of $A$ and $B$ immediately after the string is cut, are respectively

![printed figure: Blocks A (3m) and B (m) hanging by a string below a spring fixed to the ceiling](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q106_3cbba937_30102026.svg?v=3cbba937d522)

*Blocks A (3m) and B (m) hanging by a string below a spring fixed to the ceiling*

![drawn: Blocks A (3m) and B (m) hanging by a string below a spring fixed to the ceiling](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q106_question_670913d1_30102026.svg?v=670913d1cdc4)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $\frac{g}{3}, \frac{g}{3}$
- (2) $g, \frac{g}{3}$
- **(3)** $\frac{g}{3}, g$
- (4) $g, g$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Free-body diagram of block 3m: kx upward, T and 3mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q106_sol_1_0fc0680d_30102026.svg?v=0fc0680da039)

*Free-body diagram of block 3m: kx upward, T and 3mg downward*

![drawn: Free-body diagram of block 3m: kx upward, T and 3mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q106_solution_1_8e6abb6e_30102026.svg?v=8e6abb6e5d2e)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. Before the string is cut
2. $kx = T + 3mg$ $\quad ...(1)$
3. $T = mg$ $\quad ...(2)$

![printed figure: Free-body diagram of block m: tension T upward, weight mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q106_sol_2_e045dacd_30102026.svg?v=e045dacd8a38)

*Free-body diagram of block m: tension T upward, weight mg downward*

![drawn: Free-body diagram of block m: tension T upward, weight mg downward](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q106_solution_2_77293531_30102026.svg?v=772935313396)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

4. $\Rightarrow kx = 4mg$
5. After the string is cut, $T = 0$

![printed figure: Free-body diagrams of blocks 3m and m just after the string is cut](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q106_sol_3_49d496a7_30102026.svg?v=49d496a7fc65)

*Free-body diagrams of blocks 3m and m just after the string is cut*

![drawn: Free-body diagrams of blocks 3m and m just after the string is cut](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q106_solution_3_6a59b69b_30102026.svg?v=6a59b69ba299)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

6. $a = \frac{kx - 3mg}{3m}$
7. $a = \frac{4mg - 3mg}{3m}$
8. $a = \frac{g}{3}\uparrow$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Before the cut, the spring supports both blocks: $F_s = (3m + m)g = 4mg$.
2. A spring cannot change its extension instantaneously, so just after the string is cut $F_s = 4mg$ still acts on $A$.
3. Net force on $A$: $F_s - 3mg = 4mg - 3mg = mg$ upward.
4. $a_A = \frac{mg}{3m} = \frac{g}{3}$.
5. After the cut, only gravity acts on $B$: $a_B = \frac{mg}{m} = g$.
6. So the magnitudes are $\frac{g}{3}, g$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Block and Spring Problem · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 16)

![printed question 106](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q106_b69f929c_30102026.png?v=b69f929ce64d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 106](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q106_3c72aedc_30102026.png?v=3c72aedce882)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A spring cannot change its length, and so cannot change its force, in the instant the string is cut: the upper block still feels $4mg$ upward while the lower block has only its own weight
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  Dropping the stem's "immediately" turns the cut into a release of everything, so both blocks fall at $g$. At that instant the spring is still stretched to $4mg$, which leaves $A$ with $(4mg-3mg)/3m = g/3$.
- **Why:** This question requires two accelerations computed for the same instant, demonstrated by "The magnitudes of acceleration of $A$ and $B$ immediately after the string is cut, are respectively" and options that pair them, "$\frac{g}{3}, g$".
- **Note:** E1_question_type reads multi_part_respectively and agrees with the page: each option is a pair of accelerations accepted or rejected whole, recorded here as two_part. The figure supplies the arrangement — ceiling, spring, $A$ at $3m$, string, $B$ at $m$ — but the demand it gates is the pair of computed accelerations, so asks is calculate and not read_diagram.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q107

Physics · Electromagnetic Induction

A long solenoid of diameter $0.1\,\mathrm{m}$ has $2 \times 10^{4}$ turns per meter. At the centre of the solenoid, a coil of 100 turns and radius $0.01\,\mathrm{m}$ is placed with its axis coinciding with the solenoid axis. The current in the solenoid reduces at a constant rate to $0\,\mathrm{A}$ from $4\,\mathrm{A}$ in $0.05\,\mathrm{s}$. If the resistance of the coil is $10\pi^{2}\,\Omega$, the total charge flowing through the coil during this time is

- (1) $16\pi\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- (2) $32\pi\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- (3) $16\,\mu\mathrm{C}$
- **(4)** $32\,\mu\mathrm{C}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\varepsilon = -N\frac{d\phi}{dt}$
2. $\left|\frac{\varepsilon}{R}\right| = \frac{N}{R}\frac{d\phi}{dt}$
3. $dq = \frac{N}{R}\,d\phi$
4. $\Delta Q = \frac{N(\Delta\phi)}{R}$
5. $\Delta Q = \frac{\Delta\phi_{\mathrm{total}}}{R}$
6. $= \frac{(NBA)}{R}$
7. $= \frac{\mu_0 n i \pi r^{2}}{R}$
8. Putting values
9. $= \frac{4\pi \times 10^{-7} \times 100 \times 4 \times \pi \times (0.01)^{2}}{10\pi^{2}}$
10. $\Delta Q = 32\,\mu\mathrm{C}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Field inside the solenoid initially: $B = \mu_0 n I = 4\pi \times 10^{-7} \times 2 \times 10^{4} \times 4 = 32\pi \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{T}$.
2. The coil lies inside the solenoid, so the flux uses the coil's own area: $A = \pi r^2 = \pi (0.01)^2 = \pi \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{m^2}$.
3. Initial flux linkage: $N\Phi = 100 \times 32\pi \times 10^{-3} \times \pi \times 10^{-4} = 32\pi^2 \times 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{Wb}$; final flux linkage is $0$.
4. Charge through the coil: $Q = \frac{\Delta (N\Phi)}{R} = \frac{32\pi^2 \times 10^{-5}}{10\pi^2} = 3.2 \times 10^{-5}\,\mathrm{C}$.
5. $Q = 32\,\mu\mathrm{C}$, which is option $4$ (the time $0.05\,\mathrm{s}$ does not affect the charge).

**Subtopic:** Faraday's Law of Electromagnetic Induction, Mutual Induction · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 17)

![printed question 107](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q107_fa35d860_30102026.png?v=fa35d860fd42)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 107](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q107_991c3ef7_30102026.png?v=991c3ef7a259)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The charge pushed round a circuit by induction is the flux change over the resistance, $q = N\Delta\Phi/R$, independent of how long the change takes
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  Two $\pi$'s arrive in the flux linkage, one from $\mu_0 = 4\pi\times10^{-7}$ and one from $A = \pi r^{2}$, giving $32\pi^{2}\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{Wb}$; the printed $10\pi^{2}\,\Omega$ cancels both, and carrying one through leaves $32\pi\,\mu\mathrm{C}$.
- **Why:** This question requires a number chained through three relations, demonstrated by "$2\times10^{4}$ turns per meter", "radius $0.01\,\mathrm{m}$", "$10\pi^{2}\,\Omega$" and four charges as options.
- **Note:** The diameter $0.1\,\mathrm{m}$ and the time $0.05\,\mathrm{s}$ are both unused in the answer: the solenoid's field does not depend on its diameter, and the time cancels out of $q = \Delta\Phi/R$.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q108

Physics · Units and Measurements

A physical quantity of the dimensions of length that can be formed out of $c$, $G$ and $\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}$ is [$c$ is velocity of light, $G$ is universal constant of gravitation and $e$ is charge]

- (1) $\frac{1}{c}G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}$
- **(2)** $\frac{1}{c^2}\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- (3) $c^2\left[G\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}$
- (4) $\frac{1}{c^2}\left[\frac{e^2}{G4\pi\varepsilon_0}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Let $\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}} = A = \mathrm{ML^{3}T^{-2}}$
2. $l = C^{x}G^{y}(A)^{z}$
3. $L = [\mathrm{LT^{-1}}]^{x}\,[\mathrm{M^{-1}L^{3}T^{-2}}]^{y}\,[\mathrm{ML^{3}T^{-2}}]^{z}$
4. $-y + z = 0 \Rightarrow y = z$ ...(i)
5. $x + 3y + 3z = 1$ ...(ii)
6. $-x - 4z = 0$ ...(iii)
7. From (i), (ii) & (iii)
8. $z = y = \frac{1}{2},\ x = -2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Let $k = \frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}$; from Coulomb's law $F = \frac{k}{r^2}$, so $[k] = [F r^2] = M L^3 T^{-2}$.
2. From $F = \frac{G m_1 m_2}{r^2}$, $[G] = M^{-1} L^3 T^{-2}$, and $[c] = L T^{-1}$.
3. $[G k] = M^{-1} L^3 T^{-2} \cdot M L^3 T^{-2} = L^6 T^{-4}$.
4. $\left[(G k)^{1/2}\right] = L^3 T^{-2}$.
5. $\left[\frac{1}{c^2}(G k)^{1/2}\right] = \frac{L^3 T^{-2}}{L^2 T^{-2}} = L$, a length, which is option $2$.
6. Check: option $1$ gives $L^5 T^{-3}$, option $3$ gives $L^5 T^{-4}$, option $4$ gives $\frac{M}{L^2 T^{-2}} = M L^{-2} T^{2}$; none is a length.

**Subtopic:** Deducing Relation Among the Physical Quantities Using Dimensional Equation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 17)

![printed question 108](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q108_a02792d1_30102026.png?v=a02792d14d34)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 108](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q108_e04fadf0_30102026.png?v=e04fadf093cb)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Dimensions fix the combination: $G\,e^{2}/4\pi\varepsilon_0$ has dimensions $L^{6}T^{-4}$, so its square root divided by $c^{2}$ leaves a length
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  $[G\,e^{2}/4\pi\varepsilon_0]^{1/2}$ is $L^{3}T^{-2}$, from which $L^{2}T^{-2}$ has to be taken OUT to leave a length; multiplying by $c^{2}$ instead of dividing gives $L^{5}T^{-4}$ and lands on this option.
- **Why:** This question requires an expression assembled by matching dimensions, demonstrated by "A physical quantity of the dimensions of length that can be formed out of $c$, $G$ and $\frac{e^2}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}$" and four symbolic combinations as options.
- **Note:** The demand is dimensional rather than numerical, and asks has no dimensional value; calculate was used because an expression is worked out and selected, not read off.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q109

Physics · Electromagnetic Waves

In an electromagnetic wave in free space the root mean square value of the electric field is $E_{\mathrm{rms}} = 6\,\mathrm{V/m}$. The peak value of the magnetic field is

- (1) $4.23 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$
- (2) $1.41 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$
- **(3)** $2.83 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$
- (4) $0.70 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\frac{E_{\mathrm{rms}}}{B_{\mathrm{rms}}} = c$
2. $B_{\mathrm{rms}} = \frac{E_{\mathrm{rms}}}{c}$
3. $= \frac{6}{3 \times 10^{8}}$
4. $B_{\mathrm{rms}} = 2 \times 10^{-8}$
5. $B_{\mathrm{rms}} = \frac{B_0}{\sqrt{2}}$
6. $B_0 = \sqrt{2} \times B_{\mathrm{rms}}$
7. $= \sqrt{2} \times 2 \times 10^{-8}$
8. $= 2.83 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The peak electric field is $E_0 = \sqrt{2}\,E_{\mathrm{rms}} = \sqrt{2} \times 6 = 8.485\,\mathrm{V/m}$.
2. In free space $B_0 = \frac{E_0}{c}$.
3. $B_0 = \frac{8.485}{3 \times 10^{8}} = 2.83 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Relation Between E, B, and Speed of Light · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 17)

![printed question 109](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q109_2945b9cb_30102026.png?v=2945b9cb2a9f)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 109](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q109_c28e1fbc_30102026.png?v=c28e1fbc3b4d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** $B_0 = E_0/c$, and the peak field is $\sqrt{2}$ TIMES the rms field, not the rms field divided by $\sqrt{2}$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  Taking $E_0 = 6/\sqrt{2} = 4.24\,\mathrm{V/m}$ and dividing by $c$ gives $1.41\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$; the conversion runs the other way, $E_0 = \sqrt{2}\times 6 = 8.49\,\mathrm{V/m}$, so $B_0 = 2.83\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$.
- **Why:** This question requires a number obtained by converting a field and dividing by $c$, demonstrated by "the root mean square value of the electric field is $E_{\mathrm{rms}} = 6\,\mathrm{V/m}$" with "The peak value of the magnetic field" asked and four fields as options.
- **Note:** Worth recording that the plainest error of all — answering with the rms magnetic field, $E_{\mathrm{rms}}/c = 2.0\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{T}$ — is not printed; the paper's three distractors are instead a $\sqrt{2}$ ladder around the answer.

## Q110

Physics · Current Electricity

The resistance of a wire is ‘$R$’ ohm. If it is melted and stretched to ‘$n$’ times its original length, its new resistance will be

- (1) $\frac{R}{n^{2}}$
- (2) $nR$
- (3) $\frac{R}{n}$
- **(4)** $n^{2}R$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\frac{R_2}{R_1} = \frac{l_2^2}{l_1^2}$
2. $= \frac{n^2 l_1^2}{l_1^2}$
3. $\frac{R_2}{R_1} = n^2$
4. $R_2 = n^2 R_1$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Melting and stretching keeps the volume constant: $A l = A' (n l)$.
2. So the new area is $A' = \frac{A}{n}$.
3. $R' = \frac{\rho (n l)}{A/n} = n^{2}\frac{\rho l}{A}$.
4. $R' = n^{2} R$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Resistance · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 18)

![printed question 110](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q110_ffb89c2b_30102026.png?v=ffb89c2b284c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 110](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q110_c1e03aa3_30102026.png?v=c1e03aa3d385)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** Melting and re-drawing a wire keeps its volume fixed, so stretching the length $n$ times thins the area to $1/n$ and resistance goes up as the square of the length
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  Taking $R=\rho l/A$ with the area left alone makes resistance proportional to length and gives $nR$; the word 'melted' is what fixes the volume, so the area must fall to $A/n$ as well
- **Why:** This question requires chaining $R=\rho l/A$ with the constant-volume condition to get the factor by which $R$ changes, demonstrated by 'if it is melted and stretched to ‘$n$’ times its original length'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q111

Physics · Ray Optics

The ratio of resolving powers of an optical microscope for two wavelengths $\lambda_1 = 4000\,\mathrm{\AA}$ and $\lambda_2 = 6000\,\mathrm{\AA}$ is

- (1) $16 : 81$
- (2) $8 : 27$
- (3) $9 : 4$
- **(4)** $3 : 2$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Resolving power $\propto \frac{1}{\lambda}$
2. $\frac{R_1}{R_2} = \frac{\lambda_2}{\lambda_1}$
3. $= \frac{6000\,\mathrm{\AA}}{4000\,\mathrm{\AA}}$
4. $= \frac{3}{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Resolving power of an optical microscope: $RP = \frac{2n\sin\theta}{1.22\,\lambda}$, so $RP \propto \frac{1}{\lambda}$.
2. $\frac{RP_1}{RP_2} = \frac{\lambda_2}{\lambda_1}$.
3. $\frac{RP_1}{RP_2} = \frac{6000\,\mathrm{\AA}}{4000\,\mathrm{\AA}} = \frac{3}{2}$, i.e. $3 : 2$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Resolving and Magnifying Power of Microscope · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 18)

![printed question 111](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q111_d1c66404_30102026.png?v=d1c66404519f)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 111](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q111_7985a7bb_30102026.png?v=7985a7bb06ca)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** A microscope's resolving power is $\frac{2\mu\sin\theta}{1.22\lambda}$ — one inverse power of wavelength, so the shorter wavelength resolves better in that same proportion
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  Holding the dependence as $1/\lambda^2$ instead of $1/\lambda$ turns the ratio $6000:4000$ into its square, $9:4$
- **Why:** This question requires the factor by which resolving power changes when the wavelength changes, demonstrated by the options being bare ratios for '$\lambda_1 = 4000\,\mathrm{\AA}$ and $\lambda_2 = 6000\,\mathrm{\AA}$'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q112

Physics · Ray Optics

A thin prism having refracting angle $10^\circ$ is made of glass of refractive index 1.42. This prism is combined with another thin prism of glass of refractive index 1.7. This combination produces dispersion without deviation. The refracting angle of second prism should be

- (1) $10^\circ$
- (2) $4^\circ$
- **(3)** $6^\circ$
- (4) $8^\circ$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $(\mu - 1)A + (\mu' - 1)A' = 0$
2. $|(\mu - 1)A| = |(\mu' - 1)A'|$
3. $(1.42 - 1) \times 10^\circ = (1.7 - 1)A'$
4. $4.2 = 0.7A'$
5. $A' = 6^\circ$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a thin prism the deviation is $\delta = (\mu - 1)A$.
2. Dispersion without deviation requires the two deviations to cancel: $(\mu_1 - 1)A_1 = (\mu_2 - 1)A_2$.
3. Substitute: $(1.42 - 1)(10^\circ) = (1.7 - 1)A_2$, so $4.2^\circ = 0.7\,A_2$.
4. $A_2 = \frac{4.2^\circ}{0.7} = 6^\circ$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Dispersion · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 18)

![printed question 112](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q112_6d22777d_30102026.png?v=6d22777da9ed)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 112](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q112_cbdc3519_30102026.png?v=cbdc35190e1c)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Dispersion without deviation needs the two thin prisms' deviations to cancel: $A_1(n_1-1)=A_2(n_2-1)$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  Setting $A_1 n_1 = A_2 n_2$ instead of $A_1(n_1-1)=A_2(n_2-1)$ gives $A_2 = 10\times\frac{1.42}{1.7} = 8.35^\circ$, which the paper prints as $8^\circ$
- **Why:** This question requires computing the second refracting angle from the zero-net-deviation condition, demonstrated by 'This combination produces dispersion without deviation'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q113

Physics · Wave Optics

Two Polaroids $P_1$ and $P_2$ are placed with their axis perpendicular to each other. Unpolarised light $I_0$ is incident on $P_1$. A third polaroid $P_3$ is kept in between $P_1$ and $P_2$ such that its axis makes an angle $45^\circ$ with that of $P_1$. The intensity of transmitted light through $P_2$ is

- (1) $\frac{I_0}{16}$
- (2) $\frac{I_0}{2}$
- (3) $\frac{I_0}{4}$
- **(4)** $\frac{I_0}{8}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Unpolarised beam I0 through polaroids P1, P3, P2 with their axes drawn as dotted lines marked 45° and 90°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q113_sol_1_72f580b4_30102026.svg?v=72f580b40489)

*Unpolarised beam I0 through polaroids P1, P3, P2 with their axes drawn as dotted lines marked 45° and 90°*

![drawn: Unpolarised beam I0 through polaroids P1, P3, P2 with their axes drawn as dotted lines marked 45° and 90°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q113_solution_1_f3b52495_30102026.svg?v=f3b5249513a1)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. $I_2 = \frac{I_0}{2}\cos^2 45^\circ$
2. $= \frac{I_0}{2} \times \frac{1}{2}$
3. $= \frac{I_0}{4}$
4. $I_3 = \frac{I_0}{4}\cos^2 45^\circ$
5. $I_3 = \frac{I_0}{8}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Unpolarised light through $P_1$: $I_1 = \frac{I_0}{2}$.
2. $P_3$ is at $45^\circ$ to $P_1$, so by Malus's law $I_3 = I_1 \cos^2 45^\circ = \frac{I_0}{2} \times \frac{1}{2} = \frac{I_0}{4}$.
3. $P_2$ is perpendicular to $P_1$, so the angle between $P_3$ and $P_2$ is $90^\circ - 45^\circ = 45^\circ$.
4. $I_2 = I_3 \cos^2 45^\circ = \frac{I_0}{4} \times \frac{1}{2} = \frac{I_0}{8}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Polaroids · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 18)

![printed question 113](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q113_7607497d_30102026.png?v=7607497d8dac)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 113](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q113_468bdd82_30102026.png?v=468bdd828513)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A polaroid passes half of unpolarised light, and Malus's law $I\cos^2\theta$ then applies at each later polaroid relative to the one before it
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 3  
  Stopping one polaroid early — after $P_1$ and $P_3$ — gives $\frac{I_0}{2}\cos^2 45^\circ = \frac{I_0}{4}$ and drops the last factor of $\cos^2 45^\circ = \frac12$
- **Why:** This question requires computing the intensity through a three-polaroid stack by applying Malus's law twice, demonstrated by 'A third polaroid $P_3$ is kept in between $P_1$ and $P_2$ such that its axis makes an angle $45^\circ$ with that of $P_1$'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q114

Physics · Current Electricity

A potentiometer is an accurate and versatile device to make electrical measurements of E.M.F, because the method involves :

- (1) A combination of cells, galvanometer and resistances
- (2) Cells
- (3) Potential gradients
- **(4)** A condition of no current flow through the galvanometer

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Reading of potentiometer is accurate because during taking reading it does not draw any current from the circuit.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At the balance point the galvanometer shows null deflection, $I_g = 0$, so no current is drawn from the cell under test and there is no internal-resistance drop.
2. Hence the measured value is the true EMF, $E = \phi\, l$, and the accuracy comes from the no-current condition, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Potentiometer · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 18)

![printed question 114](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q114_5a95b4ba_30102026.png?v=5a95b4ba20cd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 114](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q114_30c40fdf_30102026.png?v=30c40fdf5fac)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** The potentiometer measures emf by the null method: at balance the galvanometer carries no current, so the cell delivers none and its internal resistance drops no potential
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 3  
  'Potential gradients' is the true answer to what the potentiometer works ON, while the stem asks what makes the measurement accurate, which is the balance point where no current is drawn
- **Why:** This question requires the reason the textbook gives for the potentiometer's accuracy, demonstrated by the stem's 'because the method involves :' and by the key option naming 'A condition of no current flow through the galvanometer'.

## Q115

Physics · Waves

The two nearest harmonics of a tube closed at one end and open at other end are $220\,\mathrm{Hz}$ and $260\,\mathrm{Hz}$. What is the fundamental frequency of the system?

- (1) $40\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (2) $10\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- **(3)** $20\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (4) $30\,\mathrm{Hz}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Two successive frequencies of closed pipe
2. $\frac{nv}{4l} = 220$ ...(i)
3. $\frac{(n + 2)v}{4l} = 260$ ...(ii)
4. Dividing (ii) by (i), we get
5. $\frac{n + 2}{n} = \frac{260}{220} = \frac{13}{11}$
6. $11n + 22 = 13n$
7. $n = 11$
8. So, $11\frac{v}{4l} = 220$
9. $\frac{v}{4l} = 20$
10. So fundamental frequency is $20\,\mathrm{Hz}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A tube closed at one end supports only odd harmonics: $f = (2n-1) f_0$, i.e. $f_0, 3f_0, 5f_0, \dots$
2. So two neighbouring harmonics differ by $2f_0$: $2f_0 = 260\,\mathrm{Hz} - 220\,\mathrm{Hz} = 40\,\mathrm{Hz}$.
3. $f_0 = \frac{40}{2} = 20\,\mathrm{Hz}$.
4. Check: $\frac{220}{20} = 11$ and $\frac{260}{20} = 13$, both odd and consecutive odd numbers, so this fits; this is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Organ Pipe · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 19)

![printed question 115](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q115_450ea851_30102026.png?v=450ea851da44)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 115](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q115_6b91da19_30102026.png?v=6b91da19bfb9)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A pipe closed at one end sounds only odd harmonics, so successive harmonics differ by twice the fundamental, not by the fundamental
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 1  
  Taking the gap between neighbouring harmonics as the fundamental gives $260-220=40\,\mathrm{Hz}$, which is the rule for a pipe open at both ends, not for the closed one the stem specifies
- **Why:** This question requires computing the fundamental from the harmonic spacing of a pipe that sounds only odd harmonics, demonstrated by 'a tube closed at one end and open at other end are $220\,\mathrm{Hz}$ and $260\,\mathrm{Hz}$'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q116

Physics · Ray Optics

A beam of light from a source $L$ is incident normally on a plane mirror fixed at a certain distance $x$ from the source. The beam is reflected back as a spot on a scale placed just above the source $L$. When the mirror is rotated through a small angle $\theta$, the spot of the light is found to move through a distance $y$ on the scale. The angle $\theta$ is given by

- (1) $\frac{x}{y}$
- **(2)** $\frac{y}{2x}$
- (3) $\frac{y}{x}$
- (4) $\frac{x}{2y}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. When mirror is rotated by $\theta$ angle reflected ray will be rotated by $2\theta$.

![printed figure: Isosceles triangle: two arrowed sides of length x from the apex with apex angle 2θ, base spot displacement y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q116_sol_1_84082876_30102026.svg?v=840828765558)

*Isosceles triangle: two arrowed sides of length x from the apex with apex angle 2θ, base spot displacement y*

![drawn: Isosceles triangle: two arrowed sides of length x from the apex with apex angle 2θ, base spot displacement y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q116_solution_1_32162b96_30102026.svg?v=32162b96b70a)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

2. $\frac{y}{x} = 2\theta$
3. $\theta = \frac{y}{2x}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. When a plane mirror is rotated through $\theta$ with the incident ray fixed, the reflected ray rotates through $2\theta$.
2. The spot on the scale at distance $x$ moves by $y$, so $\tan 2\theta = \frac{y}{x}$.
3. For a small angle, $\tan 2\theta \approx 2\theta$, so $2\theta = \frac{y}{x}$.
4. $\theta = \frac{y}{2x}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Reflection at a Smooth Surface · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

## Q117

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

The given electrical network is equivalent to

![printed figure: Inputs A and B into a NOR gate, its output into a NOR gate with both inputs tied together, then a NOT gate giving output Y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q117_60652085_30102026.svg?v=60652085d4f0)

*Inputs A and B into a NOR gate, its output into a NOR gate with both inputs tied together, then a NOT gate giving output Y*

![drawn: Inputs A and B into a NOR gate, its output into a NOR gate with both inputs tied together, then a NOT gate giving output Y](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q117_question_c98d43cc_30102026.svg?v=c98d43ccea9b)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) NOT gate
- (2) AND gate
- (3) OR gate
- **(4)** NOR gate

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $Y = A + B$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The first gate is a NOR gate with inputs $A$ and $B$: $Y_1 = \overline{A + B}$.
2. The second gate is a NOR gate with both inputs tied to $Y_1$, so it acts as a NOT gate: $Y_2 = \overline{Y_1 + Y_1} = \overline{Y_1} = A + B$.
3. The last gate is a NOT gate: $Y = \overline{Y_2} = \overline{A + B}$.
4. $Y = \overline{A + B}$ is the NOR operation, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Logic Gates · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 19)

![printed question 117](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q117_83c88aa3_30102026.png?v=83c88aa3e3fe)

- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  Counting only one inversion after the first gate — treating the tied-input NOR as if it passed its signal through — gives $((A+B)')' = A+B$, an OR gate
- **Why:** This question requires tracing a drawn gate chain and applying the tied-input identity before anything can be named, demonstrated by the stem 'The given electrical network is equivalent to' with the middle gate's two inputs drawn joined to one wire.

## Q118

Physics · Oscillations

A particle executes linear simple harmonic motion with an amplitude of $3\,\mathrm{cm}$. When the particle is at $2\,\mathrm{cm}$ from the mean position, the magnitude of its velocity is equal to that of its acceleration. Then its time period in seconds is

- (1) $\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}$
- (2) $\frac{\sqrt{5}}{\pi}$
- (3) $\frac{\sqrt{5}}{2\pi}$
- **(4)** $\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{5}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $v = \omega\sqrt{A^{2} - x^{2}}$
2. $a = x\omega^{2}$
3. $v = a$
4. $\omega\sqrt{A^{2} - x^{2}} = x\omega^{2}$
5. $\sqrt{(3)^{2} - (2)^{2}} = 2\left(\frac{2\pi}{T}\right)$
6. $\sqrt{5} = \frac{4\pi}{T}$
7. $T = \frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{5}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Speed at displacement $x$: $|v| = \omega\sqrt{A^2 - x^2} = \omega\sqrt{3^2 - 2^2} = \sqrt{5}\,\omega$.
2. Acceleration magnitude at $x$: $|a| = \omega^2 x = 2\omega^2$.
3. Set them equal: $\sqrt{5}\,\omega = 2\omega^2 \Rightarrow \omega = \frac{\sqrt{5}}{2}\,\mathrm{rad\,s^{-1}}$.
4. $T = \frac{2\pi}{\omega} = \frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{5}/2} = \frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{5}}\,\mathrm{s}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Calculate Time Period of SHM · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 19)

![printed question 118](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q118_088b4a28_30102026.png?v=088b4a287396)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 118](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q118_fae1e65f_30102026.png?v=fae1e65f12a1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In SHM $v = \omega\sqrt{A^2-x^2}$ and $a = \omega^2 x$, so equating magnitudes at one position fixes $\omega$ and hence the period
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  I worked the two substitutions a prepared student actually makes wrongly here — using $v_{max} = \omega A$ gives $3\omega = 2\omega^2$ and $T = 4\pi/3$, and using $a_{max} = \omega^2 A$ gives $\omega = \sqrt5/3$ and $T = 6\pi/\sqrt5$ — and neither is printed. Reporting the frequency instead of the period gives $\sqrt5/4\pi$, which is also not printed; the two frequency-shaped options are $2\times$ and $4\times$ that value, so even the wrong-quantity route misses them. The remaining option carries $\sqrt3$, and no combination of the stem's $3\,\mathrm{cm}$ and $2\,\mathrm{cm}$ through these relations produces $\omega^2 = 3$.
- **Why:** This question requires computing $\omega$ from an equality of two SHM magnitudes and converting it to a period, demonstrated by 'When the particle is at $2\,\mathrm{cm}$ from the mean position, the magnitude of its velocity is equal to that of its acceleration'.
- **Note:** no_trap here is a finding about the option set, not a gap: the three wrong options are rearrangements of $\sqrt5$ and $\pi$ (and one $\sqrt3$) rather than the outputs of the two substitution errors this stem invites, both of which the paper leaves unprinted. Three fillers on one question is flagged by the brief as a reason to look again, which is why each proof names the arithmetic I tried.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q119

Physics · Kinematics

Preeti reached the metro station and found that the escalator was not working. She walked up the stationary escalator in time $t_1$. On other days, if she remains stationary on the moving escalator, then the escalator takes her up in time $t_2$. The time taken by her to walk up on the moving escalator will be

- (1) $t_1 - t_2$
- (2) $\frac{t_1 + t_2}{2}$
- (3) $\frac{t_1 t_2}{t_2 - t_1}$
- **(4)** $\frac{t_1 t_2}{t_2 + t_1}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Velocity of girl w.r.t. elevator $= \frac{d}{t_1} = v_{ge}$
2. Velocity of elevator w.r.t. ground $v_{eG} = \frac{d}{t_2}$ then
3. velocity of girl w.r.t. ground $\vec{v}_{gG} = \vec{v}_{ge} + \vec{v}_{eG}$
4. i.e, $v_{gG} = v_{ge} + v_{eG}$
5. $\frac{d}{t} = \frac{d}{t_1} + \frac{d}{t_2}$
6. $\frac{1}{t} = \frac{1}{t_1} + \frac{1}{t_2}$
7. $t = \frac{t_1 t_2}{(t_1 + t_2)}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Let the escalator length be $L$; her walking speed is $v_1 = \frac{L}{t_1}$.
2. The escalator's speed is $v_2 = \frac{L}{t_2}$.
3. Walking on the moving escalator, the speeds add: $v = v_1 + v_2 = \frac{L}{t_1} + \frac{L}{t_2} = \frac{L(t_1 + t_2)}{t_1 t_2}$.
4. $t = \frac{L}{v} = \frac{t_1 t_2}{t_1 + t_2}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Relative Motion in 1D · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 119](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q119_3345100c_30102026.png?v=3345100cd0ca)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 119](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q119_afa30174_30102026.png?v=afa301745ec7)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Walking speed and escalator speed add for the same staircase length, so the reciprocals of the times add: $\frac1t = \frac1{t_1} + \frac1{t_2}$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  Combining the two speeds by subtraction instead of addition — the relative-velocity reflex $v_1 - v_2$ — turns $\frac{t_1t_2}{t_1+t_2}$ into the printed $\frac{t_1t_2}{t_2-t_1}$
- **Why:** This question requires deriving the combined time from two speeds over a fixed length, demonstrated by 'She walked up the stationary escalator in time $t_1$' set against 'the escalator takes her up in time $t_2$'.

## Q120

Physics · Rotational Motion

Two discs of same moment of inertia rotating about their regular axis passing through centre and perpendicular to the plane of disc with angular velocities $\omega_1$ and $\omega_2$. They are brought into contact face to face coinciding the axis of rotation. The expression for loss of energy during this process is

- (1) $\frac{I}{8}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$
- (2) ${}_{2}I(\omega_1+\omega_2)$
- **(3)** $\frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$
- (4) $I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\Delta \mathrm{KE} = \frac{1}{2}\frac{I_1 I_2}{I_1 + I_2}(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
2. $= \frac{1}{2}\frac{I^2}{(2I)}(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$
3. $= \frac{1}{4}I(\omega_1 - \omega_2)^2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. No external torque acts, so angular momentum is conserved: $I\omega_1 + I\omega_2 = 2I\omega$.
2. $\omega = \frac{\omega_1+\omega_2}{2}$.
3. Initial kinetic energy: $K_i = \frac{1}{2}I\omega_1^2 + \frac{1}{2}I\omega_2^2$.
4. Final kinetic energy: $K_f = \frac{1}{2}(2I)\left(\frac{\omega_1+\omega_2}{2}\right)^2 = \frac{I}{4}(\omega_1+\omega_2)^2$.
5. $\Delta K = K_i - K_f = \frac{I}{4}\left[2\omega_1^2 + 2\omega_2^2 - (\omega_1+\omega_2)^2\right]$.
6. $\Delta K = \frac{I}{4}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Conservation of Angular Momentum, Kinetic Energy of a Rigid Body Rotating About a Given Axis · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 120](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q120_6b3ded58_30102026.png?v=6b3ded58fa10)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 120](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q120_26600dc1_30102026.png?v=26600dc12403)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Angular momentum is conserved when the two discs couple, and the kinetic energy lost is $\frac{I_1I_2}{2(I_1+I_2)}(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$, which for equal moments of inertia is $\frac14 I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Option 2 as this record stores it reads ${}_{2}I(\omega_1+\omega_2)$ — a stray leading subscript and no square — which is an angular momentum, not an energy, so one of the four options is not confirmed as printed and no trap mechanism can be read off the set.
- **Why:** This question requires computing the energy lost when two rotating discs couple under conservation of angular momentum, demonstrated by 'They are brought into contact face to face coinciding the axis of rotation'.
- **Note:** Option 2 is stored as '${}_{2}I(\omega_1+\omega_2)$'. The leading '${}_{2}$' is subscript debris and the bracket carries no square, so the expression has the dimensions of $I\omega$ rather than of energy; a reviewer with the printed paper should check whether it reads $\frac12 I(\omega_1+\omega_2)^2$ and then re-run this rating. The stem, the key and the other three options are intact and consistent: $\frac14 I(\omega_1-\omega_2)^2$ follows from $\omega_f = \frac{\omega_1+\omega_2}{2}$, so I am not disputing the key. The mechanisms a reviewer should expect once the option is repaired are option 4 (the shape $I(\Delta\omega)^2$ remembered without its coefficient) and option 1 (the coefficient halved to $\frac18$).
<!--/ecat-->

## Q121

Physics · Thermodynamics

A gas mixture consists of 2 moles of $\mathrm{O_2}$ and 4 moles of Ar at temperature $T$. Neglecting all vibrational modes, the total internal energy of the system is

- **(1)** $11\,RT$
- (2) $4\,RT$
- (3) $15\,RT$
- (4) $9\,RT$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $U = n_1\frac{f_1}{2}RT + n_2\frac{f_2}{2}RT$
2. $= 2\times\frac{5}{2}RT + 4\times\frac{3}{2}RT$
3. $= 5\,RT + 6\,RT$
4. $U = 11\,RT$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. With vibrational modes neglected, diatomic $\mathrm{O_2}$ has $f_1 = 5$ degrees of freedom (3 translational + 2 rotational).
2. Monatomic Ar has only translational motion, so $f_2 = 3$.
3. By equipartition, internal energy of $n$ moles is $U = n\frac{f}{2}RT$.
4. $U_{\mathrm{O_2}} = 2 \times \frac{5}{2}RT = 5RT$.
5. $U_{\mathrm{Ar}} = 4 \times \frac{3}{2}RT = 6RT$.
6. $U = 5RT + 6RT = 11RT$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Specific Heat Capacity of Monoatomic, Diatomic, and Polyatomic Gases · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 121](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q121_833d989d_30102026.png?v=833d989d067e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 121](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q121_a9c9e7a2_30102026.png?v=a9c9e7a21fb1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Internal energy counts degrees of freedom per mole: $\frac52 RT$ for each mole of a diatomic gas with vibration neglected and $\frac32 RT$ for each mole of a monatomic one
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  Applying $U = \frac32 nRT$ to the whole mixture gives $\frac32(6)RT = 9RT$ and treats the 2 moles of $\mathrm{O_2}$ as if they had only three degrees of freedom
- **Why:** This question requires totalling the internal energy of two species with different degrees of freedom, demonstrated by 'A gas mixture consists of 2 moles of $\mathrm{O_2}$ and 4 moles of Ar' with 'Neglecting all vibrational modes'.

## Q122

Physics · Mechanical Properties of Solids

The bulk modulus of a spherical object is ‘$B$’. If it is subjected to uniform pressure ‘$p$’, the fractional decrease in radius is

- **(1)** $\frac{p}{3B}$
- (2) $\frac{p}{B}$
- (3) $\frac{B}{3p}$
- (4) $\frac{3p}{B}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $B = \frac{p}{\left(\frac{\Delta V}{V}\right)}$
2. $\frac{\Delta V}{V} = \frac{p}{B}$
3. $3\frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{B}$
4. $\frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{3B}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. By definition of bulk modulus, $B = \frac{p}{\Delta V / V}$, so $\frac{\Delta V}{V} = \frac{p}{B}$.
2. For a sphere $V = \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3$, so $\frac{\Delta V}{V} = 3\frac{\Delta r}{r}$.
3. $3\frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{B} \Rightarrow \frac{\Delta r}{r} = \frac{p}{3B}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Relation Between Density, Pressure and Bulk Modulus · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 122](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q122_d466c00c_30102026.png?v=d466c00cf17e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 122](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q122_2b40b1e3_30102026.png?v=2b40b1e3654e)

---

<!--ecat-->

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Bulk modulus relates pressure to volume strain, and for a sphere the volume strain is three times the fractional change in radius
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 2  
  $p/B$ is the volume strain the definition of bulk modulus hands you, and it is offered where the fractional change in RADIUS was asked, so the chain stops one step short of the factor 3
- **Why:** This question requires the bulk-modulus definition converted from volume strain to radial strain, demonstrated by the stem asking for "the fractional decrease in radius" of a spherical object under uniform pressure.

## Q123

Physics · Laws of Motion

One end of string of length $l$ is connected to a particle of mass ‘$m$’ and the other end is connected to a small peg on a smooth horizontal table. If the particle moves in circle with speed ‘$v$’, the net force on the particle (directed towards center) will be ($T$ represents the tension in the string)

- (1) Zero
- **(2)** $T$
- (3) $T+\frac{mv^2}{l}$
- (4) $T-\frac{mv^2}{l}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Centripetal force $\left(\frac{mv^2}{l}\right)$ is provided by tension so the net force will be equal to tension i.e., $T$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Vertically, $mg$ and the normal reaction $N$ balance, and the table is smooth so there is no friction.
2. The only horizontal force on the particle is the tension $T$ along the string, directed towards the peg (centre).
3. Hence the net force towards the centre is $F_{net} = T$ (and this supplies the centripetal force, $T = \frac{mv^2}{l}$), which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Tension in String in Circular Motion · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 123](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q123_baaf00c9_30102026.png?v=baaf00c9ea24)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 123](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q123_2f9ff711_30102026.png?v=2f9ff7112ceb)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** For a particle in uniform circular motion the net inward force IS the centripetal requirement, and on a smooth horizontal table the tension is the only horizontal force supplying it
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  $mv^2/l$ is treated as a force acting in addition to the tension, so the two are added, when $mv^2/l$ is the value the single inward force must take
- **Why:** This question requires Newton's second law applied to circular motion with the forces present identified, demonstrated by the stem specifying a "smooth horizontal table" and asking for "the net force on the particle (directed towards center)".

## Q124

Physics · Rotational Motion

A rope is wound around a hollow cylinder of mass $3\,\mathrm{kg}$ and radius $40\,\mathrm{cm}$. What is the angular acceleration of the cylinder if the rope is pulled with a force of $30\,\mathrm{N}$?

- (1) $5\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- (2) $25\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$
- (3) $0.25\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}$
- **(4)** $25\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A rope leaves a cylinder of radius 40 cm tangentially and is pulled down with F = 30 N](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q124_sol_1_b3c99820_30102026.svg?v=b3c99820ee77)

*A rope leaves a cylinder of radius 40 cm tangentially and is pulled down with F = 30 N*

![drawn: A rope leaves a cylinder of radius 40 cm tangentially and is pulled down with F = 30 N](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q124_solution_1_d79beaa7_30102026.svg?v=d79beaa7f11c)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

1. $\tau = I\,\alpha$
2. $F \times R = MR^{2}\alpha$
3. $30 \times 0.4 = 3 \times (0.4)^{2}\,\alpha$
4. $12 = 3 \times 0.16\,\alpha$
5. $400 = 16\,\alpha$
6. $\alpha = 25\,\mathrm{rad/s^{2}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The rope pulls tangentially at the rim, so the torque is $\tau = FR = 30\,\mathrm{N} \times 0.4\,\mathrm{m} = 12\,\mathrm{N\,m}$.
2. For a thin hollow cylinder about its axis, $I = MR^2 = 3 \times (0.4)^2 = 0.48\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}$.
3. $\alpha = \frac{\tau}{I} = \frac{12}{0.48} = 25\,\mathrm{rad/s^2}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Torque of a Force About the Axis of Rotation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 124](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q124_2bd0c18d_30102026.png?v=2bd0c18d4d90)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 124](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q124_14a9d5ee_30102026.png?v=14a9d5ee97db)

---

#### Categories

- **Concept:** Angular acceleration is torque over moment of inertia, and for a hollow cylinder pulled by a rope at the rim this reduces to $\alpha = F/MR$ with the radius in metres
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 3  
  The radius is left in centimetres: $\alpha = F/MR = 30/(3\times 40) = 0.25$ instead of $30/(3\times 0.4) = 25$
- **Why:** This question requires torque divided by moment of inertia with the lengths converted, demonstrated by the stem giving "radius $40\,\mathrm{cm}$" and "a force of $30\,\mathrm{N}$" and asking for the angular acceleration.

## Q125

Physics · Wave Optics

Young’s double slit experiment is first performed in air and then in a medium other than air. It is found that $8^{\mathrm{th}}$ bright fringe in the medium lies where $5^{\mathrm{th}}$ dark fringe lies in air. The refractive index of the medium is nearly

- **(1)** 1.78
- (2) 1.25
- (3) 1.59
- (4) 1.69

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $X_1 = X_{\text{5th dark}} = (2 \times 5 - 1)\frac{\lambda D}{2d}$
2. $X_2 = X_{\text{8th bright}} = 8\frac{\lambda D}{\mu d}$
3. $X_1 = X_2$
4. $\frac{9}{2}\frac{\lambda D}{d} = 8\frac{\lambda D}{\mu d}$
5. $\mu = \frac{16}{9} = 1.78$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In a medium of refractive index $\mu$ the wavelength becomes $\lambda' = \frac{\lambda}{\mu}$.
2. Position of the $8^{\mathrm{th}}$ bright fringe in the medium: $y_8 = \frac{8\lambda' D}{d} = \frac{8\lambda D}{\mu d}$.
3. Position of the $n^{\mathrm{th}}$ dark fringe in air: $y = \frac{(2n-1)\lambda D}{2d}$, so for $n=5$: $y_5 = \frac{9\lambda D}{2d} = 4.5\frac{\lambda D}{d}$.
4. Equate the two positions: $\frac{8\lambda D}{\mu d} = \frac{9\lambda D}{2d}$.
5. $\mu = \frac{16}{9} \approx 1.78$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Fringe Width in YDSE · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 125](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q125_ac81b070_30102026.png?v=ac81b0706028)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 125](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q125_10cbdf19_30102026.png?v=10cbdf19e1c2)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Fringe spacing shrinks by the refractive index in a medium, and dark fringes sit half a fringe off the bright ones, so the 8th bright in the medium coinciding with the 5th dark in air gives $\mu = 8/4.5$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  The 5th dark fringe is placed at 5 fringe widths, using the bright-fringe positions for a dark fringe, which gives $\mu = 8/5 = 1.60$ and lands on the nearest printed value
- **Why:** This question requires the positions of a bright and a dark fringe equated across two media, demonstrated by the stem's "$8^{\mathrm{th}}$ bright fringe in the medium lies where $5^{\mathrm{th}}$ dark fringe lies in air".
- **Note:** The option values are rounded: the no-offset route gives exactly 1.60 and the nearest printed option is 1.59, which is where the trap is recorded. Two options have no derivable route at all and are recorded as fillers with the ratios checked.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q126

Physics · Electrostatics

Suppose the charge of a proton and an electron differ slightly. One of them is $-e$, the other is $(e + \Delta e)$. If the net of electrostatic force and gravitational force between two hydrogen atoms placed at a distance $d$ (much greater than atomic size) apart is zero, then $\Delta e$ is of the order of [Given mass of hydrogen $m_h = 1.67 \times 10^{-27}\,\mathrm{kg}$]

- (1) $10^{-47}\,\mathrm{C}$
- (2) $10^{-20}\,\mathrm{C}$
- (3) $10^{-23}\,\mathrm{C}$
- **(4)** $10^{-37}\,\mathrm{C}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $F_e = F_g$
2. $\frac{1}{4\pi\varepsilon_0}\frac{\Delta e^2}{d^2} = \frac{Gm^2}{d^2}$
3. $9 \times 10^{9}\,(\Delta e^2) = 6.67 \times 10^{-11} \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27} \times 1.67 \times 10^{-27}$
4. $\Delta e^2 = \frac{6.67 \times 1.67 \times 1.67}{9} \times 10^{-74}$
5. $\Delta e \approx 10^{-37}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Each hydrogen atom has net charge $(e + \Delta e) - e = \Delta e$.
2. Electrostatic repulsion between the atoms: $F_e = \frac{k(\Delta e)^2}{d^2}$.
3. Gravitational attraction between the atoms: $F_g = \frac{G m_h^2}{d^2}$.
4. Net force zero: $\frac{k(\Delta e)^2}{d^2} = \frac{G m_h^2}{d^2} \Rightarrow \Delta e = m_h\sqrt{\frac{G}{k}}$.
5. $\sqrt{\frac{G}{k}} = \sqrt{\frac{6.67 \times 10^{-11}}{9 \times 10^{9}}} = \sqrt{7.41 \times 10^{-21}} \approx 8.6 \times 10^{-11}$.
6. $\Delta e = 1.67 \times 10^{-27} \times 8.6 \times 10^{-11} \approx 1.44 \times 10^{-37}\,\mathrm{C}$, of the order $10^{-37}\,\mathrm{C}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Coulomb's Law · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 126](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q126_9ac307a3_30102026.png?v=9ac307a3a66c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 126](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q126_20f2604f_30102026.png?v=20f2604ffb86)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Setting the Coulomb repulsion of the charge imbalance equal to the gravitational attraction gives $\Delta e = m_h\sqrt{G/k}$, so the charge difference scales as the square root of the ratio of the two force constants
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  The square root is never taken: $m_h\,(G/k) = 1.67\times10^{-27}\times 7.4\times10^{-21} \approx 10^{-47}\,\mathrm{C}$, which is the printed option
- **Why:** This question requires equating the electrostatic and gravitational forces between two hydrogen atoms and solving for the charge difference, demonstrated by the stem's "the net of electrostatic force and gravitational force ... is zero, then $\Delta e$ is of the order of".
- **Note:** The separation $d$ cancels from the balance, so the only numbers that matter are $m_h$ and the two force constants.
