## Q127

Physics · Thermal Properties of Matter

Two rods $A$ and $B$ of different materials are welded together as shown in figure. Their thermal conductivities are $K_1$ and $K_2$. The thermal conductivity of the composite rod will be

![printed figure: Rods A (K1) and B (K2) welded one above the other, ends at T1 and T2, length d](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q127_7dfb3230_30102026.svg?v=7dfb32308c64)

*Rods A (K1) and B (K2) welded one above the other, ends at T1 and T2, length d*

![drawn: Rods A (K1) and B (K2) welded one above the other, ends at T1 and T2, length d](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q127_question_85e3c93b_30102026.svg?v=85e3c93bec5d)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) $2(K_1 + K_2)$
- **(2)** $\dfrac{K_1 + K_2}{2}$
- (3) $\dfrac{3(K_1 + K_2)}{2}$
- (4) $K_1 + K_2$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Thermal current
2. $H = H_1 + H_2$
3. $= \frac{K_1A(T_1 - T_2)}{d} + \frac{K_2A(T_1 - T_2)}{d}$
4. $\frac{K_{EQ}2A(T_1 - T_2)}{d} = \frac{A(T_1 - T_2)}{d}[K_1 + K_2]$
5. $K_{EQ} = \left[\frac{K_1 + K_2}{2}\right]$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure, rods $A$ and $B$ lie side by side between $T_1$ and $T_2$, each of length $d$ and (as drawn) equal cross-section $A$, so they conduct in parallel.
2. Total heat current: $H = \frac{K_1 A (T_1 - T_2)}{d} + \frac{K_2 A (T_1 - T_2)}{d}$.
3. For the composite rod of area $2A$: $H = \frac{K_{eq} (2A)(T_1 - T_2)}{d}$.
4. Equating: $K_{eq}(2A) = (K_1 + K_2)A \Rightarrow K_{eq} = \frac{K_1 + K_2}{2}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Connection of Rods · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 21)

![printed question 127](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q127_a28dcdb3_30102026.png?v=a28dcdb35032)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 127](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q127_348e0902_30102026.png?v=348e09025360)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Two rods welded side by side between the same two temperatures conduct in parallel, so the conductances add while the cross-section doubles, giving the mean of the two conductivities
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 4  
  The two conductivities are added and the doubled cross-section is not divided out, so the combined conductance $K_1A + K_2A$ is reported as a conductivity over the area $A$ instead of over $2A$
- **Why:** This question requires the parallel combination of two conductivities read off the arrangement, demonstrated by the figure showing rods $A$ and $B$ welded one above the other with both ends held at $T_1$ and $T_2$ over the same length $d$.
- **Note:** The figure was supplied as a checked drawn spec with all seven labels, so the parallel arrangement was readable; the series alternative, $2K_1K_2/(K_1+K_2)$, is not among the options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q128

Physics · Modern Physics

The photoelectric threshold wavelength of silver is $3250 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$. The velocity of the electron ejected from a silver surface by ultraviolet light of wavelength $2536 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$ is (Given $h = 4.14 \times 10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eVs}$ and $c = 3 \times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$)

- (1) $\approx 0.3 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$
- **(2)** $\approx 6 \times 10^{5}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$
- **(3)** $\approx 0.6 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$
- (4) $\approx 61 \times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2, 3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\lambda_{0} = 3250 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$
2. $\lambda = 2536 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$
3. $\phi = \frac{1242\,\mathrm{eV}\text{-}\mathrm{nm}}{325\,\mathrm{nm}} = 3.82\,\mathrm{eV}$
4. $h\nu = \frac{1242\,\mathrm{eV}\text{-}\mathrm{nm}}{253.6\,\mathrm{nm}} = 4.89\,\mathrm{eV}$
5. $\mathrm{KE}_{\mathrm{max}} = (4.89 - 3.82)\,\mathrm{eV} = 1.077\,\mathrm{eV}$
6. $\frac{1}{2}mv^{2} = 1.077 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}$
7. $v = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 1.077 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}}{9.1 \times 10^{-31}}}$
8. $v = 0.6 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{m/s}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $hc = 4.14 \times 10^{-15}\,\mathrm{eV\,s} \times 3 \times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}} = 1.242 \times 10^{-6}\,\mathrm{eV\,m}$.
2. Photon energy: $E = \frac{hc}{\lambda} = \frac{1.242 \times 10^{-6}}{2536 \times 10^{-10}} = 4.897\,\mathrm{eV}$.
3. Work function: $\phi_0 = \frac{hc}{\lambda_0} = \frac{1.242 \times 10^{-6}}{3250 \times 10^{-10}} = 3.822\,\mathrm{eV}$.
4. $K_{max} = E - \phi_0 = 4.897 - 3.822 = 1.076\,\mathrm{eV}$.
5. $K_{max} = 1.076 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J} = 1.722 \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J}$.
6. $v = \sqrt{\frac{2K_{max}}{m_e}} = \sqrt{\frac{2 \times 1.722 \times 10^{-19}}{9.1 \times 10^{-31}}} = \sqrt{3.78 \times 10^{11}} \approx 6.15 \times 10^{5}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$.
7. $6.15 \times 10^{5} \approx 0.6 \times 10^{6}\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}$, which is option $3$ (and equally option $2$, $6 \times 10^{5}$).

**Subtopic:** Photoelectric Effect · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 128](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q128_a0f3be2d_30102026.png?v=a0f3be2dd838)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 128](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q128_ebdb3fea_30102026.png?v=ebdb3fead704)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Photoelectric effect: light above the threshold hands the electron whatever energy is left over, so $hc\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{1}{\lambda_0}\right)=\frac{1}{2}mv^2$ fixes the speed
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  Solving $\frac{1}{2}mv^2 = E$ in one line as $v=\sqrt{E/2m}$ keeps every physical step right and puts the 2 on the wrong side, halving the speed to $3.1\times10^{5}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$ — the printed $0.3\times10^{6}$.
- **Why:** This question requires a speed computed from the energy left after the work function, demonstrated by the stem supplying 'threshold wavelength of silver is $3250 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$', the incident $2536 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$ and the constants $h$ and $c$.
- **Note:** Options 2 and 3 are the same number written two ways, $6\times10^{5} = 0.6\times10^{6}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$, which is why the printed answer line reads 'Answer (2 & 3)* Both answers are correct'; both are accepted answers, so neither is treated here as a wrong option and only options 1 and 4 are accounted for. E1's pattern called this statement_options on '15 full-statement options', but the four printed options are numerical speeds, so the shape recorded is mcq. Dropping the work function altogether gives $1.3\times10^{6}\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$, which matches no printed option, so condition_missed has nowhere to land.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q129

Physics · Waves

Two cars moving in opposite directions approach each other with speed of $22\,\mathrm{m/s}$ and $16.5\,\mathrm{m/s}$ respectively. The driver of the first car blows a horn having a frequency $400\,\mathrm{Hz}$. The frequency heard by the driver of the second car is [velocity of sound $340\,\mathrm{m/s}$]

- **(1)** $448\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (2) $350\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (3) $361\,\mathrm{Hz}$
- (4) $411\,\mathrm{Hz}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $f_A = f\left[\frac{v + v_o}{v - v_s}\right]$
2. $= 400\left[\frac{340 + 16.5}{340 - 22}\right]$
3. $f_A = 448\,\mathrm{Hz}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Source (first car) moves toward the observer with $v_s = 22\,\mathrm{m/s}$; observer (second car) moves toward the source with $v_o = 16.5\,\mathrm{m/s}$; $v = 340\,\mathrm{m/s}$.
2. For approach, $f' = f\,\frac{v + v_o}{v - v_s}$.
3. $f' = 400 \times \frac{340 + 16.5}{340 - 22} = 400 \times \frac{356.5}{318}$.
4. $f' = \frac{142600}{318} \approx 448.4\,\mathrm{Hz} \approx 448\,\mathrm{Hz}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Doppler Effect · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 129](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q129_6c4a2107_30102026.png?v=6c4a2107d487)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 129](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q129_06b29e01_30102026.png?v=06b29e019030)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Doppler effect with source and listener both moving: closing on each other, the listener's speed adds in the numerator and the source's subtracts in the denominator, $f'=f\frac{v+v_o}{v-v_s}$
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Every sign combination of $f'=f\frac{v\pm v_o}{v\mp v_s}$, with 22 and 16.5 m/s assigned to source and listener either way, gives 448, 406, 394 or 357 Hz, and holding one car still gives 419 or 428 Hz; the three wrong options printed — 350, 361 and 411 Hz — are none of these.
- **Why:** This question requires the heard frequency computed from both motions at once, demonstrated by the stem giving '22 m/s and 16.5 m/s respectively' with 'velocity of sound 340 m/s' and asking for 'the frequency heard by the driver of the second car'.
- **Note:** E1's pattern called this multi_part_respectively, but 'respectively' here only distributes the two car speeds in the stem and the answer is a single frequency, so the shape recorded is mcq. The finding worth carrying forward is that none of the three wrong values lies on any sign variant or one-car simplification of the Doppler relation, which is why the label is no_trap and not a forced reversed tag: the reversed reading yields 357.5 Hz, a value the paper does not print.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q130

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

Consider a drop of rain water having mass $1\,\mathrm{g}$ falling from a height of $1\,\mathrm{km}$. It hits the ground with a speed of $50\,\mathrm{m/s}$. Take $g$ constant with a value $10\,\mathrm{m/s^2}$. The work done by the (i) gravitational force and the (ii) resistive force of air is

- **(1)** (i) $10\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $-8.75\,\mathrm{J}$
- (2) (i) $-10\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $-8.25\,\mathrm{J}$
- (3) (i) $1.25\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $-8.25\,\mathrm{J}$
- (4) (i) $100\,\mathrm{J}$ (ii) $8.75\,\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $w_{g} + w_{a} = K_{f} - K_{i}$
2. $mgh + w_{a} = \frac{1}{2}mv^{2} - 0$
3. $10^{-3} \times 10 \times 10^{3} + w_{a} = \frac{1}{2} \times 10^{-3} \times (50)^{2}$
4. $w_{a} = -8.75\,\mathrm{J}$ i.e. work done due to air resistance and work done due to gravity $= 10\,\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Work done by gravity: $W_g = mgh = (10^{-3}\,\mathrm{kg})(10\,\mathrm{m/s^2})(10^{3}\,\mathrm{m}) = 10\,\mathrm{J}$.
2. Final kinetic energy: $K_f = \frac{1}{2}mv^2 = \frac{1}{2}(10^{-3})(50)^2 = 1.25\,\mathrm{J}$; initial $K_i = 0$.
3. Work-energy theorem: $W_g + W_r = K_f - K_i = 1.25\,\mathrm{J}$.
4. $W_r = 1.25 - 10 = -8.75\,\mathrm{J}$.
5. (i) $10\,\mathrm{J}$, (ii) $-8.75\,\mathrm{J}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Work–Energy Theorem, Work Done by Gravity or Spring Force · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 130](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q130_43fc9726_30102026.png?v=43fc97262224)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 130](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q130_c902ff18_30102026.png?v=c902ff186d8d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Work-energy theorem on a falling drop: gravity's own work is $mgh$ whatever speed the drop arrives with, and air resistance makes up the difference between that and the kinetic energy actually delivered
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 3  
  Starting from $W_{\text{net}} = \Delta KE$ gives 1.25 J, which is the work of ALL the forces together, and entering it on line (i) answers the net work rather than gravity's share of 10 J.
- **Why:** This question requires two works computed from one fall, demonstrated by every option printing both '(i)' and '(ii)' so that the gravitational and the resistive entry are graded together.
- **Note:** E1's pattern called this plain_mcq, but each option carries two values answered as one, so the printed shape recorded is two_part. half_right was considered and rejected: no wrong option pairs a correct entry with a failing one — options 2, 3 and 4 each fail on both lines — and '$-8.25\,\mathrm{J}$', printed on two options, is a one-digit shift of the correct $-8.75\,\mathrm{J}$ rather than a derivable value.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q131

Physics · Thermal Properties of Matter

A spherical black body with a radius of $12\,\mathrm{cm}$ radiates $450\,\mathrm{watt}$ power at $500\,\mathrm{K}$. If the radius were halved and the temperature doubled, the power radiated in watt would be

- **(1)** $1800$
- (2) $225$
- (3) $450$
- (4) $1000$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Rate of power loss
2. $r \propto R^{2}T^{4}$
3. $\frac{r_1}{r_2} = \frac{R_1^{2}T_1^{4}}{R_2^{2}T_2^{4}}$
4. $= 4 \times \frac{1}{16}$
5. $\frac{450}{r_2} = \frac{1}{4}$
6. $r_2 = 1800\,\mathrm{watt}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. By Stefan-Boltzmann law for a black sphere, $P = \sigma (4\pi r^2) T^4$, so $P \propto r^2 T^4$.
2. $\frac{P'}{P} = \left(\frac{r'}{r}\right)^2 \left(\frac{T'}{T}\right)^4 = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 (2)^4$.
3. $\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 (2)^4 = \frac{1}{4} \times 16 = 4$.
4. $P' = 4 \times 450\,\mathrm{W} = 1800\,\mathrm{W}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Radiation Laws · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 22)

![printed question 131](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q131_c46a0468_30102026.png?v=c46a0468db98)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 131](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q131_650dd74d_30102026.png?v=650dd74d4683)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** Stefan's law: radiated power goes as $r^2T^4$, so the quarter lost by halving the radius is overtaken sixteen times over by doubling the temperature
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  Carrying Stefan's law as $r^2T^2$ makes the quarter from the halved radius and the fourfold from the doubled temperature cancel exactly, so the power reads as unchanged at the stem's own 450 W instead of 1800 W.
- **Why:** This question requires the factor by which the radiated power changes when two quantities change, demonstrated by 'If the radius were halved and the temperature doubled' with the original 450 W supplied and the radius of 12 cm never needed as a length.
- **Note:** asks is relation rather than calculate because the whole of the work is the scaling factor, $\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2(2)^4 = 4$, applied once to the printed 450 W; no quantity is built from the stem's numbers in any other way.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q132

Physics · Electrostatics

The diagrams below show regions of equipotentials. A positive charge is moved from $A$ to $B$ in each diagram.

![printed figure: Four regions of equipotentials (a)-(d), a charge moved from A to B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q132_69192377_30102026.svg?v=691923778039)

*Four regions of equipotentials (a)-(d), a charge moved from A to B*

![drawn: Four regions of equipotentials (a)-(d), a charge moved from A to B](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q132_question_7b7f4130_30102026.svg?v=7b7f4130a5ee)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its figure spec*

- (1) Maximum work is required to move $q$ in figure (b).
- (2) Maximum work is required to move $q$ in figure (c).
- **(3)** In all the four cases the work done is the same.
- (4) Minimum work is required to move $q$ in figure (a).

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Work done $w = q\Delta V$
2. $\Delta V$ is same in all the cases so work is done will be same in all the cases.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Read the figure: in (a), (b), (c) and (d), point $A$ lies on the $10\,\mathrm{V}$ equipotential and point $B$ lies on the $40\,\mathrm{V}$ equipotential.
2. The electrostatic field is conservative, so the work needed to move $q$ is $W = q(V_B - V_A)$, which is independent of the path and the shape of the equipotentials.
3. $W = q(40 - 10)\,\mathrm{V} = 30q$ for each of the four figures.
4. So the work done is the same in all four cases, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Equipotential Surfaces · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 132](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q132_06260c71_30102026.png?v=06260c717109)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 132](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q132_b7ac8444_30102026.png?v=b7ac8444a5d8)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Figure reading** (`read_diagram`) — read a figure, graph or drawn structure and answer from it
- **Concept:** Work carrying a charge between two equipotentials is $q\Delta V$ and nothing else: the spacing of the surfaces, their curvature and the length of the trip do not enter
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  Reading the panels as fields and using $W=qEd$ with the drawn separation picks figure (c), where A and B are furthest apart and the equipotentials are most widely spaced, instead of reading off that A sits on 10 V and B on 40 V in every panel.
- **Why:** This question requires the potentials at A and B read off each panel, demonstrated by the printed key 'In all the four cases the work done is the same' while the panels differ only in the shape and the spacing of their equipotentials.
- **Note:** I read the figure from the drawn spec supplied with this question, whose print_match check passed on all 28 printed labels: in each of (a)-(d) the dot marked A lies on the 10 V surface and the dot marked B on the 40 V surface, so $\Delta V = 30$ V throughout. E1's pattern called this statement_options; no lettered statement block is printed and the stem is an instruction about the diagrams, so under this vocabulary the shape is mcq and the figure's role is carried by asks: read_diagram.

## Q133

Physics · Rotational Motion

Which of the following statements are correct?

- **(a)** Centre of mass of a body always coincides with the centre of gravity of the body.
- **(b)** Centre of mass of a body is the point at which the total gravitational torque on the body is zero
- **(c)** A couple on a body produce both translational and rotational motion in a body.
- **(d)** Mechanical advantage greater than one means that small effort can be used to lift a large load.

- (1) (c) and (d)
- **(2)** (b) and (d)
- (3) (a) and (b)
- (4) (b) and (c)

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Centre of mass may or may not coincide with centre of gravity.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. (a): $\vec{r}_{CM} = \frac{\sum m_i \vec{r}_i}{\sum m_i}$ while $\vec{r}_{CG}$ weights by $m_i g_i$; they coincide only when $g$ is uniform, so 'always' makes (a) false.
2. (b): With uniform $g$, $\vec{\tau} = \sum \vec{r}_i \times m_i \vec{g} = \left(\sum m_i \vec{r}_i\right) \times \vec{g} = 0$ about the CM, so (b) is true.
3. (c): A couple has $\sum \vec{F} = 0$, so $\vec{a}_{CM} = 0$; it produces only rotation, so (c) is false.
4. (d): $MA = \frac{\text{Load}}{\text{Effort}} > 1 \Rightarrow \text{Effort} < \text{Load}$, so (d) is true.
5. True statements are (b) and (d), which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Equilibrium of a Rigid Body · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 133](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q133_2c4b05e3_30102026.png?v=2c4b05e31cc1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 133](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q133_99033bd7_30102026.png?v=99033bd7f801)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Centre of gravity is the point where the total gravitational torque vanishes and it falls on the centre of mass only while the field is uniform; a couple, being two equal and opposite forces, turns a body without moving it
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 3  
  One remembered sentence - that the centre of mass and the centre of gravity are the same point - vouches for (a) and (b) together, and (b) is indeed correct, but (a) adds 'always' and fails for a body large enough for gravity to vary across it.
- **Why:** This question requires each printed sentence to be checked against what the chapter states about its own term, demonstrated by 'Which of the following statements are correct?' standing above four lettered claims whose options select pairs of them.
- **Note:** Statement (b) is the chapter's definition of the centre of GRAVITY applied to the centre of mass, which holds in the uniform field assumed throughout that chapter; the key accepts (b) while rejecting (a) for its 'always', and I have tagged the question as printed. asks is fact_line rather than compare because only (a) and (b) ask for two neighbouring terms to be held apart, while (c) on couples and (d) on mechanical advantage are separate chapter lines adjudicated on their own, so a comparison does not describe what the question as a whole demands.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q134

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

If $\theta_1$ and $\theta_2$ be the apparent angles of dip observed in two vertical planes at right angles to each other, then the true angle of dip $\theta$ is given by

- (1) $\tan^2\theta = \tan^2\theta_1 - \tan^2\theta_2$
- **(2)** $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$
- (3) $\tan^2\theta = \tan^2\theta_1 + \tan^2\theta_2$
- (4) $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 - \cot^2\theta_2$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Let the first vertical plane make angle $\alpha$ with the magnetic meridian, so the second (perpendicular) plane makes $90^\circ - \alpha$ with it.
2. The vertical component $V$ is the same in every vertical plane; the horizontal component in the first plane is $H\cos\alpha$, so $\tan\theta_1 = \frac{V}{H\cos\alpha} = \frac{\tan\theta}{\cos\alpha}$.
3. Hence $\cot\theta_1 = \cot\theta\cos\alpha$.
4. In the second plane the horizontal component is $H\sin\alpha$, so $\tan\theta_2 = \frac{V}{H\sin\alpha} = \frac{\tan\theta}{\sin\alpha}$, giving $\cot\theta_2 = \cot\theta\sin\alpha$.
5. Squaring and adding: $\cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2 = \cot^2\theta(\cos^2\alpha + \sin^2\alpha) = \cot^2\theta$.
6. So $\cot^2\theta = \cot^2\theta_1 + \cot^2\theta_2$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Earth's Magnetism · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 134](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q134_3304b4df_30102026.png?v=3304b4dfb281)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 134](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q134_1c0d0fef_30102026.png?v=1c0d0fef689e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Apparent dip: a vertical plane shows only the part of the horizontal field lying in it, so the cotangents of two perpendicular apparent dips square-add to the cotangent of the true dip
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  Keeping 'the squares add' but attaching it to tangent, the function the chapter uses for dip, prints $\tan^2\theta = \tan^2\theta_1 + \tan^2\theta_2$ — the correct relation with every term replaced by its reciprocal.
- **Why:** This question requires the relation between the true dip and two perpendicular apparent dips to be derived, demonstrated by all four options being symbolic identities in $\theta$, $\theta_1$ and $\theta_2$ with no numbers supplied.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q135

Physics · Modern Physics

> ⚠ The printed solution says "No option is correct": the question has no correct option as printed, and the printed answer comes from a corrected reading.

Radioactive material 'A' has decay constant '$8\lambda$' and material 'B' has decay constant '$\lambda$'. Initially they have same number of nuclei. After what time, the ratio of number of nuclei of material 'B' to that 'A' will be $\frac{1}{e}$?

- (1) $\frac{1}{9\lambda}$
- (2) $\frac{1}{\lambda}$
- **(3)** $\frac{1}{7\lambda}$
- (4) $\frac{1}{8\lambda}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. No option is correct
2. If we take $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = \frac{1}{e}$
3. Then $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = \frac{e^{-8\lambda t}}{e^{-\lambda t}}$
4. $\frac{1}{e} = e^{-7\lambda t}$
5. $-1 = -7\lambda t$
6. $t = \frac{1}{7\lambda}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $N_A = N_0 e^{-8\lambda t}$ and $N_B = N_0 e^{-\lambda t}$, since both start with $N_0$ nuclei.
2. $\frac{N_B}{N_A} = \frac{e^{-\lambda t}}{e^{-8\lambda t}} = e^{7\lambda t}$, which is $\ge 1$ for $t \ge 0$ because $B$ decays more slowly.
3. Taken literally, $e^{7\lambda t} = e^{-1}$ gives $t = -\frac{1}{7\lambda}$, a negative time, so the intended ratio must be $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = \frac{1}{e}$ (equivalently $\frac{N_B}{N_A} = e$).
4. $\frac{N_A}{N_B} = e^{-7\lambda t} = e^{-1} \Rightarrow 7\lambda t = 1$.
5. $t = \frac{1}{7\lambda}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Law of Radioactive Decay · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 23)

![printed question 135](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q135_7f76d4dc_30102026.png?v=7f76d4dc207f)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 135](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q135_aa5e009a_30102026.png?v=aa5e009a44aa)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Each sample runs down its own exponential, so the ratio of two samples that started equal changes with the DIFFERENCE of their decay constants, $8\lambda - \lambda = 7\lambda$
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  Dividing $e^{-8\lambda t}$ by $e^{-\lambda t}$ while adding the exponents instead of subtracting gives $e^{-9\lambda t} = 1/e$ and so $t = \frac{1}{9\lambda}$.
- **Why:** This question requires solving for the time at which an exponential ratio reaches a stated value, demonstrated by the stem fixing "decay constant '$8\lambda$'" and "decay constant '$\lambda$'" with 'same number of nuclei' initially and asking 'after what time' the ratio is $\frac{1}{e}$.
- **Note:** As printed the ratio asked for is B to A, and since B decays the more slowly that ratio equals $e^{+7\lambda t}$, which never reaches $\frac{1}{e}$ for positive $t$; the printed key, option 3, is what $N_A/N_B = \frac{1}{e}$ gives. I have tagged the question as printed and recorded the doubt in the verdict rather than re-judging the key. The exponent that decides it is $8\lambda - \lambda = 7\lambda$ whichever way up the ratio is read, so the routes to the three wrong options are unaffected.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q136

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

The equilibrium constants of the following are.
$\mathrm{N_2 + 3H_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NH_3} \quad K_1$
$\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO} \quad K_2$
$\mathrm{H_2 + \frac{1}{2}O_2 \longrightarrow H_2O} \quad K_3$
The equilibrium constant (K) of the reaction
$\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \overset{K}{\rightleftharpoons} 2NO + 3H_2O}$, will be

- (1) $K_2^3K_3/K_1$
- (2) $K_1K_3^3/K_2$
- **(3)** $K_2K_3^3/K_1$
- (4) $K_2K_3/K_1$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. (I) $\mathrm{N_2 + 3H_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NH_3}$; $K_1 = \frac{[\mathrm{NH_3}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{H_2}]^3}$
2. (II) $\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$; $K_2 = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]}$
3. (III) $\mathrm{H_2 + \frac{1}{2}O_2 \longrightarrow H_2O}$; $K_3 = \frac{[\mathrm{H_2O}]}{[\mathrm{H_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]^{1/2}}$
4. $(\mathrm{II} + 3 \times \mathrm{III} - \mathrm{II})$ will give
5. $\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \overset{K}{\rightleftharpoons} 2NO + 3H_2O}$;
6. $\therefore K = K_2 \times K_3^{3} / K_1$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Reverse reaction 1: $\mathrm{2NH_3 \rightleftharpoons N_2 + 3H_2}$, with $K = \frac{1}{K_1}$.
2. Keep reaction 2 as written: $\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$, with $K = K_2$.
3. Multiply reaction 3 by 3: $\mathrm{3H_2 + \frac{3}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons 3H_2O}$, with $K = K_3^3$.
4. Add the three: $\mathrm{N_2}$ and $\mathrm{3H_2}$ cancel, $\mathrm{O_2}$ totals $1 + \frac{3}{2} = \frac{5}{2}$, giving $\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO + 3H_2O}$.
5. Constants multiply when reactions add: $K = \frac{1}{K_1} \times K_2 \times K_3^3 = \frac{K_2 K_3^3}{K_1}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Law of Chemical Equilibrium · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 136](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q136_61305efb_30102026.png?v=61305efb4d17)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 136](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q136_c72e51b1_30102026.png?v=c72e51b1ea3d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Combining equilibria: reversing an equation inverts its K, and taking an equation n times raises its K to the nth power
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 4  
  A student who reverses the ammonia synthesis and adds the other two equations once each writes $K_2K_3/K_1$, because the water equation has to be taken three times to supply $3\mathrm{H_2O}$ and its constant cubed.
- **Why:** This question requires chaining three printed equilibrium constants with the correct inversion and powers, demonstrated by the target line '$\mathrm{2NH_3 + \frac{5}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO + 3H_2O}$', whose $3\mathrm{H_2O}$ forces the third equation to be taken three times.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q137

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

The heating of phenyl-methyl ethers with $\mathrm{HI}$ produces.

- (1) Benzene
- (2) Ethyl chlorides
- (3) Iodobenzene
- **(4)** Phenol

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: I. anisole →(HI) phenol + iodomethane ($\ce{CH3I}$)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q137_sol_1_93e4a018_30102026.svg?v=93e4a0181b2f)

*I. anisole →[HI] phenol + iodomethane ($\ce{CH3I}$)*

![drawn: I. anisole →(HI) phenol + iodomethane ($\ce{CH3I}$)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q137_solution_1_736ee766_30102026.svg?v=736ee7669373)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In an alkyl aryl ether, the $\mathrm{O-C_{aryl}}$ bond has partial double-bond character from resonance, so it is stronger than the $\mathrm{O-CH_3}$ bond.
2. Protonated anisole is attacked by $\mathrm{I^-}$ at the methyl carbon ($S_N2$), breaking the $\mathrm{O-CH_3}$ bond.
3. $\mathrm{C_6H_5OCH_3 + HI \rightarrow C_6H_5OH + CH_3I}$, so the product is phenol, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Ethers · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 137](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q137_cacda0d9_30102026.png?v=cacda0d95e50)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 137](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q137_5c09b9cc_30102026.png?v=5c09b9cce0c9)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** HI cleaves an aryl alkyl ether at the alkyl carbon, giving phenol and iodomethane; the aryl C-O bond is not broken
- **Trap:** **Wrong site attacked** (`wrong_site_attacked`) — the reagent is right but it attacks the other site, atom or bond than the one the question turns on — option 3  
  Iodide does displace a group from the protonated ether, and a student who lets it attack the ring carbon rather than the methyl carbon writes iodobenzene; the sp$^2$ ring carbon is closed to that attack, so the oxygen stays on the ring as phenol.
- **Why:** This question requires knowing which C-O bond of an aryl alkyl ether HI breaks, demonstrated by the stem's 'phenyl-methyl ethers with HI' set against options that print both possible cleavage products, 'Phenol' and 'Iodobenzene'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q138

Chemistry · organic · Basic Principles and Techniques of Organic Compounds

The most suitable method of separation of $1 : 1$ mixture of ortho and para-nitrophenols is

- **(1)** Steam distillation
- (2) Sublimation
- (3) Chromatography
- (4) Crystallisation

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Steam distillation is the most suitable method of separation of $1 : 1$ mixture of ortho and para nitrophenols as there is intramolecular $\mathrm{H}$-bonds in ortho nitrophenol.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. o-Nitrophenol forms an intramolecular hydrogen bond, so it does not associate and is steam volatile; p-nitrophenol forms intermolecular hydrogen bonds, so it is less volatile and not steam volatile; hence the $1:1$ mixture is separated by steam distillation, option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Methods of Purification of Organic Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 138](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q138_ae6aab35_30102026.png?v=ae6aab356266)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 138](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q138_034c779b_30102026.png?v=034c779b1bef)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Experiment** (`experiment`) — a procedure, its setup, or what its data show
- **Concept:** ortho-nitrophenol is steam-volatile because its intramolecular hydrogen bond leaves no OH free for water, while para-nitrophenol is held by intermolecular hydrogen bonds, so steam distillation separates the pair
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 4  
  Crystallisation is the method the same purification section teaches for solids that differ in solubility, so a student who selects by the physical state of the mixture takes it and never uses the volatility difference the ortho isomer's intramolecular hydrogen bond creates.
- **Why:** This question requires picking the bench technique that exploits the ortho isomer's steam volatility, demonstrated by the stem's 'most suitable method of separation of $1:1$ mixture of ortho and para-nitrophenols' against four named purification methods as options.
- **Note:** The swap test was applied to the trap: replacing 'crystallisation' with an unlike word keeps the pull, because what carries it is that all four options are methods printed together in one purification section, so this is nearby_fact and not look_alike_term.

## Q139

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

Predict the correct intermediate and product in the following reaction $\mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv CH\xrightarrow[HgSO_4]{H_2O,\ H_2SO_4}}\underset{\text{(A)}}{\text{intermediate}}\longrightarrow\underset{\text{(B)}}{\text{product}}$

- **(1)** prop-1-en-2-ol `C=C(C)O`; propan-2-one `CC(C)=O` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle OH}{|}}{C}=CH_2}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle O}{\|}}{C}-CH_3}$
- (2) prop-1-en-2-yl sulfate `C=C(C)OS(=O)(=O)[O-]`; propan-2-one `CC(C)=O` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle SO_4}{|}}{C}=CH_2}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle O}{\|}}{C}-CH_3}$
- (3) prop-1-en-2-ol `C=C(C)O`; prop-1-en-2-yl sulfate `C=C(C)OS(=O)(=O)[O-]` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle OH}{|}}{C}=CH_2}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle SO_4}{|}}{C}=CH_2}$
- (4) propan-2-one `CC(C)=O`; propyne `C#CC` — A : $\mathrm{H_3C-\underset{\underset{\displaystyle O}{\|}}{C}-CH_3}$ B : $\mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv CH}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: I. propyne → 2-hydroxyprop-1-en-1-yl (A); (A) →(Tautomerism) propan-2-one (B)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q139_sol_1_c522516c_30102026.svg?v=c522516c8d26)

*I. propyne → 2-hydroxyprop-1-en-1-yl (A); (A) →[Tautomerism] propan-2-one (B)*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Acid-catalysed hydration of an alkyne in the presence of $\mathrm{Hg^{2+}}$ ($\mathrm{HgSO_4/H_2SO_4}$) adds $\mathrm{H_2O}$ across the triple bond by Markovnikov's rule.
2. For propyne, $\mathrm{OH}$ goes to the more substituted carbon (C-2) and $\mathrm{H}$ to the terminal carbon: $\mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv CH + H_2O \rightarrow H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2}$, an enol, which is intermediate (A).
3. The enol is unstable and undergoes keto-enol tautomerism: $\mathrm{H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2 \rightleftharpoons H_3C-CO-CH_3}$, giving acetone as product (B).
4. A = enol $\mathrm{H_3C-C(OH)=CH_2}$, B = $\mathrm{H_3C-CO-CH_3}$, which matches option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Properties of Alkynes · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 139](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q139_2f4ae457_30102026.png?v=2f4ae4570ffc)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 139](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q139_773f15af_30102026.png?v=773f15af72e3)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Acid and Hg(II) hydration of an alkyne adds water by Markovnikov to give an enol, which tautomerises to the ketone; propyne gives acetone
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  Acetone is the product this hydration is known for, and a student who takes the $\mathrm{H_2SO_4}$ printed over the arrow as a species that bonds to carbon writes a sulfate adduct as the intermediate, so B is right and only A fails.
- **Why:** This question requires naming both the enol intermediate and the carbonyl product of Markovnikov hydration, demonstrated by the stem's 'Predict the correct intermediate and product' and by every option printing an A part and a B part.
- **Note:** E1 recorded reaction_scheme, which has no counterpart in the current qtype list; the printed shape is two blanks answered together, so I recorded two_part.

## Q140

Chemistry · organic · Amines

Which of the following reactions is zappropriate for converting acetamide to methanamine?

- (1) Gabriels phthalimide synthesis
- (2) Carbylamine reaction
- **(3)** Hoffmann hypobromamide reaction
- (4) Stephens reaction

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{CH_3-\overset{\overset{\displaystyle O}{\|}}{C}-NH_2 + Br_2 + 4NaOH \xrightarrow{\Delta} CH_3-NH_2 + 2NaBr + Na_2CO_3 + 3H_2O}$
2. This is Hoffmann Bromamide reaction.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Acetamide is $\mathrm{CH_3CONH_2}$ (2 C) and methanamine is $\mathrm{CH_3NH_2}$ (1 C), so the conversion removes one carbon and keeps the $\mathrm{-NH_2}$ group.
2. Hoffmann bromamide degradation does exactly this: $\mathrm{RCONH_2 + Br_2 + 4NaOH \rightarrow RNH_2 + Na_2CO_3 + 2NaBr + 2H_2O}$.
3. With $\mathrm{R = CH_3}$: $\mathrm{CH_3CONH_2 \rightarrow CH_3NH_2}$.
4. Gabriel synthesis starts from an alkyl halide, the carbylamine reaction turns a primary amine into an isocyanide, and the Stephen reaction reduces a nitrile to an aldehyde, so none of them fits; the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Methods of Preparation of Amines · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 140](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q140_e1563e90_30102026.png?v=e1563e9096b7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 140](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q140_ed5f0e5a_30102026.png?v=ed5f0e5abed9)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Hofmann bromamide degradation turns an amide into a primary amine with one carbon fewer, so acetamide gives methanamine
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 1  
  Gabriel's phthalimide synthesis is printed in the same list of ways to make a pure primary amine, so a student who selects on 'gives a primary amine' takes it without checking that its starting material is an alkyl halide and not an amide.
- **Why:** This question requires the one named reaction that converts an amide to an amine with one carbon fewer, demonstrated by the stem's 'converting acetamide to methanamine' set against four reactions all named in the amines chapter.
- **Note:** The stem as stored reads 'is zappropriate for converting', a stray character in 'appropriate'; it does not change the task. Option 3 is printed 'Hoffmann hypobromamide reaction', this paper's spelling of the Hofmann bromamide (hypobromite) degradation, and is tagged as printed. The swap test was applied: replacing the four names with unlike words keeps the pull, because position in one printed list of amine preparations carries it, so nearby_fact rather than look_alike_term.

## Q141

Chemistry · organic · IUPAC Nomenclature of Organic Chemistry

The IUPAC name of the compound [figure] is ________.

![printed figure: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q141_e39a5c8f_30102026.svg?v=e39a5c8fae43)

*(4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal*

![drawn: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q141_question_cd82a4a5_30102026.svg?v=cd82a4a5da34)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

Structures: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal `C/C=C/C(=O)C(C)C=O` ![(4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q141_question_s1_e7e47b95_30102026.svg?v=e7e47b95c8d7)

- (1) 3-keto-2-methylhex-5-enal
- **(2)** 3-keto-2-methylhex-4-enal
- (3) 5-formylhex-2-en-3-one
- (4) 5-methyl-4-oxohex-2-en-5-al

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal with carbon numbers printed: 1 beside the methyl branch, 2 at the CH, 3 at the ketone C, 4 and 5 at the C=C carbons, 6 at the terminal CH3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q141_sol_1_20126605_30102026.svg?v=20126605a8a9)

*(4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal with carbon numbers printed: 1 beside the methyl branch, 2 at the CH, 3 at the ketone C, 4 and 5 at the C=C carbons, 6 at the terminal CH3*

![drawn: (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal with carbon numbers printed: 1 beside the methyl branch, 2 at the CH, 3 at the ketone C, 4 and 5 at the C=C carbons, 6 at the terminal CH3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q141_solution_1_ca4aa891_30102026.svg?v=ca4aa89139fe)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

1. Aldehydes get higher priority over ketone and alkene in numbering of principal C-chain.
2. $\therefore$ 3-keto-2-methylhex-4-enal

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure the compound is $\mathrm{OHC{-}CH(CH_3){-}CO{-}CH{=}CH{-}CH_3}$.
2. The aldehyde is the principal group, so the $\mathrm{CHO}$ carbon is $C1$ and the longest chain containing it and the $C{=}C$ has 6 carbons (hex).
3. Substituents: $\mathrm{CH_3}$ at $C2$, keto ($=\mathrm{O}$) at $C3$, and the double bond starts at $C4$ ($C4{=}C5$).
4. Name: 2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal, i.e. 3-keto-2-methylhex-4-enal, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Nomenclature of Organic Compounds Having Functional Group(s) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 24)

![printed question 141](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q141_0f2904d4_30102026.png?v=0f2904d4deb3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 141](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q141_cfb38eb5_30102026.png?v=cfb38eb5c0bc)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** In IUPAC naming the aldehyde outranks the ketone, so numbering starts at the CHO and the ketone becomes an oxo (keto) prefix, with the double bond cited by the lower of its two carbons
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  Taking the ketone as the senior group forces the chain to be numbered from the methyl end, which inverts every locant: the double bond is then cited as 2-ene instead of 4-ene and the CHO is demoted to a 5-formyl prefix.
- **Why:** This question requires applying IUPAC group seniority and the lowest-locant rule to the drawn structure, demonstrated by option 3's '5-formylhex-2-en-3-one', which numbers the same chain from the opposite end.
- **Note:** E1 recorded fill_in_blank, which the rulebook treats as an overlay rather than a shape, so qtype is mcq. The figure was supplied: its text and drawn SMILES give (4E)-2-methyl-3-oxohex-4-enal, the modern form of the key's older 'keto' wording in option 2.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q142

Chemistry · inorganic · Environmental Chemistry

Which of the following is a sink for $\mathrm{CO}$?

- (1) Plants
- (2) Haemoglobin
- **(3)** Micro-organisms present in the soil
- (4) Oceans

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Micro-organisms present in the soil is a sink for $\mathrm{CO}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A sink for a pollutant is the part of the environment that removes it from the atmosphere; soil micro-organisms take up and oxidise $\mathrm{CO}$ (to $\mathrm{CO_2}$), so they are the main sink for $\mathrm{CO}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Air Pollution · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

## Q143

Chemistry · organic · Aldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids

Of the following, which is the product formed when cyclohexanone undergoes aldol condensation followed by heating?

- (1) 2-(2-oxocyclohexylidene)cyclohexan-1-one `O=C1CCCCC1=C1CCCCC1=O` ![2-(2-oxocyclohexylidene)cyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q143_option_1_s1_e81bdcf2_30102026.svg?v=e81bdcf2f8b3)
- (2) 2-(2-hydroxycyclohexyl)cyclohexan-1-one `O=C1CCCCC1C1CCCCC1O` ![2-(2-hydroxycyclohexyl)cyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q143_option_2_s1_570b0857_30102026.svg?v=570b08577a73)
- **(3)** 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one `O=C1CCCCC1=C1CCCCC1` ![2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q143_option_3_s1_92674f6a_30102026.svg?v=92674f6aaea1)
- (4) 2-cyclohexylcyclohexan-1-ol `OC1CCCCC1C1CCCCC1` ![2-cyclohexylcyclohexan-1-ol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q143_option_4_s1_947f9300_30102026.svg?v=947f93008fab)

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: I. cyclohexanone + cyclohexanone →((i) OH⁽⁻⁾, (ii) Δ) 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q143_sol_1_194a2f67_30102026.svg?v=194a2f67edc2)

*I. cyclohexanone + cyclohexanone →[(i) OH⁽⁻⁾, (ii) Δ] 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one*

![drawn: I. cyclohexanone + cyclohexanone →((i) OH⁽⁻⁾, (ii) Δ) 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q143_solution_1_09fd5ddc_30102026.svg?v=09fd5ddca71d)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Base removes an $\alpha$-H from one cyclohexanone to form its enolate.
2. The enolate $\alpha$-carbon attacks the $C=O$ carbon of a second cyclohexanone, giving the aldol (a $\beta$-hydroxy ketone): 2-(1-hydroxycyclohexyl)cyclohexan-1-one.
3. On heating, the aldol loses $H_2O$ (the $-OH$ on the former carbonyl carbon and the $\alpha$-H), forming a $C=C$ conjugated with the remaining $C=O$.
4. The product is 2-cyclohexylidenecyclohexan-1-one: one ring keeps its $C=O$, the other ring is joined by an exocyclic $C=C$ and carries no oxygen, which is the structure in option $3$.
5. Option $1$ has two $C=O$ groups, and options $2$ and $4$ have $-OH$ groups with no $C=C$, so none of them is the condensation product.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Reactions: Reactions Due to Alpha Hydrogen · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 143](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q143_bdc48c8b_30102026.png?v=bdc48c8b99d9)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 143](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q143_a866249c_30102026.png?v=a866249c66f6)

---

<!--ecat-->

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Cyclohexanone self-aldol followed by heating loses water to give the alpha,beta-unsaturated ketone 2-(cyclohexylidene)cyclohexanone
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  All four options are stored as empty strings, so there is no option text or structure against which any route could be tested.
- **Why:** This question requires the product of an aldol condensation taken through dehydration, demonstrated by the stem's 'undergoes aldol condensation followed by heating'.
- **Note:** Source incomplete: options_en holds four empty strings, so the four printed structures were not carried into this record. No trap is written. A reviewer holding the printed options should check whether the beta-hydroxy ketone -- the aldol before the water is lost -- is offered as a distractor, since that is what the stem's 'followed by heating' is there to exclude.

## Q144

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

Which of the following pairs of compounds is isoelectronic and isostructural?

- (1) $\mathrm{IF_3}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
- (2) $\mathrm{BeCl_2}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
- (3) $\mathrm{Tel_2}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$, $\mathrm{XeF_2}$
2. Total number of valence electrons are equal in both the species and both the species are linear also.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{XeF_2}$: valence electrons $= 8 + 2\times 7 = 22$; central Xe has $2$ bond pairs and $3$ lone pairs ($sp^3d$), so the shape is linear.
2. $\mathrm{IF_3}$: valence electrons $= 7 + 3\times 7 = 28 \neq 22$; I has $3$ bp $+ 2$ lp, T-shaped, so option $1$ fails.
3. $\mathrm{BeCl_2}$: valence electrons $= 2 + 2\times 7 = 16 \neq 22$; linear but not isoelectronic, so option $2$ fails.
4. $\mathrm{TeI_2}$: valence electrons $= 6 + 2\times 7 = 20 \neq 22$; Te has $2$ bp $+ 2$ lp, bent, so option $3$ fails.
5. $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$: valence electrons $= 7 + 2\times 7 + 1 = 22$; I has $2$ bp $+ 3$ lp ($sp^3d$), linear.
6. $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$ and $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ are both isoelectronic (22 valence $e^-$) and isostructural (linear), which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 144](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q144_df5fd20d_30102026.png?v=df5fd20d376a)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 144](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q144_93aeb303_30102026.png?v=93aeb303ca32)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Isoelectronic and isostructural demands both the same valence-electron count and the same shape; $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ has 22 valence electrons and is linear with three lone pairs, which $\mathrm{IBr_2^-}$ matches
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  Gaseous $\mathrm{BeCl_2}$ is linear, so a student who checks the shape against $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ and stops there satisfies one of the stem's two printed requirements and misses that 16 valence electrons cannot match 22.
- **Why:** This question requires applying two printed criteria at once to each candidate, demonstrated by the stem's 'isoelectronic and isostructural' and by option 2 pairing a linear species that has the wrong electron count.
- **Note:** Option 3 is stored as '$\mathrm{Tel_2}$', a capital I read as a lowercase l; the species is $\mathrm{TeI_2}$, which is unambiguous beside $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ in a VSEPR question, and the typography should be repaired. Each option prints a pair, but $\mathrm{XeF_2}$ is the common member throughout, so the shape on the page is a plain MCQ against a fixed reference and I recorded mcq.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q145

Chemistry · organic · Aldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids

Consider the reactions :
Identify A, X, Y and Z

![printed figure: X ($\ce{C2H6O}$) →(Cu / 573 K) A; A →($\ce{(Ag(NH3)2)+}$, -OH, Δ) Silver mirror observed; A →(-OH, Δ) Y; A + semicarbazide → Z](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q145_87755004_30102026.svg?v=877550044dc6)

*X ($\ce{C2H6O}$) →[Cu / 573 K] A; A →[$\ce{[Ag(NH3)2]+}$, -OH, Δ] Silver mirror observed; A →[-OH, Δ] Y; A + semicarbazide → Z*

Structures: semicarbazide `NNC(N)=O`

- (1) A-Ethanol, X-Acetaldehyde, Y-Butanone, Z-Hydrazone
- (2) A-Methoxymethane, X-Ethanoic acid, Y-Acetate ion, Z-hydrazine
- (3) A-Methoxymethane, X-Ethanol, Y-Ethanoic acid, Z-Semicarbazide
- **(4)** A-Ethanal, X-Ethanol, Y-But-2-enal, Z-Semicarbazone

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Since 'A' gives positive silver mirror test therefore, it must be an aldehyde or $\alpha$-Hydroxyketone.
2. Reaction with semicarbazide indicates that A can be an aldehyde or ketone.
3. Reaction with $\mathrm{OH^{-}}$ i.e., aldol condensation (by assuming alkali to be dilute) indicates that A is aldehyde as aldol reaction of ketones is reversible and carried out in special apparatus.
4. These indicates option (4).

![printed figure: ethanol (X) →(Cu, 573 K) ethanal (A) →($\ce{(Ag(NH3)2)+}$, $\ce{OH-}$, Δ) ethanoic acid; ethanal (A) + semicarbazide → acetaldehyde semicarbazone (Z); ethanal (A) →($\ce{OH-}$) 3-hydroxybutanal →(Δ) but-2-enal (Y)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q145_sol_1_fbf9966c_30102026.svg?v=fbf9966c7ffb)

*ethanol (X) →[Cu, 573 K] ethanal (A) →[$\ce{[Ag(NH3)2]+}$, $\ce{OH-}$, Δ] ethanoic acid; ethanal (A) + semicarbazide → acetaldehyde semicarbazone (Z); ethanal (A) →[$\ce{OH-}$] 3-hydroxybutanal →[Δ] but-2-enal (Y)*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $X$ ($\mathrm{C_2H_6O}$) is converted by $\mathrm{Cu}/573\,\mathrm{K}$ (dehydrogenation) to $A$, which gives a silver mirror, so $A$ is an aldehyde and $X$ is a primary alcohol: $X = \mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH}$ (ethanol).
2. $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{Cu},\,573\,\mathrm{K}} \mathrm{CH_3CHO} + \mathrm{H_2}$, so $A$ = ethanal.
3. $A$ with $\mathrm{OH^-}$ and heat undergoes aldol condensation: $2\,\mathrm{CH_3CHO} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{OH^-},\,\Delta} \mathrm{CH_3CH{=}CHCHO}$, so $Y$ = but-2-enal.
4. $A$ with semicarbazide: $\mathrm{CH_3CHO} + \mathrm{NH_2NHCONH_2} \rightarrow \mathrm{CH_3CH{=}NNHCONH_2}$, so $Z$ = semicarbazone.
5. $A$-Ethanal, $X$-Ethanol, $Y$-But-2-enal, $Z$-Semicarbazone matches option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Reactions: Reactions Due to Alpha Hydrogen, Nucleophilic Addition Reactions · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 25)

![printed question 145](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q145_e73631a8_30102026.png?v=e73631a8d745)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Ethanol dehydrogenated over copper at 573 K gives ethanal, which gives a silver mirror with Tollens' reagent, self-condenses with base and heat to but-2-enal, and condenses with semicarbazide to a semicarbazone

## Q146

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

Which one is the most acidic compound?

- **(1)** 2,4,6-trinitrophenol `O=[N+]([O-])c1cc([N+](=O)[O-])c(O)c([N+](=O)[O-])c1` ![2,4,6-trinitrophenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q146_option_1_s1_73ccaa02_30102026.svg?v=73ccaa02347c)
- (2) 4-methylphenol `Cc1ccc(O)cc1` ![4-methylphenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q146_option_2_s1_cca12045_30102026.svg?v=cca12045aaa5)
- (3) phenol `Oc1ccccc1` ![phenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q146_option_3_s1_4778923b_30102026.svg?v=4778923bbceb)
- (4) 4-nitrophenol `O=[N+]([O-])c1ccc(O)cc1` ![4-nitrophenol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q146_option_4_s1_d3215500_30102026.svg?v=d3215500892f)

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ group has very strong $-\mathrm{I}$ & $-\mathrm{R}$ effects.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Phenols are acidic because the phenoxide ion $\mathrm{C_6H_5O^-}$ is resonance stabilised; groups that stabilise the anion raise acidity.
2. $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ is a strong electron-withdrawing group ($-I$, $-R$) and at ortho/para positions it delocalises the negative charge of the phenoxide ion.
3. $-\mathrm{CH_3}$ is electron-donating ($+I$, hyperconjugation), destabilising the phenoxide ion, so p-cresol is less acidic than phenol.
4. Order: 2,4,6-trinitrophenol $>$ p-nitrophenol $>$ phenol $>$ p-cresol.
5. The most acidic is 2,4,6-trinitrophenol (picric acid), which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Acidity of Phenols · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 146](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q146_218352c2_30102026.png?v=218352c21bf0)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

- **Concept:** Acidity among hydroxy compounds follows how well the conjugate base is stabilised: ring conjugation and electron-withdrawing substituents make the O-H more acidic
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  All four options are stored as empty strings, so the compounds being ranked are not in the record and no route to a wrong structure can be tested.
- **Why:** This question requires ranking four compounds against each other on one property, demonstrated by the stem's 'most acidic compound' with no criterion printed beyond the superlative.

A reviewer holding the printed options should check whether the distractors differ only in the position of a substituent or in its type, since that is what decides whether the pull is the site of the group or its electron-withdrawing power.

## Q147

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Name the gas that can readily decolourises acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ solution:

- (1) $\mathrm{P_2O_5}$
- (2) $\mathrm{CO_2}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{SO_2}$
- (4) $\mathrm{NO_2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{SO_2}$ is readily decolourises acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Decolourising acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ requires a reducing agent that reduces purple $\mathrm{MnO_4^-}$ to nearly colourless $\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}$.
2. $\mathrm{P_2O_5}$ ($\mathrm{P}$ in $+5$) and $\mathrm{CO_2}$ ($\mathrm{C}$ in $+4$) are in their highest oxidation states, so they cannot act as reducing agents.
3. $\mathrm{SO_2}$ ($\mathrm{S}$ in $+4$) is readily oxidised: $5\mathrm{SO_2} + 2\mathrm{MnO_4^-} + 2\mathrm{H_2O} \rightarrow 5\mathrm{SO_4^{2-}} + 4\mathrm{H^+} + 2\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}$.
4. NCERT gives this decolourisation as the characteristic reducing property of $\mathrm{SO_2}$, so the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Allotropes, Oxyacids and Oxides of Sulphur · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 147](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q147_a16ab0c5_30102026.png?v=a16ab0c56fe3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 147](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q147_f4ceea11_30102026.png?v=f4ceea11d277)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Sulphur dioxide is a reducing gas: it reduces $\mathrm{MnO_4^-}$ to $\mathrm{Mn^{2+}}$ as sulphur goes from +4 to +6, discharging the purple colour
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 4  
  $\mathrm{NO_2}$ is the other pungent, coloured, acidic oxide gas taught a few pages away in the same p-block chapter, so a student picking the reactive-looking gas from that set takes it instead of asking which gas can give electrons away.
- **Why:** This question requires knowing which gas is a reducing agent toward permanganate, demonstrated by the stem's 'readily decolourises acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ solution' against four oxides of which only one has an oxidisable central atom.
- **Note:** The swap test was applied: replacing '$\mathrm{NO_2}$' with an unlike formula keeps the pull, because what carries it is that all four options are oxides printed together in the p-block chapter, so nearby_fact rather than look_alike_term.

## Q148

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

Which one is the correct order of acidity?

- (1) $\mathrm{CH_3-CH_3 > CH_2=CH_2 > CH_3-C\equiv CH > CH\equiv CH}$
- (2) $\mathrm{CH_2=CH_2 > CH_3-CH=CH_2 > CH_3-C\equiv CH > CH\equiv CH}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{CH\equiv CH > CH_3-C\equiv CH > CH_2=CH_2 > CH_3-CH_3}$
- (4) $\mathrm{CH\equiv CH > CH_2=CH_2 > CH_3-C\equiv CH > CH_3-CH_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Correct order is $\mathrm{H-C\equiv C-H}$ (Two acidic hydrogens) $> \mathrm{H_3C-C\equiv C-H}$ (One acidic hydrogen) $> \mathrm{H_2C=CH_2} > \mathrm{CH_3-CH_3}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Acidity of a C$-$H bond increases with the $s$-character of the carbon: $sp\,(50\%) > sp^2\,(33\%) > sp^3\,(25\%)$.
2. So both terminal alkynes are more acidic than ethene, and ethene is more acidic than ethane: $\mathrm{alkynes} > \mathrm{CH_2=CH_2} > \mathrm{CH_3-CH_3}$.
3. In $\mathrm{CH_3-C\equiv CH}$ the $\mathrm{CH_3}$ group's $+I$ effect pushes electron density onto the acetylide carbon, destabilising $\mathrm{CH_3-C\equiv C^-}$, so $\mathrm{CH\equiv CH} > \mathrm{CH_3-C\equiv CH}$.
4. Order: $\mathrm{CH\equiv CH > CH_3-C\equiv CH > CH_2=CH_2 > CH_3-CH_3}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Properties of Alkynes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 148](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q148_e4968ba2_30102026.png?v=e4968ba2290a)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 148](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q148_0a0e7958_30102026.png?v=0a0e7958e67e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** The C-H of a terminal alkyne is the most acidic because an sp carbon holds the pair closest to the nucleus; acidity falls sp > sp2 > sp3, and a methyl on the alkyne lowers it slightly
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The taught list runs alkane, alkene, alkyne in order of increasing acidity, and a student who writes that list down left to right without checking which way the printed $>$ signs point produces exactly the reverse of the key.
- **Why:** This question requires ordering four hydrocarbons by the acidity of their C-H bonds, demonstrated by option 1 printing the key's sequence with every $>$ sign pointing the other way.
- **Note:** E1 recorded arrange_in_order, which has no counterpart in the current qtype list; the shape on the page is four orderings offered as options, so qtype is mcq and the answer's form is carried by asks: order.

## Q149

Chemistry · physical · Ionic Equilibrium

Concentration of the $\mathrm{Ag^{+}}$ ions in a saturated solution of $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$ is $2.2 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$. Solubility product of $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$ is

- **(1)** $5.3 \times 10^{-12}$
- (2) $2.42 \times 10^{-8}$
- (3) $2.66 \times 10^{-12}$
- (4) $4.5 \times 10^{-11}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4(s)} \rightleftharpoons \underset{2s}{2\,\mathrm{Ag^{+}(aq)}} + \underset{s}{\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}(aq)}}$
2. $K_{SP} = [\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]^{2}\,[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$
3. $[\mathrm{Ag^{+}}] = 2.2 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{M}$
4. $\therefore\ [\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}] = \frac{2.2 \times 10^{-4}}{2}\,\mathrm{M} = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{M}$
5. $\therefore\ K_{SP} = (2.2 \times 10^{-4})^{2}\,(1.1 \times 10^{-4})$
6. $= 5.324 \times 10^{-12}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Dissociation: $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4 \rightleftharpoons 2Ag^{+} + C_2O_4^{2-}}$.
2. $[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}] = \frac{[\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]}{2} = \frac{2.2 \times 10^{-4}}{2} = 1.1 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$.
3. $K_{sp} = [\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]^2[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$.
4. $[\mathrm{Ag^{+}}]^2 = (2.2 \times 10^{-4})^2 = 4.84 \times 10^{-8}$.
5. $K_{sp} = 4.84 \times 10^{-8} \times 1.1 \times 10^{-4} = 5.324 \times 10^{-12} \approx 5.3 \times 10^{-12}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Solubility Product Constant · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 149](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q149_d31cd6b8_30102026.png?v=d31cd6b8586e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 149](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q149_5e1a302d_30102026.png?v=5e1a302dda2d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** For $\mathrm{Ag_2C_2O_4}$ the solubility product is $[\mathrm{Ag^+}]^2[\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$, with the oxalate at half the silver concentration
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  Carrying the AgCl form of the expression, $K_{sp}=[\mathrm{Ag^+}][\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}]$, gives $2.2\times10^{-4}\times1.1\times10^{-4}=2.42\times10^{-8}$, which is the printed option; the coefficient 2 has to appear as a power, not be left out.
- **Why:** This question requires building the solubility product from a stoichiometry in which the cation coefficient is 2, demonstrated by the stem giving '$\mathrm{Ag^{+}}$ ... is $2.2\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}}$' rather than the solubility of the salt.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q150

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

With respect to the conformers of ethane, which of the following statements is true?

- **(1)** Both bond angles and bond length remains same
- (2) Bond angle remains same but bond length changes
- (3) Bond angle changes but bond length remains same
- (4) Both bond angle and bond length change

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. There is no change in bond angles and bond lengths in the conformations of ethane.
2. There is only change in dihedral angle.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Conformers of ethane (staggered, eclipsed, skew) differ only by rotation about the $\mathrm{C{-}C}$ $\sigma$ bond, which changes the dihedral angle; the $\mathrm{H{-}C{-}H}$ and $\mathrm{H{-}C{-}C}$ bond angles and the $\mathrm{C{-}C}$ and $\mathrm{C{-}H}$ bond lengths stay the same, so option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Isomerism · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 150](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q150_76591cd3_30102026.png?v=76591cd39bd5)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 150](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q150_96eab61d_30102026.png?v=96eab61d82cf)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Conformers of ethane differ only by rotation about the C-C single bond, so no bond is broken or deformed: bond lengths and bond angles stay the same and only the torsion angle changes
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 3  
  Something does change between the staggered and eclipsed forms and it is called an angle -- the torsion angle between front and back hydrogens -- so a student who reads 'bond angle' as that angle gets the bond-length half right and lets it vouch for the other half.
- **Why:** This question requires applying what a conformer is to what it permits to change, demonstrated by the stem's 'With respect to the conformers of ethane' and by options that cross bond angle against bond length.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q151

Chemistry · organic · General Organic Chemistry

The correct statement regarding electrophile is

- **(1)** Electrophile can be either neutral or positively charged species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from a nucleophile
- (2) Electrophile is a negatively charged species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from a nucleophile
- (3) Electrophile is a negatively charged species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from another electrophile
- (4) Electrophiles are generally neutral species and can form a bond by accepting a pair of electrons from a nucleophile

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Fact.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. An electrophile is an electron-seeking species: it forms a bond by accepting an electron pair from a nucleophile (the electron-pair donor).
2. Electrophiles may be positively charged (e.g. $\mathrm{H^+}$, $\mathrm{NO_2^+}$, $\mathrm{R_3C^+}$) or neutral with an electron-deficient centre (e.g. $\mathrm{BF_3}$, $\mathrm{AlCl_3}$, carbonyl carbon of $\mathrm{>C=O}$), so options $2$ and $3$ (negatively charged) are wrong.
3. Option $4$ is too narrow (not 'generally neutral'); option $1$ states neutral or positive and acceptance of a pair from a nucleophile, so the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Electrophile and Nucleophile · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 151](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q151_fd7cc3ce_30102026.png?v=fd7cc3ce9d76)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 151](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q151_565b65b8_30102026.png?v=565b65b852ac)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** An electrophile is electron-deficient -- positively charged or neutral -- and forms a bond by accepting an electron pair from a nucleophile
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 4  
  The second clause is word for word correct, and a student whose examples of electrophiles are the neutral ones -- $\mathrm{BF_3}$, $\mathrm{AlCl_3}$, a carbonyl carbon -- accepts 'generally neutral' as the first clause and never compares it with the option that admits both.
- **Why:** This question requires the definition of an electrophile as electron-deficient rather than negatively charged, demonstrated by option 1's 'either neutral or positively charged species' against option 4's narrower 'generally neutral species'.
