## Q152

Chemistry · physical · Structure of Atom

Which one is the wrong statement?

- **(1)** The energy of $2s$ orbital is less than the energy of $2p$ orbital in case of Hydrogen like atoms
- (2) de-Broglie's wavelength is given by $\lambda = \frac{h}{mv}$, where $m$ = mass of the particle, $v$ = group velocity of the particle
- (3) The uncertainty principle is $\Delta E \times \Delta t \geq \frac{h}{4\pi}$
- (4) Half-filled and fully filled orbitals have greater stability due to greater exchange energy, greater symmetry and more balanced arrangement

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Energy of $2s$-orbital and $2p$-orbital in case of hydrogen like atoms is equal.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For hydrogen-like species $E_n = -\frac{13.6\,Z^2}{n^2}\,\text{eV}$ depends only on $n$, so $E_{2s} = E_{2p}$; statement 1 (that $E_{2s} < E_{2p}$) is wrong.
2. de Broglie relation $\lambda = \frac{h}{mv}$ uses the particle velocity, which equals the group velocity of the matter wave; statement 2 is correct.
3. Energy-time uncertainty $\Delta E \times \Delta t \geq \frac{h}{4\pi}$ is a valid form of the uncertainty principle; statement 3 is correct.
4. Half-filled and fully filled subshells are extra stable owing to symmetry and greater exchange energy; statement 4 is correct.
5. The only wrong statement is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Atomic Orbitals: Shapes, Energies and Nodes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 26)

![printed question 152](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q152_c2dcadb7_30102026.png?v=c2dcadb758b7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 152](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q152_a38fa8e8_30102026.png?v=a38fa8e808df)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** In a one-electron, hydrogen-like atom the orbital energy depends only on n, so 2s and 2p are degenerate; the splitting of s below p needs more than one electron
- **Trap:** **New trap (proposed)** (`new_trap`) — a way of catching a student that none of the above describes — option 3  
  The uncertainty principle is carried as $\Delta x\cdot\Delta p \ge h/4\pi$, and a student hunting for the planted error flags the statement whose symbols are not the pair they memorised, even though the energy-time form is the same principle.
- **Proposed:** `true_but_unfamiliar_wording` — Under a 'which one is the wrong statement' stem this option is TRUE, and what pulls is that the relation is printed in a form the textbook does not use, so the unfamiliar notation reads as the planted error. I ruled out answers_other_question, which is true of every wrong option on a negated stem and so names no mechanism, and formula_misused, which needs a relation applied wrongly -- here the relation is stated correctly and only its pair of variables is unfamiliar.
- **Why:** This question requires knowing that orbital energy in a one-electron species depends on n alone, demonstrated by option 1's qualification 'in case of Hydrogen like atoms' attached to a 2s-below-2p claim.
- **Note:** The task is negated ('Which one is the wrong statement?'), so every option other than the key is true and the pull has to be why a true statement looks false. Option 3 was recorded as new_trap with a proposal rather than forced into answers_other_question.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q153

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

Correct increasing order for the wavelengths of absorption in the visible region for the complexes of $\mathrm{Co^{3+}}$ is

- (1) $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$
- (3) $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. The order of the ligand in the spectrochemical series
2. $\mathrm{H_2O < NH_3 < en}$
3. Hence, the wavelength of the light observed will be in the order
4. $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+} < [Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+} < [Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$
5. Thus, wavelength absorbed will be in the opposite order
6. i.e., $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}}$, $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Spectrochemical series for these ligands: $\mathrm{H_2O} < \mathrm{NH_3} < \mathrm{en}$, so $\Delta_o$: $\mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}} < \mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}} < \mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}}$.
2. Absorbed energy $\Delta_o = \frac{hc}{\lambda}$, so $\lambda \propto \frac{1}{\Delta_o}$.
3. Increasing wavelength: $\mathrm{[Co(en)_3]^{3+}} < \mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]^{3+}} < \mathrm{[Co(H_2O)_6]^{3+}}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Colour in Coordination Compounds, Crystal Field Theory and Its Limitations · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 153](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q153_1ed09dfa_30102026.png?v=1ed09dfa4601)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

- **Concept:** Wavelength absorbed runs inversely to the crystal field splitting, so the field-strength order $\mathrm{H_2O} < \mathrm{NH_3} < \mathrm{en}$ gives the reverse order of absorption wavelengths

## Q154

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Match the interhalogen compounds of column I with the geometry in column II and assign the correct code
Code :
(a) (b) (c) (d)

| List-I | | List-II | |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) | $\mathrm{XX'}$ | (i) | T-shape |
| (b) | $\mathrm{XX'_3}$ | (ii) | Pentagonal bipyramidal |
| (c) | $\mathrm{XX'_5}$ | (iii) | Linear |
| (d) | $\mathrm{XX'_7}$ | (iv) | Square-pyramidal |
|  |  | (v) | Tetrahedral |

- (1) (iv) (iii) (ii) (i)
- (2) (iii) (iv) (i) (ii)
- **(3)** (iii) (i) (iv) (ii)
- (4) (v) (iv) (iii) (ii)

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{XX'} \rightarrow$ Linear
2. $\mathrm{XX'_3} \rightarrow$ Example : $\mathrm{CIF_3} \rightarrow$ T-shape
3. $\mathrm{XX'_5} \rightarrow$ Example : $\mathrm{BrF_5} \rightarrow$ Square pyramidal
4. $\mathrm{XX'_7} \rightarrow$ Example : $\mathrm{IF_7} \rightarrow$ Pentagonal bipyramidal

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{XX'}$ is a diatomic molecule, so its geometry is linear: (a) $\rightarrow$ (iii).
2. $\mathrm{XX'_3}$ has 3 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs on X ($sp^3d$), giving a T-shape: (b) $\rightarrow$ (i).
3. $\mathrm{XX'_5}$ has 5 bond pairs and 1 lone pair on X ($sp^3d^2$), giving a square pyramid: (c) $\rightarrow$ (iv).
4. $\mathrm{XX'_7}$ has 7 bond pairs and no lone pair on X ($sp^3d^3$), giving a pentagonal bipyramid: (d) $\rightarrow$ (ii).
5. The code (iii) (i) (iv) (ii) is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Interhalogen Compounds & Important Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 154](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q154_94310724_30102026.png?v=943107248708)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 154](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q154_a05de54d_30102026.png?v=a05de54d2343)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Match the columns** (`match_columns`) — two labelled lists to pair, the answer is a pairing code
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** VSEPR on an interhalogen: count bond pairs plus lone pairs to get the parent polyhedron, then take out the lone-pair corners, giving linear, T-shaped, square pyramidal and pentagonal bipyramidal for $XX'$, $XX'_3$, $XX'_5$ and $XX'_7$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  Counting the electron pairs correctly and then naming the shape that has that many corners gives five pairs for $XX'_3$ and reads off square-pyramidal, leaving T-shape for $XX'_5$; the lone pairs still take corners, and the shape is what remains once they are removed.
- **Why:** This question requires a known rule applied row by row to a new case, demonstrated by column I holding only the generic formulae $XX'$, $XX'_3$, $XX'_5$ and $XX'_7$, which name no element to recall and yield a shape only once bond pairs and lone pairs are counted.
- **Note:** Column II prints five shapes for four rows and the spare one, tetrahedral, is used only in option 4. Options 1 and 4 both fail on row (a) by atom count, so the question discriminates between options 2 and 3 only, and on the two middle rows alone.

## Q155

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

The species, having bond angles of $120^\circ$ is

- **(1)** $\mathrm{BCl_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{PH_3}$
- (3) $\mathrm{ClF_3}$
- (4) $\mathrm{NCl_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: boron trichloride ($\ce{BCl3}$), trigonal planar, with the Cl–B–Cl bond angle marked 120°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q155_sol_1_4a9ee9c6_30102026.svg?v=4a9ee9c6a22f)

*boron trichloride ($\ce{BCl3}$), trigonal planar, with the Cl–B–Cl bond angle marked 120°*

![drawn: boron trichloride ($\ce{BCl3}$), trigonal planar, with the Cl–B–Cl bond angle marked 120°](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q155_solution_1_3de1e9f6_30102026.svg?v=3de1e9f6a25a)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{BCl_3}$: B has $3$ bond pairs and $0$ lone pairs, $sp^2$ hybridised, trigonal planar, so $\angle \mathrm{Cl\!-\!B\!-\!Cl} = 120^\circ$.
2. $\mathrm{PH_3}$: P has $3$ bond pairs and $1$ lone pair, pyramidal, $\angle \mathrm{H\!-\!P\!-\!H} \approx 93.5^\circ$.
3. $\mathrm{ClF_3}$: Cl has $3$ bond pairs and $2$ lone pairs ($sp^3d$), T-shaped, $\angle \mathrm{F\!-\!Cl\!-\!F} \approx 87.5^\circ$.
4. $\mathrm{NCl_3}$: N has $3$ bond pairs and $1$ lone pair ($sp^3$), pyramidal, angle about $107^\circ$, below $120^\circ$.
5. Only $\mathrm{BCl_3}$ has $120^\circ$ bond angles, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 155](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q155_e6e40185_30102026.png?v=e6e40185e1d8)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 155](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q155_f657286a_30102026.png?v=f657286a62e1)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** VSEPR counts bond pairs plus lone pairs: three bond pairs and no lone pair give a trigonal planar species with $120^\circ$ angles, and each lone pair added squeezes the angle below that.
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  Nitrogen is carried as trivalent, so its three bonds are counted as the whole electron count, $\mathrm{AX_3}$ is read as trigonal planar and $\mathrm{NCl_3}$ is awarded $120^\circ$; the fourth pair is a lone pair, which makes the ion pyramidal at about $107^\circ$.
- **Why:** This question requires VSEPR's electron-domain count applied to each named species, demonstrated by the stem asking for "The species, having bond angles of $120^\circ$" while all four options are $\mathrm{AX_3}$ formulas that differ only in their lone pairs.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q156

Chemistry · organic · Amines

The correct increasing order of basic strength for the following compounds is

![printed figure: (I) aniline; (II) 4-nitroaniline; (III) 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q156_f7e03e9d_30102026.svg?v=f7e03e9d1276)

*(I) aniline; (II) 4-nitroaniline; (III) 4-methylaniline*

![drawn: (I) aniline; (II) 4-nitroaniline; (III) 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q156_question_09891d62_30102026.svg?v=09891d62dd44)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

Structures: aniline `Nc1ccccc1` ![aniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q156_question_s1_1e3ada3e_30102026.svg?v=1e3ada3e29d0); 4-nitroaniline `Nc1ccc([N+](=O)[O-])cc1` ![4-nitroaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q156_question_s2_2b4e4196_30102026.svg?v=2b4e41963ced); 4-methylaniline `Cc1ccc(N)cc1` ![4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q156_question_s3_5f1a0db7_30102026.svg?v=5f1a0db74b01)

- **(1)** $\mathrm{II} < \mathrm{I} < \mathrm{III}$
- (2) $\mathrm{II} < \mathrm{III} < \mathrm{I}$
- (3) $\mathrm{III} < \mathrm{I} < \mathrm{II}$
- (4) $\mathrm{III} < \mathrm{II} < \mathrm{I}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ has strong $-R$ effect and $-\mathrm{CH_3}$ shows $+R$ effect.
2. $\therefore$ Order of basic strength is

![printed figure: 4-nitroaniline < aniline < 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q156_sol_1_a0ed2a81_30102026.svg?v=a0ed2a817b70)

*4-nitroaniline < aniline < 4-methylaniline*

![drawn: 4-nitroaniline < aniline < 4-methylaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q156_solution_1_02f4ac2f_30102026.svg?v=02f4ac2f214f)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. From the figure, $\mathrm{I}$ is aniline ($\mathrm{C_6H_5NH_2}$), $\mathrm{II}$ is $p$-nitroaniline and $\mathrm{III}$ is $p$-toluidine ($p$-$\mathrm{CH_3C_6H_4NH_2}$).
2. Basic strength depends on how available the lone pair on $\mathrm{N}$ of $-\mathrm{NH_2}$ is.
3. The $p$-$\mathrm{NO_2}$ group is electron-withdrawing ($-I$, $-R$), so it pulls the lone pair into the ring and makes $\mathrm{II}$ less basic than aniline $\mathrm{I}$.
4. The $p$-$\mathrm{CH_3}$ group is electron-donating ($+I$, hyperconjugation), so it increases electron density on $\mathrm{N}$ and makes $\mathrm{III}$ more basic than aniline $\mathrm{I}$.
5. Hence the increasing order is $\mathrm{II} < \mathrm{I} < \mathrm{III}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Reactions of Amines (Basicity, Alkylation and Acylation) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 156](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q156_3d161a8b_30102026.png?v=3d161a8b6c82)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 156](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q156_bd4fcbe7_30102026.png?v=bd4fcbe71bc8)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** A para substituent tunes aniline's basicity through the nitrogen lone pair: a nitro group pulls it into the ring and weakens the base, a methyl group pushes electrons in and strengthens it.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  The three are ranked correctly as $\mathrm{III > I > II}$ and that line is then copied out while the stem asked for increasing strength, which lands exactly on $\mathrm{III < I < II}$.
- **Why:** This question requires three anilines placed in a sequence by substituent effect, demonstrated by the stem asking for "the correct increasing order of basic strength" with every option being an ordering of I, II and III.
- **Note:** E1's `arrange_in_order` maps to qtype `mcq`. The three structures were supplied as drawings with $\mathrm{NH_2}$ on each ring and $\mathrm{NO_2}$ and $\mathrm{CH_3}$ para, so the substituents the ranking needs are present.

## Q157

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

> ⚠ The booklet lost this question's number and first line, and no other booklet code of this exam prints it; the stem starts mid-sentence.

Which one of the following statements is not correct?

- (1) Coenzymes increase the catalytic activity of enzyme
- (2) Catalyst does not initiate any reaction
- **(3)** The value of equilibrium constant is changed in the presence of a catalyst in the reaction at equilibrium
- (4) Enzymes catalyse mainly bio-chemical reactions

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. A catalyst decreases activation energies of both the forward and backward reaction by same amount, therefore, it speeds up both forward and backward reaction by same rate.
2. Equilibrium constant is therefore not affected by catalyst at a given temperature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A catalyst lowers the activation energy of the forward and backward reactions by the same amount, so both rates rise by the same factor; $K = \frac{k_f}{k_b}$ stays unchanged, so statement $3$ is not correct, while statements $1$, $2$ and $4$ are correct as NCERT teaches them.

**Subtopic:** Effect of a Catalyst on Equilibrium · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 27)

![printed question 157](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q157_899ff71b_30102026.png?v=899ff71b8b50)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 157](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q157_c4aa649f_30102026.png?v=c4aa649f4cb4)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A catalyst lowers the activation barrier of the forward and the reverse reaction by the same amount, so it changes how fast equilibrium is reached and not the equilibrium constant.
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  The stem asks which statement is NOT correct and option 2 carries its own "does not", so the sentence that reads as a denial is ticked as the denial the question is after — but "Catalyst does not initiate any reaction" is a true line.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated fact that an equilibrium constant is untouched by a catalyst, demonstrated by option 3 reading "The value of equilibrium constant is changed in the presence of a catalyst in the reaction at equilibrium".

## Q158

Chemistry · physical · Thermodynamics

A gas is allowed to expand in a well insulated container against a constant external pressure of $2.5\,\mathrm{atm}$ from an initial volume of $2.50\,\mathrm{L}$ to a final volume of $4.50\,\mathrm{L}$. The change in internal energy $\Delta U$ of the gas in joules will be

- (1) $+505\,\mathrm{J}$
- (2) $1136.25\,\mathrm{J}$
- (3) $-500\,\mathrm{J}$
- **(4)** $-505\,\mathrm{J}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\Delta U = q + w$
2. For adiabatic process, $q = 0$
3. $\therefore\ \Delta U = w$
4. $= -P\cdot\Delta V$
5. $= -2.5\,\mathrm{atm} \times (4.5 - 2.5)\,\mathrm{L}$
6. $= -2.5 \times 2\,\mathrm{L\text{-}atm}$
7. $= -5 \times 101.3\,\mathrm{J}$
8. $= -506.5\,\mathrm{J}$
9. $\approx -505\,\mathrm{J}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The container is well insulated, so the process is adiabatic: $q = 0$.
2. First law: $\Delta U = q + w = w$.
3. $\Delta V = 4.50\,\mathrm{L} - 2.50\,\mathrm{L} = 2.00\,\mathrm{L}$.
4. Irreversible expansion against constant external pressure: $w = -P_{ext}\Delta V = -(2.5\,\mathrm{atm})(2.00\,\mathrm{L}) = -5.0\,\mathrm{L\,atm}$.
5. Convert: $-5.0\,\mathrm{L\,atm} \times 101\,\mathrm{J\,L^{-1}\,atm^{-1}} = -505\,\mathrm{J}$ (using $101.3$ gives $-506.5\,\mathrm{J}$, nearest option $-505\,\mathrm{J}$).
6. $\Delta U = -505\,\mathrm{J}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Internal Energy and First Law of Thermodynamics · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 158](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q158_ce965965_30102026.png?v=ce9659659aa1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 158](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q158_04b94f86_30102026.png?v=04b94f869a67)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In an insulated vessel $q=0$, so $\Delta U = w$, and expansion against a constant external pressure makes $w = -P_{ext}\Delta V$, a negative number.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  $P_{ext}\Delta V = 2.5 \times 2.00 = 5.0\,\mathrm{L\,atm} = 505\,\mathrm{J}$ is computed correctly and then recorded as a gain, when the gas expanded and so lost that energy.
- **Why:** This question requires the first law applied to an insulated expansion and a number produced from the stem's data, demonstrated by "a constant external pressure of $2.5\,\mathrm{atm}$" with volumes of $2.50\,\mathrm{L}$ and $4.50\,\mathrm{L}$ and the request for "$\Delta U$ of the gas in joules".
- **Note:** The key's $-505\,\mathrm{J}$ follows from $1\,\mathrm{L\,atm} \approx 101\,\mathrm{J}$; the fuller conversion gives $-506.5\,\mathrm{J}$. Tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q159

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

A $20\,\text{litre}$ container at $400\,\mathrm{K}$ contains $\mathrm{CO_2(g)}$ at pressure $0.4\,\mathrm{atm}$ and an excess of $\mathrm{SrO}$ (neglect the volume of solid $\mathrm{SrO}$). The volume of the containers is now decreased by moving the movable piston fitted in the container. The maximum volume of the container, when pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ attains its maximum value, will be (Given that : $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2(g)}$. $K_p = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$)

- (1) $2\,\text{litre}$
- **(2)** $5\,\text{litre}$
- (3) $10\,\text{litre}$
- (4) $4\,\text{litre}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Max. pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ = Pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ at equilibrium
2. For reaction,
3. $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2}$
4. $K_p = P_{\mathrm{CO_2}} = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$ = maximum pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$
5. Volume of container at this stage,
6. $V = \frac{nRT}{P}$ …(i)
7. Since container is sealed and reaction was not earlier at equilibrium
8. $\therefore\ n = \text{constant}$
9. $n = \frac{PV}{RT} = \frac{0.4 \times 20}{RT}$ …(ii)
10. Put equation (ii) in equation (i)
11. $V = \left[\frac{0.4 \times 20}{RT}\right]\frac{RT}{1.6} = 5\,\mathrm{L}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2(g)}$, only the gas appears in the expression: $K_p = p_{\mathrm{CO_2}} = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$, so the maximum possible $p_{\mathrm{CO_2}}$ is $1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$.
2. Initially $p_{\mathrm{CO_2}} = 0.4\,\mathrm{atm} < K_p$, so on compression the CO$_2$ behaves as a fixed amount of gas at constant $T$ until its pressure reaches $1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$ (further compression only converts CO$_2$ to $\mathrm{SrCO_3}$).
3. Boyle's law: $p_1 V_1 = p_2 V_2 \Rightarrow 0.4 \times 20 = 1.6 \times V_2$.
4. $V_2 = \frac{0.4 \times 20}{1.6} = 5\,\text{litre}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Homogeneous and Heterogeneous Equilibria · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 159](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q159_0299617d_30102026.png?v=0299617db7ea)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 159](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q159_f00c7193_30102026.png?v=f00c71939041)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** For $\mathrm{SrCO_3(s) \rightleftharpoons SrO(s) + CO_2(g)}$ the solids drop out, so $K_p$ IS the highest pressure the $\mathrm{CO_2}$ can reach; squeeze further and the gas is taken back into carbonate, and Boyle's law fixes the volume at which that ceiling is met.
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 4  
  The pressure has to rise by a factor of $1.6/0.4 = 4$, and that 4 is written down as the answer in litres instead of being divided into the $20\,\mathrm{L}$ the vessel started at.
- **Why:** This question requires $K_p$ read as the ceiling pressure of the one gaseous species and Boyle's law then applied to it, demonstrated by the stem asking for the volume "when pressure of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ attains its maximum value" with "$K_p = 1.6\,\mathrm{atm}$" supplied.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q160

Chemistry · organic · Biomolecules

Which of the following statements is not correct?

- **(1)** Denaturation makes the proteins more active
- (2) Insulin maintains sugar level in the blood of a human body
- (3) Ovalbumin is a simple food reserve in egg-white
- (4) Blood proteins thrombin and fibrinogen are involved in blood clotting

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Due to denaturation of proteins, globules unfold and helix get uncoiled and protein loses its biological activity.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Denaturation disrupts the $2^\circ$ and $3^\circ$ structure of a protein, so the protein loses its biological activity. Statement $1$ is therefore not correct, while statements $2$ (insulin regulates blood sugar), $3$ (ovalbumin is a simple protein food reserve in egg-white) and $4$ (thrombin and fibrinogen act in clotting) are correct, so the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Denaturation of Proteins and Enzymes · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 160](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q160_1103399a_30102026.png?v=1103399a46a2)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 160](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q160_decde39e_30102026.png?v=decde39eefcc)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Denaturation unfolds a protein's secondary and tertiary structure, and the biological activity goes with the shape — a denatured enzyme no longer works.
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 3  
  "Simple food reserve" is carbohydrate language — starch in plants, glycogen in animals — so the egg-white line reads as the misplaced one and is ticked under the stem's "not correct", when it is a statement the chapter prints.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated fact about what denaturation does to a protein, demonstrated by option 1 reading "Denaturation makes the proteins more active" under a stem asking which statement "is not correct".
<!--/ecat-->

## Q161

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Kinetics

Mechanism of a hypothetical reaction $\mathrm{X_2 + Y_2 \rightarrow 2XY}$ is given below :
(i) $\mathrm{X_2 \rightarrow X + X}$ (fast)
(ii) $\mathrm{X + Y_2 \rightleftharpoons XY + Y}$ (slow)
(iii) $\mathrm{X + Y \rightarrow XY}$ (fast)
The overall order of the reaction will be

- **(1)** 1.5
- (2) 1
- (3) 2
- (4) 0

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. The solution of this question is given by assuming step (i) to be reversible which is not given in question
2. Overall rate = Rate of slowest step (ii)
3. $= k[\mathrm{X}]\,[\mathrm{Y_2}]$ ...(1)
4. $k$ = rate constant of step (ii)
5. Assuming step (i) to be reversible, its equilibrium constant,
6. $k_{eq} = \frac{[\mathrm{X}]^{2}}{[\mathrm{X_2}]} \Rightarrow [\mathrm{X}] = k_{eq}^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}$ ...(2)
7. Put (2) in (1)
8. Rate $= kk_{eq}^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathrm{Y_2}]$
9. Overall order $= \frac{1}{2} + 1 = \frac{3}{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The slow step (ii) is rate-determining, so $\text{rate} = k_2[\mathrm{X}][\mathrm{Y_2}]$.
2. $\mathrm{X}$ is an intermediate; the fast step (i) acts as a pre-equilibrium $\mathrm{X_2 \rightleftharpoons 2X}$ with $K = \frac{[\mathrm{X}]^2}{[\mathrm{X_2}]}$.
3. Hence $[\mathrm{X}] = K^{1/2}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}$.
4. Substituting, $\text{rate} = k_2 K^{1/2}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{Y_2}]$.
5. Overall order $= \frac{1}{2} + 1 = 1.5$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Order of a Reaction · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 28)

![printed question 161](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q161_33506714_30102026.png?v=3350671427af)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 161](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q161_66ca4259_30102026.png?v=66ca42592466)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** The slow step sets the rate law, and an intermediate standing in it has to be replaced through the fast equilibrium that supplies it — a dissociation into two atoms brings in a square root, and so a half order.
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  The order is read off the molecularity: the slow step $\mathrm{X + Y_2}$ has two reactant particles, and the overall change $\mathrm{X_2 + Y_2 \rightarrow 2XY}$ has two reactant molecules, so either reading gives 2 — but $[\mathrm{X}]$ is an intermediate and must be replaced by $K^{1/2}[\mathrm{X_2}]^{1/2}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the rate law built from the step marked slow with its intermediate eliminated, demonstrated by "(ii) $\mathrm{X + Y_2 \rightleftharpoons XY + Y}$ (slow)" standing under the fast dissociation "(i) $\mathrm{X_2 \rightarrow X + X}$".
- **Note:** E1's `reaction_scheme` maps to qtype `mcq`; the scheme is in the stem and the options are bare numbers. `asks` is `apply_rule` rather than `calculate` because the stem supplies no numbers to put in — the answer comes from the rate-determining-step rule.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q162

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

In which pair of ions both the species contain $\mathrm{S-S}$ bond?

- (1) $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_8^{2-}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: I. tetrathionate ion, labelled $\ce{(S4O6)^{2-}}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q162_sol_1_90744325_30102026.svg?v=907443256d71)

*I. tetrathionate ion, labelled $\ce{[S4O6]^{2-}}$*

![drawn: I. tetrathionate ion, labelled $\ce{(S4O6)^{2-}}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q162_solution_1_0dd5f782_30102026.svg?v=0dd5f7824f26)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

![printed figure: thiosulfate ion, labelled $\ce{(S2O3)^{2-}}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q162_sol_2_293d7955_30102026.svg?v=293d79556574)

*thiosulfate ion, labelled $\ce{[S2O3]^{2-}}$*

![drawn: thiosulfate ion, labelled $\ce{(S2O3)^{2-}}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q162_solution_2_518a4cd2_30102026.svg?v=518a4cd2317e)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$ (tetrathionate) is $\mathrm{^-O_3S{-}S{-}S{-}SO_3^-}$, so it contains $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bonds.
2. $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$ (thiosulfate) has one S bonded directly to the central S, so it contains an $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond.
3. $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$ (pyrosulfate) is $\mathrm{^-O_3S{-}O{-}SO_3^-}$, linked by an $\mathrm{S{-}O{-}S}$ bridge, so it has no $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond.
4. $\mathrm{S_2O_8^{2-}}$ (peroxodisulfate) is $\mathrm{^-O_3S{-}O{-}O{-}SO_3^-}$, linked by a peroxo $\mathrm{O{-}O}$ bridge, so it has no $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond.
5. Only the pair $\mathrm{S_4O_6^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{S_2O_3^{2-}}$ has an $\mathrm{S{-}S}$ bond in both ions, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Allotropes, Oxyacids and Oxides of Sulphur · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 162](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q162_ebb2feac_30102026.png?v=ebb2feac24a1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 162](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q162_42146188_30102026.png?v=42146188012d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Sulphur's oxoanions divide by what joins the two sulphurs: thiosulphate and tetrathionate hold a direct S–S link, pyrosulphate is bridged through an oxygen, and peroxodisulphate through a peroxide O–O.
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  Thiosulphate's S–S bond is secure, and $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$ is then taken to have one as well because its formula puts two sulphurs together — the bridge there is an oxygen, S–O–S.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated structures of sulphur's oxoanions, demonstrated by the stem asking "In which pair of ions both the species contain $\mathrm{S-S}$ bond?" with $\mathrm{S_2O_7^{2-}}$, the oxygen-bridged ion, planted in three of the four options.
- **Note:** The device here is set-level: the same pyrosulphate misreading sits in options 1, 2 and 4. The trap is placed on option 2 because its other half is the structure the chapter actually draws.

## Q163

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

Which one of the following pairs of species have the same bond order?

- (1) $\mathrm{N_2}$, $\mathrm{O_2^-}$
- (2) $\mathrm{CO}$, $\mathrm{NO}$
- (3) $\mathrm{O_2}$, $\mathrm{NO^+}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{CN^-}$, $\mathrm{CO}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{CN^{(-)}}$ and $\mathrm{CO}$ have bond order $3$ each.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Bond order from MO theory: $\text{B.O.} = \frac{N_b - N_a}{2}$.
2. $\mathrm{N_2}$ (14 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-4}{2} = 3$; $\mathrm{O_2^-}$ (17 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-7}{2} = 1.5$. Not equal.
3. $\mathrm{CO}$ (14 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = 3$; $\mathrm{NO}$ (15 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-5}{2} = 2.5$. Not equal.
4. $\mathrm{O_2}$ (16 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-6}{2} = 2$; $\mathrm{NO^+}$ (14 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = 3$. Not equal.
5. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ (14 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-4}{2} = 3$; $\mathrm{CO}$ (14 e$^-$): $\text{B.O.} = 3$. Equal, so option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Molecular Orbital Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 163](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q163_984349bd_30102026.png?v=984349bd0da5)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 163](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q163_9fe7bada_30102026.png?v=9fe7bada884d)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Bond order is $(N_b - N_a)/2$ from the molecular orbital filling, so isoelectronic species share it: $\mathrm{CN^-}$ and $\mathrm{CO}$ both hold 14 electrons and a bond order of 3.
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 2  
  The 14-electron family carried in memory is $\mathrm{N_2}$, $\mathrm{CO}$, $\mathrm{CN^-}$ and $\mathrm{NO^+}$; the option prints NO without the charge, one electron richer, and that extra electron in $\pi^*$ drops its bond order to $2.5$ against CO's 3.
- **Why:** This question requires the molecular-orbital bond-order rule run over each printed species, demonstrated by the stem asking "Which one of the following pairs of species have the same bond order?" with pairs that differ by single electrons and single charges. The swap test decides the trap axis: replace NO with an unlike formula and the pull dies, so the likeness to $\mathrm{NO^+}$ is what carries the student, and the label is `look_alike_term` rather than `nearby_fact`.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q165

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

It is because of inability of $ns^{2}$ electrons of the valence shell to participate in bonding that

- (1) $\mathrm{Sn^{4+}}$ is reducing while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is oxidising
- **(2)** $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ is reducing while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is oxidising
- (3) $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ is oxidising while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is reducing
- (4) $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ and $\mathrm{Pb^{2+}}$ are both oxidising and reducing

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Inability of $ns^{2}$ electrons of the valence shell to participate in bonding on moving down the group in heavier p-block elements is called inert pair effect
2. As a result, $\mathrm{Pb(II)}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Pb(IV)}$
3. $\mathrm{Sn(IV)}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Sn(II)}$
4. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Pb(IV)}$ is easily reduced to $\mathrm{Pb(II)}$
5. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Pb(IV)}$ is oxidising agent
6. $\mathrm{Sn(II)}$ is easily oxidised to $\mathrm{Sn(IV)}$
7. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Sn(II)}$ is reducing agent

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The reluctance of $ns^{2}$ electrons to bond is the inert pair effect, which increases down group 14.
2. For Pb the inert pair effect is strong, so $\mathrm{Pb^{2+}}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$; $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ gains electrons readily and is oxidising.
3. For Sn the effect is weaker, so $\mathrm{Sn^{4+}}$ is more stable than $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$; $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ loses electrons readily and is reducing.
4. So $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ is reducing while $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ is oxidising, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** General Properties & Trends of Group 14 (Carbon Family) · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q165_0e7bd046_30102026.png?v=0e7bd046ffbd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q165_c2c155d0_30102026.png?v=c2c155d059ca)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** The inert pair effect: the $ns^2$ pair is held more tightly down group 14, so lead is stable as Pb(II) and tin as Sn(IV) — which makes $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ an oxidising agent and $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ a reducing one.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  The inert pair effect is stored as a phrase attached to "tin and lead" without which way it runs; giving the stable $ns^2$ pair to tin instead of lead swaps both halves and lands exactly on $\mathrm{Sn^{2+}}$ oxidising, $\mathrm{Pb^{4+}}$ reducing.
- **Why:** This question requires the consequence that follows from a reason printed in the stem, demonstrated by the stem reading "It is because of inability of $ns^{2}$ electrons of the valence shell to participate in bonding that" with the options supplying rival outcomes.
- **Note:** E1's `statement_options` maps to qtype `statements`: no lettered block is printed and each option is a full claim judged whole.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q166

Chemistry · physical · Thermodynamics

For a given reaction, $\Delta H = 35.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and $\Delta S = 83.6\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$. The reaction is spontaneous at : (Assume that $\Delta H$ and $\Delta S$ do not vary with temperature)

- (1) $T > 298\,\mathrm{K}$
- (2) $T < 425\,\mathrm{K}$
- **(3)** $T > 425\,\mathrm{K}$
- (4) All temperatures

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\because\ \Delta G = \Delta H - T\Delta S$
2. For a reaction to be spontaneous, $\Delta G = -\mathrm{ve}$
3. i.e., $\Delta H < T\Delta S$
4. $\therefore\ T > \frac{\Delta H}{\Delta S} = \frac{35.5 \times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{J}}{83.6\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}}}$
5. i.e., $T > 425\,\mathrm{K}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Spontaneity requires $\Delta G = \Delta H - T\Delta S < 0$.
2. Convert units: $\Delta H = 35.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}} = 35500\,\mathrm{J\,mol^{-1}}$.
3. Since $\Delta H > 0$ and $\Delta S > 0$, $\Delta G < 0$ when $T > \frac{\Delta H}{\Delta S}$.
4. $T = \frac{35500}{83.6} \approx 424.6\,\mathrm{K} \approx 425\,\mathrm{K}$.
5. So the reaction is spontaneous at $T > 425\,\mathrm{K}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Gibbs Energy and Spontaneity · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 166](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q166_24f63250_30102026.png?v=24f6325008f4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 166](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q166_068e716d_30102026.png?v=068e716daf82)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** With $\Delta H$ and $\Delta S$ both positive, $\Delta G = \Delta H - T\Delta S$ turns negative only above the crossover temperature $\Delta H/\Delta S$.
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  The crossover comes out right at $35500/83.6 = 425\,\mathrm{K}$ and the inequality is then written the way an exothermic reaction would need it, where a low temperature is what favours spontaneity.
- **Why:** This question requires the crossover temperature computed from the two quantities given, demonstrated by "$\Delta H = 35.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$ and $\Delta S = 83.6\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$" with temperature ranges as the options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q167

Chemistry · physical · Solutions

If molality of the dilute solution is doubled, the value of molal depression constant ($K_f$) will be

- **(1)** Unchanged
- (2) Doubled
- (3) Halved
- (4) Tripled

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $K_f$ (molal depression constant) is a characteristic of solvent and is independent of molality.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\Delta T_f = K_f\, m$, where $K_f = \frac{R\, M_1\, T_f^2}{1000\, \Delta_{fus}H}$ depends only on the solvent's molar mass, freezing point and enthalpy of fusion.
2. Doubling $m$ doubles $\Delta T_f$ but does not change $K_f$, so $K_f$ is unchanged, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Depression of Freezing Point · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q167_1150de85_30102026.png?v=1150de851e95)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q167_12ffcb2f_30102026.png?v=12ffcb2ff86b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** $K_f$ belongs to the solvent alone — it is the freezing point depression per unit molality — so doubling the molality doubles $\Delta T_f$ and leaves $K_f$ where it was.
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 2  
  "Doubled" is the true answer for the freezing point depression, since $\Delta T_f = K_f m$; the stem asks about the constant in that equation, which the solvent fixes.
- **Why:** This question requires how one quantity responds when another is changed, demonstrated by the stem reading "If molality of the dilute solution is doubled, the value of molal depression constant ($K_f$) will be" with Unchanged / Doubled / Halved / Tripled as the options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q168

Chemistry · physical · Solutions

Which of the following is dependent on temperature?

- (1) Weight percentage
- (2) Molality
- **(3)** Molarity
- (4) Mole fraction

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Molarity includes volume of solution which can change with change in temperature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Molarity is $M = \frac{n_{\text{solute}}}{V_{\text{solution}}\,(\text{L})}$, which contains the solution volume.
2. Volume expands or contracts with temperature, so $V = V(T)$ and hence $M$ changes with $T$.
3. Weight percentage $= \frac{m_{\text{solute}}}{m_{\text{solution}}} \times 100$, molality $m = \frac{n_{\text{solute}}}{\text{kg solvent}}$ and mole fraction $x_i = \frac{n_i}{n_{\text{total}}}$ use only masses and moles, which do not change with $T$.
4. So only molarity is temperature dependent, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Molarity · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q168_c10f53c4_30102026.png?v=c10f53c4cf4c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q168_818bf661_30102026.png?v=818bf661963c)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Only a concentration term with a volume in it varies with temperature: molarity is moles per litre of solution and the solution expands when heated, while mass- and mole-based terms do not.
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 2  
  Molality and molarity are stored as one half-remembered item — "the one with the kilogram of solvent" — and a student who cannot place which is which takes the other member of the pair.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated fact about which concentration term carries a volume and so varies with temperature, demonstrated by the stem asking "Which of the following is dependent on temperature?" with four concentration terms as the options. The swap test decides the trap axis: replace "Molality" with an unlike term and the pull dies, so the one-letter likeness to molarity is what carries the student.

## Q169

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

Pick out the correct statement with respect $\mathrm{[Mn(CN)_6]^{3-}}$

- (1) It is $dsp^{2}$ hybridised and square planar
- (2) It is $sp^{3}d^{2}$ hybridised and octahedral
- (3) It is $sp^{3}d^{2}$ hybridised and tetrahedral
- **(4)** It is $d^{2}sp^{3}$ hybridised and octahedral

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[Mn(CN)_6]^{3-}}$
2. $\mathrm{Mn(III)} = [\mathrm{Ar}]3d^{4}$
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ being strong field ligand forces pairing of electrons
4. This gives $t_{2g}^{4}e_{g}^{0}$
5. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Mn(III)} = [\mathrm{Ar}]$

![printed figure: orbital-box diagram: 3d boxes (↑↓, ↑, ↑, empty, empty), 4s box (empty), 4p boxes (empty, empty, empty); the two empty 3d boxes, the 4s box and the three 4p boxes are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^{2}sp^{3}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q169_sol_1_4cf37daf_30102026.svg?v=4cf37daf99fc)

*orbital-box diagram: 3d boxes (↑↓, ↑, ↑, empty, empty), 4s box (empty), 4p boxes (empty, empty, empty); the two empty 3d boxes, the 4s box and the three 4p boxes are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^{2}sp^{3}$*

6. $\because$ Coordination number of $\mathrm{Mn} = 6$
7. $\therefore$ Structure = octahedral
8. $\mathrm{[Mn(CN)_6]^{3-}} =$

![printed figure: orbital-box diagram: (Ar) followed by boxes ↑↓, ↑, ↑, ××, ××, then ××, then ××, ××, ××; the six boxes holding ×× pairs are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^{2}sp^{3}$](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q169_sol_2_105dbc2b_30102026.svg?v=105dbc2b37af)

*orbital-box diagram: [Ar] followed by boxes ↑↓, ↑, ↑, ××, ××, then ××, then ××, ××, ××; the six boxes holding ×× pairs are enclosed in a dotted box labelled $d^{2}sp^{3}$*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Oxidation state: $x + 6(-1) = -3 \Rightarrow x = +3$, so the metal ion is $\mathrm{Mn^{3+}}$.
2. $\mathrm{Mn^{3+}}$ has configuration $[\mathrm{Ar}]\,3d^{4}$.
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is a strong-field ligand, so the four electrons pair in the lower orbitals: $t_{2g}^{4}e_{g}^{0}$, leaving two unpaired electrons and two $3d$ orbitals empty.
4. The two empty $3d$, one $4s$ and three $4p$ orbitals hybridise to give $d^{2}sp^{3}$ (inner-orbital complex).
5. Six ligands on $d^{2}sp^{3}$ hybrid orbitals give an octahedral geometry, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Bond Theory, Its Limitations and Magnetic Properties of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 29)

![printed question 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q169_250d5ff0_30102026.png?v=250d5ff0d704)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q169_830a1a2c_30102026.png?v=830a1a2c01a8)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Six ligands mean an octahedron, and a strong-field ligand such as $\mathrm{CN^-}$ pairs the metal's d electrons so that inner d orbitals are free: the hybridisation is $d^2sp^3$, not $sp^3d^2$.
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  Counting six ligands gives octahedral, and the hybridisation is then filled in as $sp^3d^2$ — the outer-orbital set — without asking whether $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is a strong enough field to open the inner 3d orbitals.
- **Why:** This question requires valence bond theory applied to a named complex, demonstrated by the stem reading "Pick out the correct statement with respect $\mathrm{[Mn(CN)_6]^{3-}}$" and every option pairing a hybridisation with a shape.
- **Note:** `E1_question_type` arrived as plain_mcq from the pattern's default case, but the stem says "Pick out the correct statement" and each option is a two-part claim judged whole, so the shape recorded here is the one on the page, `statements`.

## Q170

Chemistry · physical · The Solid State

Which is the incorrect statement?

- **(1)** Frenkel defect is favoured in those ionic compounds in which sizes of cation and anions are almost equal
- **(2)** $\mathrm{FeO_{0.98}}$ has non stoichiometric metal deficiency defect
- (3) Density decreases in case of crystals with Schottky's defect
- (4) $\mathrm{NaCl(s)}$ is insulator, silicon is semiconductor, silver is conductor, quartz is piezo electric crystal

**Answer** — booklet: (1, 2) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Frenkel defect occurs in those ionic compounds in which size of cation and anion is largely different.
2. Non-stoichiometric ferrous oxide is $\mathrm{Fe_{0.93-0.96}O_{1.00}}$ and it is due to metal deficiency defect.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Frenkel defect occurs when the smaller ion (usually the cation) leaves its site and occupies an interstitial site, so it is favoured when there is a large difference in size between cation and anion; equal sizes favour Schottky defect, so statement $1$ is incorrect.
2. In $\mathrm{FeO_{0.98}}$ there is less Fe than required by $\mathrm{FeO}$, i.e. a metal deficiency non-stoichiometric defect, so statement $2$ is correct.
3. Schottky defect removes equal numbers of cations and anions from the lattice, lowering the mass per unit volume, so density decreases and statement $3$ is correct.
4. $\mathrm{NaCl(s)}$ is an insulator, Si is a semiconductor, Ag is a conductor and quartz is piezoelectric, so statement $4$ is correct.
5. The only incorrect statement is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Imperfections in Solids · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q170_2fded173_30102026.png?v=2fded1738d2c)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q170_c003fc3b_30102026.png?v=c003fc3baa9e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** The point defects differ in what they do to the crystal: Schottky takes a cation and an anion out of the lattice and so lowers the density, while Frenkel only moves an ion into an interstitial site and leaves the density alone.
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 3  
  Density is the fact that separates the two defects — it falls for Schottky and holds for Frenkel — and attaching the Frenkel half to Schottky makes this printed line read as the false one, so it is ticked under "incorrect".
- **Why:** This question requires the stated facts about the point defects, demonstrated by option 1 reading "Frenkel defect is favoured in those ionic compounds in which sizes of cation and anions are almost equal" under a stem asking "Which is the incorrect statement?".
- **Note:** Options 1 and 2 are both in `accepted_answers` ("Answer (1 & 2)*"), so neither carries a trap or a filler here and only options 3 and 4 are accounted for. As printed, option 2 reads $\mathrm{FeO_{0.98}}$, which is oxygen-deficient, rather than the metal-deficient $\mathrm{Fe_{0.98}O}$; recorded, not re-judged.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q171

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

$\mathrm{HgCl_2}$ and $\mathrm{I_2}$ both when dissolved in water containing $\mathrm{I^-}$ ions the pair of species formed is

- (1) $\mathrm{Hg_2I_2}$, $\mathrm{I^-}$
- (2) $\mathrm{HgI_2}$, $\mathrm{I_3^-}$
- (3) $\mathrm{HgI_2}$, $\mathrm{I^-}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{HgI_4^{2-}}$, $\mathrm{I_3^-}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In a solution containing $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$, $\mathrm{I_2}$ and $\mathrm{I^-}$, both $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$ and $\mathrm{I_2}$ compete for $\mathrm{I^-}$.
2. Since formation constant of $[\mathrm{HgI_4}]^{2-}$ is $1.9 \times 10^{30}$ which is very large as compared with $\mathrm{I_3^-}$ $(K_f = 700)$
3. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{I^-}$ will preferentially combine with $\mathrm{HgCl_2}$.
4. $\mathrm{HgCl_2} + 2\mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow \underset{\text{Red ppt}}{\mathrm{HgI_2}\downarrow} + 2\mathrm{Cl^-}$
5. $\mathrm{HgI_2} + 2\mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow \underset{\text{soluble}}{[\mathrm{HgI_4}]^{2-}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{HgCl_2} + 2\mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow \mathrm{HgI_2} + 2\mathrm{Cl^-}$ (red precipitate first).
2. In the iodide-rich solution the precipitate dissolves: $\mathrm{HgI_2} + 2\mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow [\mathrm{HgI_4}]^{2-}$.
3. Iodine dissolves in iodide solution as triiodide: $\mathrm{I_2} + \mathrm{I^-} \rightarrow \mathrm{I_3^-}$.
4. The pair of species is $\mathrm{HgI_4^{2-}}$ and $\mathrm{I_3^-}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Interhalogen Compounds & Important Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q171_64517e53_30102026.png?v=64517e53b15a)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q171_8c009dd9_30102026.png?v=8c009dd9ac8c)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Excess iodide carries both reactions a step past the obvious one: $\mathrm{HgI_2}$ dissolves to the tetraiodomercurate(II) ion, and iodine takes up iodide to give triiodide.
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  The iodine half is right — $\mathrm{I_2 + I^- \rightarrow I_3^-}$ — and the mercury half is stopped at the plain double decomposition $\mathrm{HgCl_2 + 2I^- \rightarrow HgI_2}$, with the excess iodide that redissolves the precipitate left out.
- **Why:** This question requires the products of two reactions run in the presence of an excess reagent, demonstrated by the stem's "dissolved in water containing $\mathrm{I^-}$ ions" and options that are pairs of species.

## Q172

Chemistry · inorganic · General Principles and Processes of Isolation of Elements

Extraction of gold and silver involves leaching with $\mathrm{CN^{-}}$ ion. Silver is later recovered by

- **(1)** Displacement with $\mathrm{Zn}$
- (2) Liquation
- (3) Distillation
- (4) Zone refining

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Zn}$ being more reactive than $\mathrm{Ag}$ and $\mathrm{Au}$, displaces them.
2. From Native ore,
3. $4\mathrm{Ag} + 8\mathrm{NaCN} + 2\mathrm{H_2O} + \mathrm{O_2} \xrightarrow{\text{Leaching}} \underset{\substack{\text{Soluble}\\ \text{Sodium dicyanoargentate(I)}}}{4\mathrm{Na[Ag(CN)_2]}} + 4\mathrm{NaOH}$
4. $2\mathrm{Na[Ag(CN)_2]} + \mathrm{Zn} \xrightarrow{\text{Displacement}} \mathrm{Na_2[Zn(CN)_4]} + 2\mathrm{Ag}\downarrow$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Leaching with $\mathrm{CN^{-}}$ in air dissolves silver as the soluble complex $[\mathrm{Ag(CN)_2}]^{-}$.
2. Zinc is more electropositive than silver, so it reduces the complex: $2[\mathrm{Ag(CN)_2}]^{-} + \mathrm{Zn} \rightarrow [\mathrm{Zn(CN)_4}]^{2-} + 2\mathrm{Ag}$.
3. Silver is therefore recovered by displacement with $\mathrm{Zn}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Concentration of Ores · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q172_1521039c_30102026.png?v=1521039c54bc)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q172_7791042b_30102026.png?v=7791042b8bb2)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** In the cyanide process the metal is leached out as a dicyanido complex and then displaced from that solution by a more electropositive metal, zinc — a recovery step, not a refining one.
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 3  
  Zinc is the metal the chapter ties distillation to, and zinc is the metal this process adds, so "recovered" is answered with the step that drives the zinc off instead of the step that brings the silver out of solution.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated step the chapter prints for the cyanide process, demonstrated by the stem reading "Extraction of gold and silver involves leaching with $\mathrm{CN^{-}}$ ion. Silver is later recovered by" with three refining methods standing against one displacement. The position test decides the trap axis: the wrong options are the methods printed beside this process in the same chapter, and swapping their names for unlike words leaves the pull intact, so it is `nearby_fact` and not `look_alike_term`.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q173

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The correct order of the stoichiometries of $\mathrm{AgCl}$ formed when $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$ in excess is treated with the complexes : $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 6NH_3}$, $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 5NH_3}$, $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 4NH_3}$ respectively is

- (1) $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$
- (2) $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$
- (3) $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$
- **(4)** $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Complexes are respectively $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_6]Cl_3}$, $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_5Cl]Cl_2}$ and $\mathrm{[Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2]Cl}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Co(III) in these complexes has coordination number $6$, and only chloride outside the coordination sphere is ionisable and precipitates as $\mathrm{AgCl}$.
2. $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 6NH_3} = [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_6}]\mathrm{Cl_3}$, so it gives $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$.
3. $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 5NH_3} = [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_5Cl}]\mathrm{Cl_2}$, so it gives $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$.
4. $\mathrm{CoCl_3\cdot 4NH_3} = [\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2}]\mathrm{Cl}$, so it gives $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$.
5. The order $3\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $2\,\mathrm{AgCl}$, $1\,\mathrm{AgCl}$ is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Werner’s Theory and Applications of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q173_b7c8ab71_30102026.png?v=b7c8ab714e74)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q173_60dd9cf3_30102026.png?v=60dd9cf3bf8f)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Werner's theory: the six coordination sites of cobalt(III) are filled first, and only the chloride left outside the coordination sphere is free to come down as AgCl
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 3  
  The hexaammine's 3 AgCl is the entry Werner's table is remembered for, and the 1 and 2 are then read off the chlorides written inside the brackets of $[\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_5Cl}]\mathrm{Cl_2}$ and $[\mathrm{Co(NH_3)_4Cl_2}]\mathrm{Cl}$ -- the chlorides that stay put rather than the ones that precipitate.
- **Why:** This question requires Werner's coordination-sphere rule applied to three complexes at once, demonstrated by 'AgNO_3 in excess is treated with the complexes : CoCl_3.6NH_3, CoCl_3.5NH_3, CoCl_3.4NH_3 respectively' with each option supplying all three counts together.
- **Note:** E1's pattern called this arrange_in_order. The printed shape is one question whose options each bundle three stoichiometries accepted or rejected whole, which is two_part in the current vocabulary; asks records the rule rather than 'order' because nothing is being ranked -- the three complexes are fixed in the stem's sequence and each slot is one application of the coordination-number rule.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q174

Chemistry · organic · Haloalkanes and Haloarenes

Identify A and predict the type of reaction

![printed figure: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →($\ce{NaNH2}$) A](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q174_933f848e_30102026.svg?v=933f848e9806)

*1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →[$\ce{NaNH2}$] A*

![drawn: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →($\ce{NaNH2}$) A](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_question_2d2cb6bc_30102026.svg?v=2d2cb6bceb68)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

Structures: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene `COc1cccc(Br)c1` ![1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_question_s1_3ee32348_30102026.svg?v=3ee323483d67)

- (1) methoxybenzene `COc1ccccc1` ![methoxybenzene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_option_1_s1_f35aa33e_30102026.svg?v=f35aa33e5a1e) — and cine substitution reaction
- **(2)** 3-methoxyaniline `COc1cccc(N)c1` ![3-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_option_2_s1_fed4e630_30102026.svg?v=fed4e630d032) — and substitution reaction
- (3) 2-methoxyaniline `COc1ccccc1N` ![2-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_option_3_s1_87afeb56_30102026.svg?v=87afeb569572) — and elimination addition reaction
- (4) 1-bromo-2-methoxybenzene `COc1ccccc1Br` ![1-bromo-2-methoxybenzene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_option_4_s1_1426f143_30102026.svg?v=1426f14374e9) — and cine substitution reaction

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →($\ce{NH2-}$) 2-bromo-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide → 1,2-didehydro-3-methoxybenzene (Benzyne)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q174_sol_1_908a67d5_30102026.svg?v=908a67d50334)

*1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →[$\ce{NH2-}$] 2-bromo-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide → 1,2-didehydro-3-methoxybenzene (Benzyne)*

![drawn: 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene →($\ce{NH2-}$) 2-bromo-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide → 1,2-didehydro-3-methoxybenzene (Benzyne)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_solution_1_c96f8e23_30102026.svg?v=c96f8e23978b)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

![printed figure: 1,2-didehydro-3-methoxybenzene $\ce{NH2-}$ (curved arrows a, b) →(X) 2-amino-3-methoxybenzen-1-ide (Less stable); → 2-amino-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide →(H-NH₂) 3-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_agent/q174_sol_2_708c593a_30102026.svg?v=708c593a5030)

*1,2-didehydro-3-methoxybenzene $\ce{NH2-}$ (curved arrows a, b) →[X] 2-amino-3-methoxybenzen-1-ide (Less stable); → 2-amino-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide →[H-NH₂] 3-methoxyaniline*

![drawn: 1,2-didehydro-3-methoxybenzene $\ce{NH2-}$ (curved arrows a, b) →(X) 2-amino-3-methoxybenzen-1-ide (Less stable); → 2-amino-6-methoxybenzen-1-ide →(H-NH₂) 3-methoxyaniline](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/figures_drawn/q174_solution_2_0af681ea_30102026.svg?v=0af681ea217e)  
*drawn on the booklet's geometry, checked against its SMILES*

1. More stable as $-$ve charge is close to electron withdrawing group
2. $\because$ Incoming nucleophile ends on same ‘$\mathrm{C}$’ on which ‘$\mathrm{Br}$’ (Leaving group) was present
3. $\therefore$ NOT cine substitution.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The reagent $\mathrm{NaNH_2}$ is a strong base. With an aryl halide it reacts by the benzyne (elimination-addition) route.
2. The most acidic ring H is at $\mathrm{C2}$, between $\mathrm{OCH_3}$ (at $\mathrm{C1}$) and $\mathrm{Br}$ (at $\mathrm{C3}$). Both groups pull electrons inductively ($-I$), so $\mathrm{NH_2^-}$ removes this H.
3. The $\mathrm{C2}$ carbanion loses $\mathrm{Br^-}$, which gives the 2,3-benzyne (3-methoxybenzyne).
4. $\mathrm{NH_2^-}$ adds at $\mathrm{C3}$, the benzyne carbon meta to $\mathrm{OCH_3}$. This leaves the negative charge on $\mathrm{C2}$, next to the $-I$ $\mathrm{OCH_3}$ group, which stabilises it.
5. The $\mathrm{C2}$ anion takes a proton from $\mathrm{NH_3}$, which gives $A = m\text{-}\mathrm{H_2N{-}C_6H_4{-}OCH_3}$ (m-anisidine).
6. $\mathrm{NH_2}$ now sits on the carbon that held $\mathrm{Br}$, so the net change is a direct substitution, not a cine substitution. The answer is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Haloarenes · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 30)

![printed question 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q174_a311ee7e_30102026.png?v=a311ee7ed38e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q174_7b713105_30102026.png?v=7b71310524fb)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** NaNH2 on an aryl halide carrying no activating group goes through a benzyne intermediate, and the carbon the amide ion adds to decides whether the amine appears where the bromine was or on its neighbour
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Checked all four stored options: each carries only its trailing reaction-type phrase and the structure that is the identity of A is missing from every one, while options 1 and 4 read as the same text, 'and cine substitution reaction'. With no structures to set against each other no route to any option can be named.
- **Why:** This question requires the product of NaNH2 acting on 1-bromo-3-methoxybenzene together with the name of the route that gives it, demonstrated by the stem 'Identify A and predict the type of reaction'.
- **Note:** Stored options are truncated: the structure half of every option is absent and options 1 and 4 are textually identical, so the record is incomplete and no trap is written (codebook sections 5 and 5a). The printed figure and the stem are intact; it is our option text that is damaged. A reviewer holding the paper should check the benzyne route -- whether the amine ends up on the carbon that held the bromine or on the one beside it -- since that is what separates 'substitution' from 'cine substitution' among these options. The key is left exactly as printed, Answer (2).
<!--/ecat-->

## Q175

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

An example of a sigma bonded organometallic compound is

- (1) Cobaltocene
- (2) Ruthenocene
- **(3)** Grignard's reagent
- (4) Ferrocene

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Grignard's reagent i.e., $\mathrm{RMgX}$ is $\sigma$-bonded organometallic compound.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Grignard's reagent $\mathrm{RMgX}$ has a direct $\sigma$ bond between $\mathrm{Mg}$ and the alkyl carbon, so it is a $\sigma$-bonded organometallic compound.
2. Cobaltocene, ruthenocene and ferrocene are metallocenes $\mathrm{M(\eta^5\text{-}C_5H_5)_2}$ in which the metal is $\pi$-bonded to the cyclopentadienyl rings, so the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Bonding in Coordination Compounds and Metal Carbonyls · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q175_88fd6e20_30102026.png?v=88fd6e202e96)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q175_567b20c1_30102026.png?v=567b20c116fd)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A sigma bonded organometallic holds the metal to carbon by a single bond, as in the Grignard reagent R-Mg-X, while the metallocenes hold the metal by the ring's pi electrons
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 4  
  Ferrocene is the organometallic the chapter showcases, printed in the paragraph beside the sigma bonded one, so a student holding 'ferrocene is the organometallic example' answers the word organometallic and never reaches the word sigma.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated fact about which named compound carries a metal-carbon sigma bond, demonstrated by 'An example of a sigma bonded organometallic compound is' with three sandwich metallocenes and one Grignard reagent as the options.
- **Note:** Test applied for nearby_fact against look_alike_term: swap the option names for two unlike words and the pull survives, because it rests on ferrocene being the organometallic printed beside the sigma bonded line, not on 'ferrocene' resembling 'Grignard's reagent'. Position is doing the work, so nearby_fact.

## Q176

Chemistry · inorganic · The D- and F-Block Elements – Practice

The reason for greater range of oxidation states in actinoids is attributed to

- (1) $4f$ and $5d$ levels being close in energies
- (2) The radioactive nature of actinoids
- (3) Actinoid contraction
- **(4)** $5f$, $6d$ and $7s$ levels having comparable energies

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. It is a fact.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In actinoids the $5f$, $6d$ and $7s$ levels have comparable energies, so a variable number of electrons from all three can be lost or shared, giving a wider range of oxidation states than lanthanoids; this is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Lanthanoids & Actinoids · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** Actinoids reach many oxidation states because their 5f, 6d and 7s levels lie at comparable energies, so electrons from all three are available for bonding
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 1  
  Option 1 is the key with every quantum number shifted down by one -- 4f and 5d in place of 5f, 6d and 7s -- so a student who recalls the sentence but not which series owns it ticks the lanthanoid version of it.
- **Why:** This question requires the reason the textbook gives for the actinoids' wide oxidation-state range, demonstrated by the stem 'The reason for greater range of oxidation states in actinoids is attributed to'.
- **Note:** Test applied on the nearby_fact / look_alike_term pair: both hold here, since the lanthanoid sentence is printed beside the actinoid one, but replacing '4f and 5d' with an unlike phrase kills the pull, so the resemblance of the orbital labels is doing the work. The rulebook gives the likeness the win when both hold.
