## Q177

Chemistry · inorganic · Classification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties

The element $Z = 114$ has been discovered recently. It will belong to which of the following family group and electronic configuration?

- (1) Nitrogen family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{6}$
- (2) Halogen family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{5}$
- **(3)** Carbon family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{2}$
- (4) Oxygen family, $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{4}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $Z = 114$ belong to Group $14$, carbon family
2. Electronic configuration $= [\mathrm{Rn}]5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The preceding noble gas is $\mathrm{Rn}$ with $Z = 86$, so $114 - 86 = 28$ electrons remain to be placed.
2. Fill in order: $7s^{2}$ (2), $5f^{14}$ (14), $6d^{10}$ (10), total $26$, leaving $28 - 26 = 2$ electrons for $7p$.
3. Configuration: $[\mathrm{Rn}]\,5f^{14}6d^{10}7s^{2}7p^{2}$.
4. Valence shell $ns^{2}np^{2}$ means group $14$, the carbon family, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Electronic Configurations · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q177_e65e2913_30102026.png?v=e65e29138eb6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q177_bddb27b6_30102026.png?v=bddb27b6f74f)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Position in the periodic table follows from the filling order: after [Rn] the 7s, 5f and 6d subshells take 26 electrons to reach element 112, so Z = 114 is 7p2 and sits in the carbon family
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 4  
  Counting back from the closed-shell end of the period gives 118 - 114 = 4, and that 4 is written in as the number of 7p electrons instead of being taken off six, which produces 7p4 and the oxygen family.
- **Why:** This question requires the filling order applied to a new element to fix both its group and its configuration, demonstrated by 'It will belong to which of the following family group and electronic configuration?' with every option pairing a family name to a full configuration.
- **Note:** E1's pattern called this plain_mcq; each option bundles a family name with a configuration and both halves must hold, so the printed shape recorded here is two_part.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q178

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Kinetics

A first order reaction has a specific reaction rate of $10^{-2}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. How much time will it take for $20\,\mathrm{g}$ of the reactant to reduce to $5\,\mathrm{g}$?

- (1) $693.0$ second
- (2) $238.6$ second
- **(3)** $138.6$ second
- (4) $346.5$ second

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $t_{1/2} = \frac{0.693}{10^{-2}}$ second
2. For the reduction of $20\,\mathrm{g}$ of reactant to $5\,\mathrm{g}$, two $t_{1/2}$ is required.
3. $\therefore\ t = 2 \times \frac{0.693}{10^{-2}}$ second
4. $= 138.6$ second

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For a first order reaction, $t = \frac{2.303}{k}\log\frac{[A]_0}{[A]}$.
2. $\frac{[A]_0}{[A]} = \frac{20\,\mathrm{g}}{5\,\mathrm{g}} = 4$.
3. $\frac{2.303}{k} = \frac{2.303}{10^{-2}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}} = 230.3\,\mathrm{s}$.
4. $t = 230.3 \times \log 4 = 230.3 \times 0.602 = 138.6\,\mathrm{s}$.
5. Check: $t_{1/2} = \frac{0.693}{10^{-2}} = 69.3\,\mathrm{s}$ and $20 \to 5\,\mathrm{g}$ is two half-lives, $2 \times 69.3 = 138.6\,\mathrm{s}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** First Order Reactions · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 178](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q178_b60fbc5f_30102026.png?v=b60fbc5f0725)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 178](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q178_5bf20db7_30102026.png?v=5bf20db79aae)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** For a first order reaction the time depends only on the ratio of amounts: 20 g falling to 5 g is two half-lives, and $t_{1/2} = 0.693/k$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  The half-life relation is reached for as though it answered the question asked, and $0.693/10^{-2}$ is written as $0.693 \times 1000 = 693$, which is exactly the printed option.
- **Why:** This question requires the first order integrated rate law applied to the stem's own numbers, demonstrated by 'a specific reaction rate of $10^{-2}\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}$' with '20 g of the reactant to reduce to 5 g' and four times as the options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q179

Chemistry · inorganic · The s-Block Elements

Ionic mobility of which of the following alkali metal ions is lowest when aqueous solution of their salts are put under an electric field?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{Li}$
- (2) $\mathrm{Na}$
- (3) $\mathrm{K}$
- (4) $\mathrm{Rb}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Li^{+}}$ being smallest, has maximum charge density
2. $\therefore$ $\mathrm{Li^{+}}$ is most heavily hydrated among all alkali metal ions. Effective size of $\mathrm{Li^{+}}$ in aq solution is therefore, largest.
3. $\therefore$ Moves slowest under electric field.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Down the group the bare ionic radius increases: $\mathrm{Li^+} < \mathrm{Na^+} < \mathrm{K^+} < \mathrm{Rb^+}$.
2. The smallest ion has the highest charge density, so the extent of hydration is $\mathrm{Li^+} > \mathrm{Na^+} > \mathrm{K^+} > \mathrm{Rb^+}$.
3. So the hydrated radius in water is $\mathrm{Li^+(aq)} > \mathrm{Na^+(aq)} > \mathrm{K^+(aq)} > \mathrm{Rb^+(aq)}$.
4. A larger hydrated ion moves more slowly in an electric field, so ionic mobility is $\mathrm{Li^+} < \mathrm{Na^+} < \mathrm{K^+} < \mathrm{Rb^+}$; the lowest is $\mathrm{Li^+}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Alkali Metals: Properties and Trends · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 179](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q179_4902266b_30102026.png?v=4902266bf3c4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 179](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q179_c116a439_30102026.png?v=c116a439b19f)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Comparison** (`compare`) — the difference between two things, or the correct pairing
- **Concept:** In water an alkali metal ion travels inside its hydration shell, and Li+, with the largest hydration enthalpy, carries the biggest hydrated radius and so the lowest mobility
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  The rule 'the bigger the ion, the slower it moves' is applied to the bare ion, but the stem puts the salts in aqueous solution, where each ion drags a hydration shell and Li+'s shell is the biggest of the four.
- **Why:** This question requires four members of one series to be told apart on a single property, demonstrated by 'Ionic mobility of which of the following alkali metal ions is lowest when aqueous solution of their salts are put under an electric field?' with Li, Na, K and Rb as the options.
- **Note:** The options are printed as the metals, Li / Na / K / Rb, though the stem asks about their ions; tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q180

Chemistry · physical · Electrochemistry

In the electrochemical cell $\mathrm{Zn|ZnSO_4(0.01\,M)||CuSO_4(1.0\,M)|Cu}$, the emf of this Daniel cell is $E_1$. When the concentration of $\mathrm{ZnSO_4}$ is changed to $1.0\,\mathrm{M}$ and that of $\mathrm{CuSO_4}$ changed to $0.01\,\mathrm{M}$, the emf changes to $E_2$. From the following, which one is the relationship between $E_1$ and $E_2$? (Given, $\frac{RT}{F} = 0.059$)

- (1) $E_2 = 0 \neq E_1$
- (2) $E_1 = E_2$
- (3) $E_1 < E_2$
- **(4)** $E_1 > E_2$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Zn|ZnSO_4(0.01\,M)||CuSO_4(1.0\,M)|Cu}$
2. $\therefore\ E_1 = E^{\mathrm{o}}_{\mathrm{cell}} - \frac{2.303RT}{2 \times F} \times \log\frac{(0.01)}{1}$
3. When concentrations are changed
4. $\therefore\ E_2 = E^{\mathrm{o}}_{\mathrm{cell}} - \frac{2.303RT}{2F} \times \log\frac{1}{0.01}$
5. i.e., $E_1 > E_2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Cell reaction: $\mathrm{Zn + Cu^{2+} \rightarrow Zn^{2+} + Cu}$, with $n = 2$.
2. Nernst equation: $E = E^\circ - \frac{0.059}{2}\log\frac{[\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}]}{[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]}$.
3. First cell: $E_1 = E^\circ - \frac{0.059}{2}\log\frac{0.01}{1.0} = E^\circ + 0.059\,\mathrm{V}$.
4. Second cell: $E_2 = E^\circ - \frac{0.059}{2}\log\frac{1.0}{0.01} = E^\circ - 0.059\,\mathrm{V}$.
5. $E_1 - E_2 = 0.118\,\mathrm{V} > 0$, so $E_1 > E_2$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Nernst Equation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 31)

![printed question 180](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/crops/q180_f149c24d_30102026.png?v=f149c24d16e5)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 180](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/30102026/neet_papers/2017/2017_C/solutions/q180_f153645a_30102026.png?v=f153645abe1e)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** In the Nernst equation for the Daniell cell the correction term carries $\log\frac{[\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}]}{[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]}$, so raising the zinc ion concentration while lowering the copper ion concentration lowers the emf
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  With the reaction quotient written upside down, $[\mathrm{Cu^{2+}}]/[\mathrm{Zn^{2+}}]$, the sign of the $\frac{0.059}{2}\log Q$ term flips, so the first cell comes out below the second and the inequality lands the other way round.
- **Why:** This question requires the Nernst equation applied to a stated change of concentrations with no number asked for, demonstrated by the options being the four relations '$E_2 = 0 \neq E_1$', '$E_1 = E_2$', '$E_1 < E_2$' and '$E_1 > E_2$'.
- **Note:** The stem prints 'RT/F = 0.059', which is the value of $2.303RT/F$; it is not needed here in any case, since only the sign of the log term decides the inequality. Tagged as printed.
<!--/ecat-->
